Inference for Probabilistic Dependency Graphs
Oliver E. Richardson, Joseph Y. Halpern, Christopher De Sa

Abstract
Probabilistic dependency graphs (PDGs) are a flexible class of probabilistic graphical models, subsuming Bayesian Networks and Factor Graphs. They can also capture inconsistent beliefs, and provide a way of measuring the degree of this inconsistency. We present the first tractable inference algorithm for PDGs with discrete variables, making the asymptotic complexity of PDG inference similar that of the graphical models they generalize. The key components are: (1) the observation that, in many cases, the distribution a PDG specifies can be formulated as a convex optimization problem (with exponential cone constraints), (2) a construction that allows us to express these problems compactly for PDGs of boundeed treewidth, (3) contributions to the theory of PDGs that justify the construction, and (4) an appeal to interior point methods that can solve such problems in polynomial time. We verify the correctness and complexity of our approach, and provide an implementation of it. We then evaluate our implementation, and demonstrate that it outperforms baseline approaches. Our code is available at github.com/orichardson/pdg-infer-uai .



1
Introduction

Probabilistic dependency graphs (PDGs)

[
Richardson and Halpern 2021
]
,
form a very general class of probabilistic graphical models,
that includes not only
Bayesian Networks (BNs) and
Factor Graphs (FGs),
but also more recent statistical models built out of neural networks,
such as Variational Autoencoders (VAEs)
[
Kingma and Welling 2014
]
.
PDGs can also capture inconsistent
beliefs, and provide a useful way to measure the degree of this inconsistency;
for a VAE, this is the loss function used in training

[
Richardson 2022
]
.
PDGs have some
significant advantages over other representations of probabilistic information.
Their flexibility allows them to model beliefs that BNs cannot,
such as information from independent studies of the same variable
(perhaps with different controls, yielding probabilistic observations
p(Y|X)
and
q(Y|Z)
).
PDGs
can deal gracefully with conflicting information from multiple sources.
Every subcomponent of a PDG has probabilistic
meaning, independent of the other components;
compared to FGs, this makes PDGs more interpretable.
But up to now, there has been no practical way to do inference for
PDGs—that is,
to answer questions of the form
“what is the probability of
Y
given
X
?”
This paper presents the first algorithm to do so.

Before discussing our algorithm,
we must discuss what it even means to do inference for a PDG.
A BN or FG represents
a unique joint distribution.
Thus, for example, when we ask “what is the probability of
Y
given that
X{=}x
?”

in a BN, we mean “what is
\mu(Y|X{=}x)
?” for the probability
measure
\mu
that the BN represents.
But a PDG might, in general, represent more than just one distribution.

Like a BN, a PDG encodes
two types of information: “structural”
information about the independence of causal mechanisms,
and “observational” information
about conditional probabilities.
Unlike in a BN, the two can conflict in a PDG.
Corresponding to these two types of information,
a PDG has two
loss functions,
which quantify how far a distribution
\mu
is from
modeling the information of each type.
Given a number
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}\in[0,1]

indicating the importance of structure relative to observation,
we take the
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-semantics
of a PDG to be the
set of distributions that minimize
the appropriate convex combination of
losses.
We also consider the
0^{+}
​-semantics
: the limiting case that
arises as
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
goes to zero
(which focuses
on observation, using structure only to break ties).
This set
can be shown to contain precisely one distribution
for PDGs satisfying a mild regularity condition
(required by definition by
Richardson and Halpern 2021
);
we call such PDGs
proper
.
Thus, we have
a parameterized family of inference notions:
to do
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference, for
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}\in[0,1]\cup\{0^{+}\}
,
is to answer queries in a way that is true of all distributions in the
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-semantics.

If there are distributions
fully
consistent with
both the observational and the structural information
in a PDG
\mathbdcal{M}
,
then for
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}\in(0,1)\cup\{0^{+}\}
, all
notions of
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference
coincide.
If
\mathbdcal{M}
is also proper,
this means there is
a single distribution
\mu_{\mathbdcal{M}}

that minimizes both loss functions,
in which case we want to answer queries with respect to
\mu_{\mathbdcal{M}}

no matter how we weight observational and structural information.
Moreover, if
\mathbdcal{M}
represents a BN,
then
\mu_{\mathbdcal{M}}
is the distribution represented by the BN.
However, if there is no distribution that is consistent with both types of information, then the choice of
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
matters.

Since PDGs subsume BNs, and inference for BNs is already NP-hard, the same must be true of PDGs.
At a high level, the best we could hope for would be tractability on the restricted
class of models on which inference has traditionally been tractable—that is, a polynomial algorithm for models whose
underlying structure has
bounded treewidth
(see

Section

2
for formal definitions).
That is indeed what we have.
More precisely, we show that

0^{+}
​-inference
and
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference for small
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}

can be done
for discrete PDGs of bounded treewidth containing
N
variables in

\tilde{O}(N^{4})

time.

Our algorithm
is based on a line of recent work in
convex programming
that establishes
polynomial-time
for a class of optimization problems called
exponential conic programs

[
Badenbroek and Dahl 2021
,
Skajaa and Ye 2015
,
Nesterov et al. 1999
]
.
Our contribution is to show that the problem of inference in a PDG
of bounded treewidth
can be efficiently converted to a (sequence of) exponential conic program(s), at which point it can be solved with a commercial solver
(e.g.,
ApS 2022
) in polynomial time.
The direct appeal to a solver allows us
to benefit from the speed and reliability of such highly optimized solvers, and also from future improvements in exponential conic optimization.
Thus, our result is not only a theoretical one, but practical as well.

Beyond its role as a probabilistic model,
a PDG is also of interest for its degree of inconsistency—that is, the minimum value of its loss function.
As shown by

Richardson 2022
,
many loss functions and statistical divergences
can be viewed as measuring
the inconsistency
of a PDG that models the context appropriately.
This makes calculating this minimum value of interest—but up to now, there has been no way to do so.
There is a deep connection between this problem and PDG inference;
for now, we remark that this number is a byproduct of our techniques.

Contributions.

We provide the first algorithm for inference in a PDG;
in addition, it calculates a PDG’s degree of inconsistency.
We prove that
our algorithm
is correct, and also
fixed-parameter tractable: for PDGs of bounded treewidth,
it runs in polynomial time.
We also prove that PDG inference and inconsistency
calculation are equivalent problems.
Our algorithm reduces inference in PDGs to exponential conic programming
in a way that can be offloaded to powerful existing solvers.
We provide an implementation of this reduction in a
standard convex optimization framework, giving users an
interface between such solvers and the standard PDG Python library.
Finally, we evaluate our implementation. The
results suggest our method is faster and
significantly more reliable than simple baseline approaches.

2
Preliminaries & Related Work

Vector Notation.

For us, a vector is a map from a finite set
S
, called its

shape
,
to the extended reals
\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,:=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.
We write
\mathbf{u}:=[u_{i}]_{i\in S}
to define a vector
\mathbf{u}
by its components.

Vectors of the same shape
can be added (+), partially ordered (
\leq
), or multiplied (
\odot
) pointwise as usual.

\mathbf{1}
denotes an all-ones vector, of a shape implied by context.

Probabilities.

We write
\Delta S
to denote the set of probability distributions over a finite set
S
.
Every variable
X
can take on values from a finite set

\mathcal{V}\mskip-1.5muX

of possible values.
We can regard sets of variables
\mathbf{X}
as variables themselves, with

\mathcal{V}\mathbf{X}=\Pi_{X\in\mathbf{X}}\mathcal{V}X
.
A conditional probability distribution (cpd)
p(Y|X)
is a map

p:\mathcal{V}\mskip-1.5muX\to\Delta\mathcal{V}Y
assigning to each
x\in\mathcal{V}\mskip-1.5muX
a
probability distribution

p(Y|x)\in\Delta\mathcal{V}Y
, which is shorthand for
p(Y|X{=}x)
.
Given a distribution
\mu
over (the values of) a set of variables including
X
and
Y
,
we write
\mu(X)
for its marginal
on
X
,
and
\mu(Y|X)
for the cpd obtained by
conditioning on
X
and marginalizing to
Y
.
We also refer to
\mu
’s entropy

\H(\mu):=\Ex_{\mu}[\log\frac{1}{\mu}]
and
conditional entropy
\H_{\mu}(Y|X):=\Ex_{\mu}[\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mu(Y|X)}}]

of
Y
given
X
.

Hypergraphs and Treewidth.

A hypergraph

(V,\mathcal{E})
is a set
V
of vertices and a
set

\mathcal{E}
of
hyperedges
, which correspond to subsets of
V
.
An ordinary graph may be viewed as the special case in which every hyperedge contains two vertices.

Definition 1
.

A
directed hypergraph

(N,\mathcal{A})
is a set of nodes
N
, and
a set
of
(hyper)arcs

\mathcal{A}
,
each
a\in\mathcal{A}
of which
is associated with
a set of source nodes
S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\subseteq N
,
and target nodes
T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\subseteq N
.
We also write
S{\overset{\smash{\mskip-5.0mu\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}{\rightarrow}}T\in\mathcal{A}
to specify an
arc
a
together with its sources
S=S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}
and targets
T=T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}
.

A directed hypergraph
can be viewed as
a hypergraph
by joining
each source and target set,
thereby “forgetting” the direction of the arrow.
Thus, notions defined for undirected hypergraphs (like that of
treewidth, which we now review), can be
applied to directed hypergraphs as well.

Many problems that are intractable for general graphs
are tractable for trees, and
some graphs are closer to being trees than others.
A tree decomposition of a (hyper)graph
G=(V,\mathcal{E})
is a tree
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
whose vertices
C\in\mathdcal C
, called

clusters
, are subsets of
V
such that:

1.

every vertex
v\in V
and every hyperedge
E\in\mathcal{E}
is contained in at least one cluster, and

2.

every cluster
D
along the unique path from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
in
\cal T
,
contains
C_{1}\cap C_{2}
.

The
width
of a tree decomposition is one less than the size of its largest cluster,
and the
treewidth
of a (hyper)graph
G
is the smallest possible width of any tree decomposition of
G
.
It is NP-hard to determine the tree-width of a graph, but
if the tree-width is known to be bounded above, a tree decomposition may be constructed in linear time
[
Bodlaender 1993
]
.
For graphs of bounded tree-width, many problems
(indeed, any problem expressible in a certain second-order logic
[
Courcelle 1990
]
)
can be solved in
linear time.
This is also true of inference in
standard graphical models.

Graphical Models and Inference.

A
graphical model structure

is a (directed) (hyper)graph whose vertices
\mathcal{X}
are variables, and whose (hyper)edges
somehow
indicate local influences between variables.
A
probabilistic graphical model
,
or simply “graphical model”,
is a
graphical model structure
together with quantitative information about these local influences.
Semantically,
a graphical model
\cal M

typically
represents a joint probability distribution
\Pr_{\!\cal M}\in\Delta\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}
over its variables.

Inference for
\cal M
is then the ability to calculate cpds
\Pr_{\!\cal M}(Y|X{=}x)
,
where
X,Y\subset\mathcal{X}
and
x\in\mathcal{V}\!X
.

Many inference algorithms (such as belief propagation),
when applied to tree-like graphical models,
run in linear time and are provably correct.
If the same algorithms are naïvely applied to graphs with cycles (as in loopy belief propagation),
then they may not converge, and even if they do,
may give incorrect (or even inconsistent) answers

[
Wainwright et al. 2008
]
.
Nearly all exact inference algorithms
(including variable elimination
[
Bertele and Brioschi 1972
]
,
message-passing with
[
Lauritzen and Spiegelhalter 1988
]

and without division
[
Shafer and Shenoy 1990
]
,
among others
[
Wainwright et al. 2003
]
)
effectively construct a tree decomposition, and can be
viewed as running on a tree
[
Koller and Friedman 2009
, §9-11]
.
Indeed,
under widely believed assumptions,
every class of graphical models
for which (exact) inference is
not
NP-hard
has bounded treewidth

[
Chandrasekaran et al. 2012
]
.

Given a tree decomposition
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
of the
underlying model structure,
many of these algorithms
use a
standard
data structure
that we will call a
tree marginal
,
which
is a collection

\boldsymbol{\mu}=\{\mu_{C}(C)\}_{C\in\mathdcal C}

of probabilities over the clusters
[
Koller and Friedman 2009
, §10]
.
A tree marginal
\boldsymbol{\mu}

is said to be
calibrated
if neighboring clusters’ distributions agree on the variables
they share.
In this case,

\boldsymbol{\mu}
determines a joint distribution by

\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\mathcal{X})=\quad{\prod_{\mathclap{C\in\mathdcal C}}\mu_{C}(C)\penalty\ \,}\Big/{\penalty\ \penalty\ \prod_{\mathclap{(C{-}D)\mathrlap{\in\cal T}}}\mu_{C}(C\cap D)\,,}

(1)

which has the property that
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(C)=\mu_{C}
for
all

C\in\mathdcal C
.

A calibrated tree marginal summarizes the answers to
queries about
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

[see
Koller and Friedman 2009
, §10.3.3]
.
Therefore, to answer probabilistic queries with respect to a distribution
\mu
, it suffices to find a calibrated tree marginal
\boldsymbol{\mu}
that represents
\mu
, and appeal to standard algorithms.

Probabilistic Dependency Graphs.

Our presentation of PDGs is slightly
different from (but equivalent to) that of

Richardson and Halpern 2021
, which
the reader is encouraged to consult for more details and intuition.
At a high level, a PDG
is
just
a collection of cpds and causal assertions,
weighted by confidence. More precisely:

Definition 2
.

A PDG
\mathbdcal{M}\!=\!(\mathcal{X}\mskip-2.0mu,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})

is
a directed hypergraph

(\mathcal{X}\mskip-2.0mu,\mathcal{A})

whose nodes
are
variables,
together with
probabilities
\mathbb{P}

and
confidence vectors

\boldsymbol{\alpha}\!=\![\alpha_{a}]_{a\in\mathcal{A}}

and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\!=\![\beta_{a}]_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
,
so that
each
S{\overset{\smash{\mskip-5.0mu\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}{\rightarrow}}T\!\in\!\mathcal{A}
is associated with:

•

a conditional probability distribution

\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T|S)

on the target variables given
values of
the source variables,

•

a weight

\beta_{a}\in\smash{\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,}

indicating
the modeler’s confidence in
the cpd
\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T|S)
,

and

•

a weight
\smash{\alpha_{a}\in\mathbb{R}}

indicating
the modeler’s confidence in the functional dependence of

T\mskip-2.0mu
on
S\mskip-2.0mu

expressed by

a
.

If
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq\mathbf{0}
and
\alpha_{a}\!>0
implies
\beta_{a}\!>0
, we
write
\boldsymbol{\beta}\gg\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
call
\mathbdcal{M}

proper
.
Note that
\boldsymbol{\beta}\gg\boldsymbol{\alpha}
if
\boldsymbol{\beta}>\mathbf{0}
.

One significant advantage of PDGs is their modularity:
we can combine the information in
\mathbdcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathbdcal{M}_{2}

by taking the union of their variables and the disjoint union of their arcs (and associated data) to get a new PDG, denoted
\mathbdcal{M}_{1}+\mathbdcal{M}_{2}
.

As mentioned in the introduction,
a PDG contains two types of information:
“structural” information, in the hypergraph
\mathcal{A}
and
weights
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, and “observational” data,
in the cpds
\mathbb{P}
and weights
\boldsymbol{\beta}
.
PDG semantics are based on two scoring functions
that quantify discrepancy between
each type of information and a distribution

\mu\in\Delta\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}
over its variables.

The
observational incompatibility
of
\mu
with
\mathbdcal{M}
, which
can be thought of as a “distance” between
\mu
and the cpds of
\mathbdcal{M}
,
is given by the weighted sum of relative entropies:

\displaystyle\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu):=\sum_{S{\overset{\smash{\mskip-5.0mu\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}{\rightarrow}}T\mathrlap{\,\in\mathcal{A}}}\beta_{a}\,I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu(T,S)\;\Big\|\;\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T|S)\mu(S)\Big).

Under a standard interpretation of the relative entropy
I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu\;\|\;p)\mskip-1.5mu=\mskip-1.5mu\Ex_{\mu}[\log\frac{\mu}{p}]
,

\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
measures the excess cost of using codes optimized for the cpds of
\mathbdcal{M}

(weighted by their confidences),
when reality is
distributed according to
\mu
.

The second scoring function measures
the extent to which

\mu

is incompatible with
a causal picture consisting of independent mechanisms
along each hyperarc.
This is captured by the

structural incompatibility

(of
\mu
with
\mathbdcal{M}
),
and given by

\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu):=\,\Big\lparen\;\sum_{S{\overset{\smash{\mskip-5.0mu\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}{\rightarrow}}T\mathrlap{\,\in\mathcal{A}}}\alpha_{a}\,\H_{\mu}(T|S)\Big\rparen-\H(\mu).

Note that

\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}

does not depend on the cpds
of
\mathbdcal{M}
, nor the possible values of the
variables; it
is defined purely in terms of
the weighted hypergraph structure
(\mathcal{A},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.

If the observational and structural information conflict,
then the distribution(s) that best represent a PDG
will depend
on the importance of structure relative to observation,
as captured by a trade-off parameter
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}\in[0,1]

that controls the convex combination

(1-{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma})\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}+{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
.
So as to simplify the math
and match the notation in
previous work (
Richardson and Halpern 2021
,
Richardson 2022
),
we mostly use a rescaled variant with a different parameterization.
Using

\gamma:=\nicefrac{{{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}}}{{(1-{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma})}}\in[0,\infty]
,
define the overall scoring function:

\displaystyle[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}

\displaystyle(\mu):=\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)+\gamma\,\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)

(2)

\displaystyle\!\!\!=\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}}$}\Big(\,(1-{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma})\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)+{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}\,\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)\,\Big)

\displaystyle=\Ex\nolimits_{\mu}\bigg[\,\sum_{S{\overset{\smash{\mskip-5.0mu\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}{\rightarrow}}T\mathrlap{\,\in\mathcal{A}}}\log\frac{\mu(T|S)^{\beta_{a}-\gamma\alpha_{a}}}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T|S)^{\beta_{a}}}\bigg]-\gamma\H(\mu).

Let
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}:=\argmin_{\mu}[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)
denote
the set of optimal distributions at a particular value
\gamma
.
One natural conception of inference in PDGs is then parameterized by

{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
:
to do
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference
in
\mathbdcal{M}
is to respond to probabilistic queries in a way that is sound with respect to every
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
.
It is not too difficult to see that when
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq\gamma\boldsymbol{\alpha}
,
(
2
) is strictly convex, which ensures that

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
is a singleton.
This paper demonstrates that

{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference
is tractable for such PDGs.

The limiting behavior of the
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-semantics as
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}\to 0
,
which we denote
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}
and call the
0^{+}
​-semantics
,
has some special properties.
If
\mathbdcal{M}
is proper, then

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}
contains precisely one distribution.
This distribution intuitively
reflects an extreme empirical
view: observational data trumps causal structure.
Note that in the absence of a causal picture
(
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\mathbf{0}
), this
corresponds to the well-established
practice of selecting
the maximum entropy distribution consistent with some observational constraints
[
Jaynes 1957
]
.
One should be careful to distinguish

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}

from
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}
,
the set of distributions that minimize

\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
; the latter set
includes

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}

[
Richardson and Halpern 2021
, Prop 3.4]
,
but may also contain other distributions.

This paper also shows how to efficiently answer queries with respect
to the unique distribution in
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}
, which we call

0^{+}
​-inference
.

Given a PDG
\mathbdcal{M}
, the smallest possible value of its scoring function,

\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}:=\inf_{\mu}\,[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu),

is known as its
\gamma
-inconsistency
and is interesting in its own right:

\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}

is arguably a “universal” loss
function
[
Richardson 2022
]
.

Interior-Point Methods and Convex Optimization.

Interior-point methods provide an iterative way of approximately solving linear programs in polynomial time
[
Karmarkar 1984
]
.
With the theory of “symmetric cones”, these methods were extended in the 1990s to handle second-order cone programs (SOCPs) and semidefinite programs (SDPs), which allow more expressive constraints.
But the constraints that these methods can handle are insufficient for
our purposes. We need what have been called
exponential cone constraints
.
The
exponential cone
is the convex set

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} K_{\mskip-1.0mu\exp}:=\big\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})&:x_{1}\geq x_{2}e^{x_{3}/x_{2}}\!,\,x_{2}>0\big\}\\[-2.58334pt]
\quad\mathbin{\cup}\,\big\{(x_{1},0,x_{3}&):x_{1}\geq 0,\,x_{3}\leq 0\big\}\qquad\subset\mathbb{\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,}^{3}.\end{aligned}

Let
K\mskip-2.0mu:=\mskip-2.0muK_{\exp}^{p}\mskip-2.0mu\times\mskip-1.0mu[0,\infty]^{q}\subset\smash{\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,}^{n}
be a product of
p
exponential cones and
q=n-3k
non-negative orthants.
An
exponential conic program
is then an optimization problem of the form

\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\mathbf{x}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{c}^{\sf T}\mathbf{x}\quad{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}$}}\penalty\ \penalty\ A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b},\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{x}\in K,

(3)

where
\mathbf{c}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{n}
is some cost vector,
the function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathbf{c}^{\sf T}\mathbf{x}
is called the
objective
,
and
\mathbf{b}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{m}
,
\mathbf{A}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{m\times n}
encode linear constraints.

Nesterov et al. 1999
first established that such problems can be solved in polynomial time, but incur double the memory and eight times the time, compared to the symmetric counterparts. These drawbacks were eliminated in
Skajaa and Ye 2015
.
The algorithm that seems to display the best empirical performance
[
Dahl and Andersen 2022
]
, however, was only recently shown to
run in polynomial time
[
Badenbroek and Dahl 2021
]
.

Disciplined Convex Programming
[
Grant 2004
]
is a
compositional approach to convex optimization that
imposes certain restrictions on how problems can be specified.
A problem conforming to those rules is said to be
dcp
,
and can be efficiently compiled to a standard form

[
Agrawal et al. 2018
]
,
which in our case is an exponential conic program.
Only two rules are relevant to us: a constraint of the form

(x,y,z)\in K_{\exp}
is
dcp iff
x
,
y
, and
z
are affine transformations of the
optimization variables,
and a linear program
augmented
with dcp
constraints is dcp.
Because all the optimization problems that we give are
of this form,
we can easily compile them
to exponential conic programs even if they do not exactly conform to (
3
).

3
Inference as a Convex Program

Here is an obvious, if inefficient,
way
of calculating

\Pr_{\!\cal M}(Y|X{=}x)
in a
probabilistic model
\cal M
.
First compute an explicit representation of the joint distribution

\Pr_{\!\cal M}\in\Delta\mskip-2.0mu\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}
,
then marginalize to

\Pr_{\!\cal M}(X,Y)
and condition on
X{=}x
.
For a factor graph or BN,
each step is
straightforward;
the problem is the exponential time and space required to represent
\Pr_{\!\cal M}(\mathcal{X})
explicitly.
A key feature of inference algorithms for BNs and FGs is that they
do not represent joint distributions in this way.
For PDGs, though, it is not
obvious that
we can calculate the
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-semantics,
even if
we know it is unique, and
we ignore the space required to represent it (as we do in this section).
Note that
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference is already an optimization problem by definition:

\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\mu}\quad[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)\quad{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}$}}\quad\mu\in\Delta\mskip-2.0mu\mathcal{V}\mskip-2.0mu\mathcal{X}.

For small enough

\gamma
,
it is even convex.
But can we solve it efficiently?
With exponential cone constraints,
the answer is yes, as we show in
Section

3.2
.
Moreover, we can compute the
0^{+}
​-semantics with a sequence of two exponential conic programs (
Section

3.3
).
To give a flavor of our constructions and ease into
the more complicated ones, we begin by
minimizing

\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
, the simpler of the two scoring functions.

3.1
Minimizing Incompatibilty
(
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\boldsymbol{=}\mathbf{0}
)

When
\gamma=0
, we want to find minimizers of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
,
which is a
weighted sum of conditional relative entropies.
There is a straightforward connection between the exponential cone and
relative entropy:
if
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p}\in\Delta\{1,\ldots,n\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
are points on
a probability simplex,
then
(-\mathbf{u},\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p})\in K_{\exp}^{n}
if and only if

\mathbf{u}
is an upper bound on
\mathbf{m}\log\frac{\mathbf{m}}{\mathbf{p}}
,
the pointwise contribution to relative entropy at each outcome.
Thus, perhaps unsurprisingly, we can use an exponential conic program to
find minimizers of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
.
If all beliefs are unconditional and over the same space,
the construction is standard;
we review it here, so that we can build upon it.

Warm-up.

Consider a PDG with
only one variable
X

with

\mathcal{V}\mskip-2.0muX=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Suppose further that every arc
j\in\mathcal{A}=\{1,\ldots,k\}

has
T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{j}}=\{X\}
and
S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{j}}=\emptyset
.
Then each
\mathbb{P}_{\!j\mskip-2.0mu}(X)
can be identified with a vector
\mathbf{p}_{j}\in[0,1]^{n}
, and all
k
of them can conjoined to form a matrix
\mathbf{P}=[\,p_{ij}]\in[0,1]^{n\times k}
.
Similarly, a candidate distribution
\mu
can be identified with
\mathbf{m}\in[0,1]^{n}
.
Now consider a matrix
\mathbf{U}=[u_{i,j}]\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{n\times k}

that, intuitively, gives an upper bound on
the contribution to
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
due to each edge and value of
X
.
Observe that

\displaystyle(-\mathbf{U},\penalty\ [\mathbf{m},\,...\,,\mathbf{m}],\penalty\ \mathbf{P})

\displaystyle\in K_{\exp}^{n\times k}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\forall i,j.\penalty\ \penalty\ u_{ij}

\displaystyle\geq m_{i}\log(\nicefrac{{m_{i}}}{{p_{ij}}})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\forall j.\penalty\ \penalty\ {\textstyle\sum_{i}}u_{ij}

\displaystyle\geq I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu\;\|\;p_{j})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle{\textstyle\sum_{i,j}}\beta_{j}u_{ij}

\displaystyle\geq{\textstyle\sum_{j}}\beta_{j}I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu\;\|\;p_{j})

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}^{\sf T}\mathbf{U}\boldsymbol{\beta}

\displaystyle\geq\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}(\mu).

(4)

So now, if
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{m})
is a solution to the convex program

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{U}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{1}^{\sf T}\mathbf{U}\boldsymbol{\beta}\quad{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}$}}\quad

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}^{\sf T}\mathbf{m}=1,

\displaystyle(-\mathbf{U},\;

\displaystyle[\mathbf{m},\,...\,,\mathbf{m}],\;\mathbf{P})\in K_{\exp}^{n\times k},

then (a)
the
objective value
\mathbf{1}^{\sf T}\mathbf{U}\boldsymbol{\beta}

equals
the inconsistency
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0}
, and (b)
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}
,
meaning
\mu
minimizes
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
.

The General Case.

We now show how the same construction can be used to find
a distribution
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}

for an arbitrary PDG
\mathbdcal{M}=(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{P},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})
.

To further simplify the presentation,
for each arc
a\in\mathcal{A}
, let

\mathcal{V}a:=\mathcal{V}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})

denote all joint settings of
a
’s source and target variables, and
write

\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}:=\sqcup_{a\in A}\mathcal{V}a=\{(a,s,t):a\in\mathcal{A},\,(s,t)\in\mathcal{V}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\}

for the set of all choices of an arc together with values of its source and target.
For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
we can regard
\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})
and
\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})
, both distributions over
\{S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\}
,
as vectors of shape
\mathcal{V}a
.
As before, we introduce an optimization variable
\mathbf{u}
that packages together
all of the relevant pointwise upper bounds.
To that end, consider a
vector

\mathbf{u}=[u_{a,s,t}]\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}

in the optimization problem

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\mu,\mathbf{u}}

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{\mathrlap{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}\beta_{a}\,u_{a,s,t}

(5)

\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}

\displaystyle\quad\mu\in\Delta\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X},

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.\penalty\

\displaystyle\big(-{\mathbf{u}}_{a},\,\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}),\,\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\big)\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}a}.

where
\mathbf{u}_{a}=[u_{a,s,t}]_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{V}a}
consists of those
components of
\mathbf{u}
associated with arc
a
.
Note that
the marginals

\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})
and
\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})

are affine transformations of
\mu
, so (
5
) is dcp.
A straightforward generalization of the logic in (
4
) gives us:

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Proposition 1
.

If
(\mu,\mathbf{u})
is a solution to (
5
), then

\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}
,
and

\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}u_{a,s,t}=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0}
.

Thus, a solution to (
5
)
encodes a distribution that minimizes
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
, and
the (0-)inconsistency
of
\mathbdcal{M}
.
This is a start, but to do

0^{+}
​-inference,
among the minimizers of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}

we must find the unique distribution in
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}
,
while for
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference (
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}>0
), we need to find the optimizers of

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
.
Either way, we must consider

\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}

in addition to
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
.

3.2
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
-Inference
for small

\boldsymbol{\gamma}\boldsymbol{>}\mathbf{0}

When
\gamma>0
is small enough,
the scoring function (
2
) is not only convex,
but admits a straightforward representation as an exponential conic program.
To see this, note that (
2
) can be rewritten
[
Richardson and Halpern 2021
, Prop 4.6]
as:

\displaystyle[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)=

\displaystyle-\gamma\H(\mu)-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}\,\Ex_{\mu}\log{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})}

(6)

\displaystyle\penalty\penalty\displaystyle+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\gamma\alpha_{a}-\beta_{a})\H_{\mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}).

The first term,

-\gamma\H(\mu)
,
is strictly convex and has a well-known
translation into an exponential cone constraint;
the second one linear in
\mu
.
If
0<\gamma\leq\min_{a}\frac{\beta_{a}}{\alpha_{a}}
, then
every summand of the last term is a negative conditional entropy, and
can be captured by an exponential cone constraint.
The only wrinkle is that it is possible for a user to specify that some
\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t\mid s)=0
, in which case the linear term
is undefined.
The result is a requirement that
\mu(s,t)=0
at such points,
which we can instead encode directly with linear constraints.
To do this formally,
divide
\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
into two parts:

\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}:=\{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)>0\}
and

\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{0}:=\{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)=0\}
.
Armed with this notation, consider upper bound vectors

\mathbf{u}=[u_{a,s,t}]_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathbf{v}=[v_{w}]_{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}
,
in the following optimization problem:

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\mu,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)u_{a,s,t}\,+\gamma\sum_{\mathclap{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}}v_{w}

(7)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}

\displaystyle\quad\mu\in\Delta\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X},\quad(-\mathbf{v},\mu,\mathbf{1})\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}},

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.\penalty\

\displaystyle\big(-\mathbf{u}_{a},\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}),\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\big)\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}a},

\displaystyle\forall(a,s,t)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{0}\!.\penalty\ \mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\mskip 2.0mus,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\mskip 2.0mut)=0.

This optimization problem may look complex, but it
falls out of
(
6
)
fairly
directly,
and gives us what we wanted.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Proposition 2
.

If
(\mu,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
is a solution to (
7
),
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq\gamma\boldsymbol{\alpha}
,
then

\mu
is the unique element of

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
, and
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}

equals the objective of (
7
) evaluated at
(\mu,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
.

3.3
Calculating the
\mathbf{0}^{\boldsymbol{+}}
​-semantics
(
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\to}\mathbf{0}
)

Section

3.1
shows how to find a distribution
\nu
that minimizes

\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
—but to do

0^{+}
​-inference,
we need to find the minimizer
that, uniquely among them, best minimizes

\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
.
It turns out this can be done by
using
\nu
to construct a second optimization problem.
The justification requires two more results;
we start by characterizing the minimizers of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Proposition 3
.

If
\mathbdcal{M}
has arcs
\mathcal{A}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq 0
,
the minimizers of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
all have the same conditional
marginals along
\mathcal{A}
.
That is, for all
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}

and all
S{\overset{\smash{\mskip-5.0mu\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}{\rightarrow}}T\in\mathcal{A}

with
\beta_{a}>0
, we have

\mu_{1}(T,S)\mu_{2}(S)=\mu_{2}(T,S)\mu_{1}(S)
.

0
0
footnotetext:
Intuitively, this assserts

\mu_{1}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})=\mu_{2}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})
,
but also handles cases where some

\mu_{1}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s)
or
\mu_{2}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s)

equals zero.

As a result, once we find one minimizer
\nu
of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}

(e.g., via (
5
)),
it suffices to optimize
\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
among distributions that have the same
conditional marginals along
\mathcal{A}
that
\nu
does.
This presents another problem:
\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}

is typically not convex.
Fortunately, if we constrain to distributions that minimize
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
, then it is.
Moreover, on this restricted domain, it can be represented
with dcp exponential cone constraints.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Proposition 4
.

If
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}
,
then

\underset{{\mathbdcal{M}}}{\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}}(\mskip-0.5mu\mu\mskip-1.0mu)\!=\!\sum_{\mathclap{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}}\mskip-1.0mu\mu(\mskip-1.5muw\mskip-1.5mu)\log\!\bigg(\!{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mu(\mskip-1.5muw\mskip-1.5mu)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.5pt}{$\displaystyle{\,\prod_{\mathclap{a\in\mathcal{A}}}\mskip-1.0mu\nu\big(\mskip-1.0muT\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-2.2mu(\mskip-2.0muw\mskip-2.0mu)\mskip-0.5mu\big|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-2.0mu(\mskip-2.0muw\mskip-2.0mu)\mskip-2.0mu\big)^{\!\alpha_{\!{}_{a}\!}}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mu(\mskip-1.5muw\mskip-1.5mu)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.5pt}{$\textstyle{\,\prod_{\mathclap{a\in\mathcal{A}}}\mskip-1.0mu\nu\big(\mskip-1.0muT\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-2.2mu(\mskip-2.0muw\mskip-2.0mu)\mskip-0.5mu\big|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-2.0mu(\mskip-2.0muw\mskip-2.0mu)\mskip-2.0mu\big)^{\!\alpha_{\!{}_{a}\!}}}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mu(\mskip-1.5muw\mskip-1.5mu)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\prod_{\mathclap{a\in\mathcal{A}}}\mskip-1.0mu\nu\big(\mskip-1.0muT\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-2.2mu(\mskip-2.0muw\mskip-2.0mu)\mskip-0.5mu\big|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-2.0mu(\mskip-2.0muw\mskip-2.0mu)\mskip-2.0mu\big)^{\!\alpha_{\!{}_{a}\!}}}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mu(\mskip-1.5muw\mskip-1.5mu)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\,\prod_{\mathclap{a\in\mathcal{A}}}\mskip-1.0mu\nu\big(\mskip-1.0muT\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-2.2mu(\mskip-2.0muw\mskip-2.0mu)\mskip-0.5mu\big|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-2.0mu(\mskip-2.0muw\mskip-2.0mu)\mskip-2.0mu\big)^{\!\alpha_{\!{}_{a}\!}}}$}}}\!\mskip-2.0mu\bigg)\mskip-2.0mu,\!

(8)

where
\{\nu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
are the
marginals along the arcs
\mathcal{A}

shared by all distributions in
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}
(per
3
),
and
S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-1.0mu(\mskip-1.0muw\mskip-1.0mu),T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\mskip-1.0mu(\mskip-1.0muw\mskip-1.0mu)
are the values of variables
S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}
and
T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}
in
w
.

If we already know a distribution
\nu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}
,
perhaps by solving (
5
), then
the denominator of (
8
) does not depend on
\mu

and so is constant in our search for minimizers of

\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
.
For ease of exposition, aggregate these values into a vector

\mathbf{k}:=\Big[\penalty\ \prod_{\smash{a\in\mathcal{A}}}\nu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{\!{}_{a}\!}}\Big]_{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}\penalty\ \raisebox{-4.30554pt}{.}

(9)

We can now capture
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}
with a convex program.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Proposition 5
.

If
\nu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}

and
(\mu,\mathbf{u})

solves the problem

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\mu,\mathbf{u}}

\displaystyle\quad\smash{\mathbf{1}^{\sf T}\mathbf{u}}

(10)

\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}

\displaystyle\quad(-\mathbf{u},\mu,\mathbf{k})\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \quad\mu\in\Delta\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X},

\displaystyle\forall

\displaystyle S{\overset{\smash{\mskip-5.0mu\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}{\rightarrow}}T\in\mathcal{A}.\penalty\ \penalty\ \mu(S,T)\,\nu(S)=\mu(S)\,\nu(S,T),

then
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}=\{\mu\}

and
\mathbf{1}^{\sf T}\mathbf{u}=\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)
.

Running (
10
) through a convex solver gives rise to the
first algorithm
that can reliably find
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}
.

4
Polynomial-Time Inference Under Bounded Treewidth

We have now seen how
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference
(for small
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
) can be reduced to convex optimization
over joint distributions
\mu
—but
\mu
grows exponentially with the number of variables in the PDG,
so we do not yet have a tractable inference algorithm.
We now show how
\mu
can be replaced with a tree marginal over the PDG’s structure.
What makes this possible is a key independence property of traditional graphical models,
which we now prove
holds for PDGs as well.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Theorem 6

(Markov Property for PDGs)
.

If
\mathbdcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathbdcal{M}_{2}
are PDGs
over sets
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
of variables, respectively,

then

\mathcal{X}_{1}

and
\mathcal{X}_{2}

are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{X}_{1}\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}

in every

\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
,
for all
\gamma>0
and

\gamma=0^{+}
.

For the remainder of this section, fix a PDG
\mathbdcal{M}
and a tree decomposition
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
of
\mathbdcal{M}
’s hypergraph.
One significant consequence of
Theorem

6
is that, in the
search for optimizers of (
2
), we
need consider only distributions that satisfy those independencies,
all of which can be represented as a tree marginal
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\{\mu_{C}\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(C)\}_{C\in\mathdcal C}

over
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Corollary 6.1
.

If
\mathbdcal{M}
is a PDG with arcs
\mathcal{A}
,

(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
is a tree decomposition of
\mathcal{A}
,

\gamma>0
, and

\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
, then there exists a tree marginal
\boldsymbol{\mu}
over
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
such that
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\mu
.

For convenience, let

\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C:=\{(C,c):C\in\mathdcal C,c\in\mathcal{V}(C)\}

be
the set of all choices of a cluster together with a setting of its variables.
Like before, we start by optimizing
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
, this time
over calibrated tree marginals
\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
which we identify with vectors

\boldsymbol{\mu}\cong[\mu_{C}(C{=}c)]_{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}
.
We need the conditional marginals
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})
of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
along every arc
a
in order
to calculate
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
; fortunately, they are readily available.
Since
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
is a tree decomposition,
we know
S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}
and
T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}
lie entirely within some cluster
C_{\!a}\in\mathdcal C
,
and
\Pr_{\!\boldsymbol{\mu}}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})=\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})
if
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is calibrated.
For
\mathbf{u}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}
, consider the problem

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u}}

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{\mathrlap{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}\beta_{a}\,u_{a,s,t}

(11)

\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}

\displaystyle\quad\forall C\in\mathdcal C.\penalty\ \mu_{C}\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(C),

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.\penalty\ \big(

\displaystyle\!-\!\mathbf{u}_{a},\,\mu_{C\!_{a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},\mskip-2.0muT\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}),\,\mu_{C\!_{a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\big)\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}a}

\displaystyle\forall(C,D)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{T}.\penalty\ \penalty\ \mu_{C}(C\cap D)=\mu_{D}(C\cap D),

where again
\mathbf{u}_{a}
is the restriction of
\mathbf{u}
to components associated with
a
.
Problem (
11
) is similar to (
5
), except
that it requires local marginal constraints to restrict our search to calibrated tree marginals.
It is analogous to problem

CTree-Optimize-KL

of
Koller and Friedman 2009
.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Proposition 7
.

If
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u})
is a solution to (
11
), then

(a)

\boldsymbol{\mu}
is a calibrated, with
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}
, and

(b)

the objective of (
11
) evaluated at
\mathbf{u}
equals
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0}
.

We can now find a minimizer of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
and
compute
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0}
without storing a joint distribution.

But to do anything else, we must deviate from the template laid out in
Section

3
.

Dealing with Joint Entropy.

In the construction of (
11
),
we rely heavily on the fact that each term of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}

depends only on local marginal distributions
\mu_{C_{\mskip-2.0mua}}\!(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})

and
\mu_{C_{\mskip-2.0mua}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})
.
The same is not true of
\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
, which depends on the joint entropy
\H(\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
of the entire distribution.
At this point we should point out an important
reason to restrict our focus to trees:
it allows the joint entropy to be expressed
in terms of the cluster marginals
[
Wainwright et al. 2008
]
,
by

-\H(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=-\sum_{C\in\mathdcal C}\H(\mu_{C})\penalty\ +\penalty\ \sum_{\mathclap{(C,D)\in\mathcal{T}}}\H_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(C\cap D).

(12)

Even so,
it is not obvious that
(
12
) can be
captured with dcp exponential cone constraints.
(Exponential conic programs can minimize negative entropy,
but not positive entropy, which is concave.)
We now describe how this can be done.

Choose a root node
C_{0}
of the tree decomposition, and orient each edge of
\mathcal{T}
so that it points away from
C_{0}
.
Each cluster
C\in\cal C
, except for
C_{0}
, then has a parent cluster
\mathrm{Par}(C)
;
define
\mathrm{Par}(C_{0}):=\emptyset
to be an empty cluster, since
C_{0}
has no parent.
Finally, for each
C\in\mathdcal C
, let
\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}:=C\mathbin{\cap}\mathrm{Par}(C)
denote the
the set of
\mathbf{v}
ariables that cluster
C
has in
\mathbf{c}
ommon with its
\mathbf{p}
arent cluster.

1
1

1

Different choices of
C_{0}

yield
different definitions of
\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}
,
and ultimately optimization problems of different sizes;
the optimal choice
can be found with Edmund’s Algorithm
[
Chu 1965
]
,
which computes a directed analogue of the minimum spanning tree.
As
\cal T
is now a directed tree, this definition allow us to express
(
12
) in a more useful form:

\displaystyle-\H(

\displaystyle\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=-\H(\mu_{C_{0}})-\!\sum_{(C\to D)\mathrlap{\in\mathcal{T}}}\H_{\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(D\mid C)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\cal C}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{V}(C)}\mu_{C}(C{=}c)\log\frac{\mu_{C}(C{=}c)}{\mu_{C}(\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c))},

(13)

where
\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c)
is the restriction of the
joint value
c\in\mathcal{V}(C)

to the variables
\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}\subseteq C

.
Crucially, the denominator of (
13
) is an affine transformation of
\mu_{C}
.
The upshot: we have rewritten the joint entropy
as a sum of functions of the clusters, each of which can be captured with a dcp exponential cone constraint.
This gives us analogues of the problems
in
Sections

3.2
and
3.3
that
operate on tree marginals.

Finding tree marginals for
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.44446pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference.

The ability to decompose the joint entropy as in (
13
) allows us to adapt
(
7
)
to operate on calibrated tree marginals, rather than joint distributions.
Beyond the changes already present in (
11
),
the key
is to replace
the exponential cone constraint

(-\mathbf{v},\mu,\mathbf{1})\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}
,
which captures the entropy
of
\mu
,
with

\big(-\mathbf{v},\;\boldsymbol{\mu},\,[\,\mu_{C}(\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c))\,]_{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}\big)\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C},

which captures the entropy of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, by
(
13
).
Over vectors

\mathbf{v},\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}
and

\mathbf{u}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}
,
the problem becomes:

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)u_{a,s,t}+\gamma\sum_{\mathclap{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}}v_{C,c}

(14)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}

\displaystyle\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\mu_{C\!_{a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t\mid s)

\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}

\displaystyle\quad\forall C\in\mathdcal C.\penalty\ \mu_{C}\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(C),

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.\penalty\ \big(

\displaystyle\!-\!\mathbf{u}_{a},\,\mu_{C\!_{a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},\mskip-2.0muT\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}),\,\mu_{C\!_{a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\big)\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}a},

\displaystyle\forall(a,s,t)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{0}\!.\penalty\ \mu_{C\!_{a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\mskip 2.0mus,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\mskip 2.0mut)=0,

\displaystyle\forall(C,D)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{T}.\penalty\ \penalty\ \mu_{C}(C\cap D)=\mu_{D}(C\cap D),

\displaystyle\big(-\mathbf{v},\;

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu},\,[\,\mu_{C}(\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c))\,]_{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}\big)\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Proposition 8
.

If
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
is a solution to (
14
)
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq\gamma\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, then

\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is the unique element of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
,
and the objective of (
14
) at
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
equals
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
.

A related use of (
13
) is
to enable an analogue of
(
10
) that searches over tree marginals (rather than joint distributions),
to find
a compact representation of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}
.
We begin with a straightforward adaptation of
the relevant machinery in
Section

3.3
.
Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\nu}{=}\{\nu_{C}:C\in\mathdcal C\}
is a calibrated tree marginal over the tree decomposition
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
representing a distribution
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}
, say obtained by solving (
11
).
For
C\in\mathdcal C
, let
\mathcal{A}_{C}:=\{a\in\mathcal{A}:C_{a}=C\}
be the set of
arcs assigned to cluster
C
, and let

\mathbf{k}:=\smash{\bigg[}\prod_{\smash{\mathrlap{a\in\mathcal{A}_{C}}}}\nu_{C}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))^{\alpha_{a}}\smash{\bigg]\mathclose{\vphantom{\Big|}}_{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}

be the analogue of (
9
) for a cluster tree.
Once again, consider

\mathbf{u}:=[u_{(C,c)}]_{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}

in the optimization problem

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u}}

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{1}^{\sf T}\mathbf{u}

(15)

\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}

\displaystyle\quad\forall C\in\mathdcal C.\penalty\ \mu_{C}\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(C),

\displaystyle\big({-}\mathbf{u},\,\boldsymbol{\mu},\,\,

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}\odot\big[\;\mu_{C}(\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c))\;\big]_{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}\big)\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C},

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\nu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})=\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\nu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})

\displaystyle\forall(C,D)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{T}.\penalty\ \penalty\ \mu_{C}(C\cap D)=\mu_{D}(C\cap D).

The biggest change is in the second constraint:
the upper bounds
[u_{(C,c)}]_{c\in\mathcal{V}C}
for cluster
C
now account only
for the additional entropy not already modeled by

C
’s
ancestors.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Proposition 9
.

If
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u})
is a solution to (
15
),
then
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is a calibrated tree marginal and
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0^{+}}=\{\Pr_{\!\boldsymbol{\mu}}\}
.

At this point, standard algorithms can use
\boldsymbol{\mu}

to answer probabilistic queries about
\Pr_{\!\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in polynomial time
[
Koller and Friedman 2009
, §10.3.3]
.

From
9
and
8
, it follows that

{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference
(for small
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
, and for
0^{+}
)
can be reduced to a (pair of) convex optimization problem(s) with
a polynomial number of variables and constraints.
All that remains
is to show that such a problem can be solved in polynomial time.
For this, we turn to interior-point methods.
As
(
14
) and (
15
) are dcp, they
can be transformed via established methods
[
Agrawal et al. 2018
]
into
a standard form
that can be solved in polynomial time by commercial solvers
[
ApS 2022
,
Domahidi et al. 2013
]
.
Threading the details of our constructions through
the analyses of
Dahl and Andersen 2022

and
Nesterov et al. 1999

results in
our main theorem.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Theorem 10
.

Let
\mathbdcal{M}=(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})

be a proper discrete PDG with
N=|\mathcal{X}|
variables each taking at most
V
values
and
A=|\mathcal{A}|
arcs,
in which each component of

\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{A}}

and
\mathbb{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}

is specified in binary with
at most

k
bits.
Suppose that
\gamma\in\{0^{+}\}\cup(0,\,\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\beta_{a}}{\alpha_{a}}]
.
If
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
is a tree decomposition of
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
of width
T

and
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}

is the unique calibrated tree marginal over
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})

that represents the
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-semantics of
\mathbdcal{M}
,
then

(a)

Given
\mathbdcal{M}
,
\gamma
, and
\epsilon>0
,
we can find a calibrated tree marginal
\epsilon
close in
\ell_{2}
norm to

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}

in time

\displaystyle O\bigg\lparen

\displaystyle|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\Big\lparen\log|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|+\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big\rparen k^{2}\log k\bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\subseteq\tilde{O}\Big\lparen k^{2}|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle\subseteq\tilde{O}\Big\lparen k^{2}(N+A)^{4}\,V^{4(T+1)}\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}}\Big\rparen.

(b)

The unique tree marginal closest to
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}

in which every component is represented with a
k
-bit binary number,
can be calculated in time

\tilde{O}\Big\lparen k^{2}|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\Big\rparen\subseteq\tilde{O}\Big\lparen k^{2}(N\!+\!A)^{4}\,V^{4(T+1)}\Big\rparen.

Observe that the dependence on the precision is
\log(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})
, which is optimal in the sense that, in general, it takes time
\Omega(\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})
to write down the binary representation of any number within
\epsilon
of a given value.

2
2

2

More precisely: if a value
x
is chosen uniformly from
[0,1]
, then
with probability
1-\sqrt{\epsilon}
the binary representation of every
y\in[x-\epsilon,x+\epsilon]

has at least
\lfloor\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}}\rfloor-1
bits.

In practice, this procedure can be used as if it were an exact algorithm,
with no more overhead than that incurred by floating point arithmetic.

5
APPROXIMATION, HARDNESS, AND A DEEP CONNECTION BETWEEN INCONSISTENCY AND INFERENCE

While
Theorem

10
gives us a way of doing inference to machine precision in polynomial time, which is the typical use case of an exact algorithm, it is not technically an exact inference algorithm.
Indeed, if we require binary representations of numbers, exact inference for PDGs is technically not possible in finite time: in a PDG, the exact answer to an inference query may be an irrational number (even if all components of
\mathbb{P}
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
are rational).
This leads us to formulate approximate inference more precisely.

Definition 3

(approximate PDG inference)
.

An instance of problem
APPROX-PDG-INFER
is a tuple
(\mathbdcal{M},\gamma,Q,\epsilon)
, where

\mathbdcal{M}
is a PDG with variables
\mathcal{X}
,
\gamma\in\{0^{+}\}\cup[0,\infty]

is the relative importance of structural information,

Q
is a conditional probability query of the form
“
\Pr(Y{=}y|X{=}x)=?
”, where
X,Y\subseteq\mathcal{X}
and
(x,y)\in\mathcal{V}(X,Y)
,
and
\epsilon>0
is the precision desired for the answer.
A solution to this problem instance is a pair of numbers

(r^{-},r^{+})

such that

\displaystyle r^{-}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\mu\in[\hskip-16.8269pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-16.8269pt]^{*}_{\gamma}}\mu(Y{=}y|X{=}x)\leq r^{-}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad r^{+}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\mu\in[\hskip-16.8269pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-16.8269pt]^{*}_{\gamma}}\mu(Y{=}y|X{=}x)\geq r^{+}-\epsilon.

The problem we solved in
Sections

3
and
4

is the special case in which
\mathbdcal{M}
is assumed to be proper and
\gamma\in\{0^{+}\}\cup(0,\min_{a}\frac{\beta_{a}}{\alpha_{a}})
. This is enough to ensure there is a unique optimal distribution
\mu^{*}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
, with respect to which we must answer all queries. In this case, the definition above
essentially amounts to providing a single
number
p
such that
p-\epsilon\leq\mu^{*}(Y{=}y|X{=}x)\leq p+\epsilon
.
We call this easier subproblem
APPROX-INFER-CVX
.
We will also be interested in the unconditional variants of both inference
problems, in which
no additional evidence is supplied (i.e.,
X=\emptyset
).
We now define the analogous problem of approximately calculating a PDG’s degree of inconsistency.

Definition 4

(approximate inconsistency calculation)
.

An instance of problem

APPROX-CALC-INC
is a triple
(\mathbdcal{M},\gamma,\epsilon)
, where

\mathbdcal{M}
is a PDG,
\gamma\geq 0
, and
\epsilon>0
is the desired precision.
A solution to this problem instance is
a number
r
such that
|\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}-r|<\epsilon
.

The interior point method behind
Theorem

10
solves
APPROX-CALC-INC
in the process of finding a tree marginal for inference.
But, technically, it does not solve
APPROX-PDG-INFER
.
A solution to
APPROX-PDG-INFER
is
a conditional probability, not a calibrated tree marginal.
While a calibrated tree marginal does allow us to compute conditional
probabilities,
an
\epsilon
-close tree marginal does not give us
\epsilon
-close answers
to probabilistic queries, especially those conditioned on improbabable events (i.e., finding
\Pr(Y{=}y|X{=}x)
when
\Pr(X{=}x)\approx 0
).
Nevertheless, because precision is so cheap,
the interior point method behind
Theorem

10

can still be used as a subroutine to solve
APPROX-INFER-CVX
.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Theorem 11
.

APPROX-INFER-CVX
can be solved in

\displaystyle\tilde{O}\bigg\lparen\!(N\!+\!A)^{4}V^{4(T+1)}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon\mu^{\mskip-2.0mu*\!}(x)\!}\Big[\log\frac{\beta^{\max}\!}{\beta^{\min}\!}+\log\frac{1}{\epsilon\mu^{\mskip-2.0mu*\!}(x)}\Big]\!\bigg\rparen

time,

where
\mu^{\mskip-2.0mu*\!}(x)
is the probability of the event
X{=}x
in the optimal
distribution
\mu^{*}
,

\beta^{\max}:=\max_{{a\in\mathcal{A}}}\beta_{a}
is the largest observational confidence,

\text{and}\qquad\beta^{\min}:=\begin{cases}\penalty\ \displaystyle\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\{\beta_{a}:\beta_{a}>0\}&\text{if}\penalty\ \gamma=0^{+}\\[-2.15277pt]
\penalty\ \hfill\gamma\penalty\ \penalty\ &\text{if}\penalty\ \gamma>0\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{.}\end{cases}

The factor of
\log(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mu^{*}\!(x)}})
is unusual,
but even exact inference algorithms typically must write down
\mu^{*}(X{=}x)

on the way to calculating
\mu^{*}(Y{=}x|X{=}x)
, which implicitly incurs
a cost of at least
\log(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mu^{*}\!(x)}})
.
A Bayesian network with
N
variables in which cpds are articulated to precision
k

can have nonzero marginal probabilities as small as
2^{-Nk}
,
in which case the additional worst case overhead for small probabilities is linear.
We conjecture that it is not possible to form smaller marginal probabilities with PDGs, although the question remains open.
Algorithmically speaking,

Theorem

11
extends
Theorem

10

in three key ways.

1.

We must request additional precision
to ensure that the marginal probabilities deviate at most
\epsilon

from the true ones. This sense of of approximation effectively
bounds the
\ell_{1}
norm of
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
while
Theorem

10
(a) bounds it
\ell_{2}
norm and
(b) bounds its
\ell_{\infty}
norm.

2.

We must introduce a loop to refine precision until we have a suitably precise estimate of
\Pr(X{=}x)
.

3.

Rather than directly dividing our estimate of
\Pr(Y{=}y,X{=}x)
by our estimate of
\Pr(X{=}x)
, we calculate something slightly more stable.

See the proof for details.
One immediate corrolary of
Theorem

11
is that

APPROX-INFER-CVX

\subseteq\mathsf{EXP}
, without the assumption of bounded treewidth.

It may be worth noting that there is at least one instance in the literature where
approximate
Bayesian Network (BN) inference is tractable for a subclass of models other than those of bounded treewidth:
Dagum and Luby 1997
give a randomzied algorithm for the special class of Bayesian Networks that do not have extreme conditional probabilities. Specifically they show that, assuming a network with
N
nodes, the inference problem is in
\mathsf{RP}(N,\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})
.
In addition to restricting to a differet class of models (bounded conditional probabilities, but not bounded treewidth), their approximation
algorithm in another significant respect: it is polynomial in
\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}}
, rather than in
\log(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})
.
Thus, the time it requires is exponential with respect to the number of requested digits,
while our algorithm takes linear time.

Because PDGs generalize BNs, approximate inference
for PDGs is at least as hard as it is for BNs.

Proposition 12

(
[
Roth 1996
]
)
.

APPROX-PDG-INFER
is #P-hard.

Thus, the exponential time of
Theorem

11

is the best we could have hoped for, in the general case.
The argument is due to
Roth 1996
,
although we have altered it somewhat.

Figure 1:

Accuracy and resource costs for the methods in
Section

3
.
Left: a scatter plot of several algorithms on random PDGs of
\approx 10
variables. The x-axis is
the difference in scores

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)-[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu^{*})+10^{-15}
,
where
\mu
is the method’s output,
and
\mu^{*}
achieves best (smallest) known value of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
.
(Thus, the best solutions lie on the far left.)
The
y
axis is the time required to compute
\mu
.
Our methods are in gold
(0^{+}
​-inference) and violet (
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference, for
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}>0
); the baselines (black-box optimizers applied directly to (
2
)) are in green.
The area of each circle is proportional to the size of the optimization problem, as measured by

n_worlds
:=

|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}|
.
Right: how the same methods scale in run time, as
|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}|
increases.

Our approach to
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference computes

\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
as a side effect.
But suppose that we were interested in calculating only this inconsistency.
Might there be a more direct, asymptotically easier way
to do so? In general, the answer is no.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Theorem 13
.

(a)

Determining whether
there is a distribution
that satisfies all cpds of a PDG
is NP-hard.

(b)

Calculating a PDG’s degree of inconsistency (exactly) is #P hard.

(c)

APPROX-CALC-INC
is #P hard,
even for fixed
\gamma\geq 0
and
\epsilon>0
.

Richardson and Halpern 2021
’s original approach to
inferring the probability of

Y
in a PDG
\mathbdcal{M}
was to minimize their combined inconsistency.
The idea is to add a hypothesis distribution
h(Y)
to
\mathbdcal{M}
, and
adjust
h
to minimize the overall inconsistency
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
.
Parts (b) and (c) of
Theorem

13
significantly undermine this approach, because even just calculating
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
is intractable.
Typically minimizing a function is more difficult than evaluating it, so one might imagine the intractability of
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
to be merely the first of many difficulties—yet it turns out to be the only one.
There is a strong sense in which being able to calculate inconsistency is enough to perform inference efficiently.
Specifically,
with oracle access to the inconsistency
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
,

Richardson and Halpern 2021
’s approach gives right answer with the best possible asymptotic time complexity.
Thus, while it may not be a practical inference algorithm, it is a powerful reduction from inference to inconsistency calculation.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Theorem 14
.

(a)

There is an
O(\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})
-time reduction
from unconditional

APPROX-INFER-CVX
to the problem of determining which of two PDGs is more inconsistent,
using
O(\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})
subroutine calls.

(b)

There is an

\displaystyle O\left(\log\frac{\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}}{\gamma\epsilon\,\mu^{*}\mskip-2.0mu(x)}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\epsilon\,\mu^{*}\mskip-2.0mu(x)}\right)\vphantom{\Bigg|}

time
reduction
from
APPROX-INFER-CVX
to
APPROX-CALC-INC
using
O(\log(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})\log\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\mu^{*}\mskip-2.0mu(x)}})
calls to the inconsistency subroutine.

(c)

There is also an
O(|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|)
reduction
from
APPROX-CALC-INC
to
APPROX-INFER-CVX
.
With the additional assumption of bounded treewidth,
this is linear in the number of variables in the PDG.

Recall that the runtime of
O(\log(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}}))
achieved by part (a) is optimal, because it is the complexity of writing down an answer, which in general requires
\log(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})
bits.
While it is a clean result, part (a) is unsatisfying as a complexity result because it relies heavily on being able to compare the two inconsistencies in constant time.
Part (b) fleshes out the algorithm of part (a) more precisely
by reducing to inconsistency approximation (which we now know is computable),
and also extends the procedure to handle to conditional probability queries.
This leads to a significantly more complex analysis, and a more expensive reuction, although it is possible that much of the difference in the costs is due to loose bounds in our analysis.
Part (c) is a straightforward observation in light of
the results in
Section

4
.

To summarize: in the range of
\gamma
’s in which we have an (approximate) inference algorithm for PDGs, (approximately) calculating a PDG’s degree of inconsistency is at least as difficult.
For PDGs of bounded treewidth, the two problems are equivalent, and can be solved in polynomial time.

6
Experiments

We have given the first algorithm to provably do inference in polynomial
time, but that does not mean that it is the best way of answering queries in practice;
it also makes sense to use black-box optimization tools such as
Adam
[
Kingma and Ba 2014
]
or L-BFGS
[
Fletcher 2013
]

to find minimizers of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
.
Indeed, this scoring function has several properties
that make it highly amenable to such methods: it is
infinitely differentiable,
\gamma
-strongly convex, and its
derivatives have simple closed-form expressions.
So it may seem surprising that
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
poses
a challege to standard optimization tools—but it does,
even when we optimize directly over joint distributions.

Synthetic Experiment 1 (
over joint distributions
).

Repeatedly do the following.
First, randomly generate a small PDG
\mathbdcal{M}
containing
at most 10 variables and 15 arcs.
Then for various values of

\gamma\in\{0,0^{+},10^{-8},\ldots,\min_{a}\frac{\beta_{a}}{\alpha_{a}}\}
,
optimize
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)
over joint distributions
\mu
,
in one of two ways.

(a)

Use
cvxpy

[
Diamond and Boyd 2016
]

to feed
one of problems (
5
,
7
,
10
)
to the MOSEK solver
[
ApS 2022
]
, or

(b)

Choose a learning rate and a representation of
\mu
in terms of optimization variables
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then run a standard optimizer (Adam or L-BFGS) built into
pytorch

[
Paszke et al. 2019
]

to optimize
\theta

until
\mu_{\theta}
converges to a minimizer of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}

(or a time limit is reached).
Keep only the best result across all learning rates.

Figure 2:
Left: Memory footprint.
The convex solver (violet, gold)
requires more memory than baselines (green).
Right: Analogue of
Figure

1
for the cluster setting.
Here there is even more separation between exponential conic optimization
(gold, violet) and black-box optimization (greens).
The grey points represent belief propagation, which is fastest and most accurate—but only applies in the special case when
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\gamma\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.

The results are shown in
Figure

1
.
Observe that the convex solver (gold, violet) is significantly more accurate than the baselines,
and also much faster for small PDGs.
Our implementation of
0^{+}\!
-inference (gold) also appears to scale better than L-BFGS
in this regime, although
that of
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference (purple) seems to scale much worse.
We suspect that the difference comes from
cvxpy
’s complilation process,
because the two use similar amounts of memory (
Figure

2
),
and so are problems of similar sizes.

Synthetic Experiment 2 (
over tree marginals
).

For PDGs of bounded treewidth,
6.1
allows us to express these optimzation problems compactly not just for the convex solver, but for the black-box baseline approaches as well.
We adapt the previous experiment for tree marginals as follows.
First randomly sample a maximal graph
G
of tree-width
k
, called a

k
-tree
[
Patil 1986
]
;
then generate a PDG
\mathbdcal{M}
whose hyperarcs lie within cliques of
G
.
This ensures that the maximal cliques of
G
form a tree-decomposition
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
of
\mathbdcal{M}
’s underlying
hypergraph.
We can now proceed as before:
either encode
