(
11
,
14
,
15
)
as disciplined convex programs in
cvxpy
,
or use
torch
to directly minimize
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\Pr_{\!\boldsymbol{\mu}})

amongst tree marginals
\boldsymbol{\mu}
over
(\mathdcal C,\mathcal{T})
.

In the latter case, however, there is now an additional difficulty:
it is not easy to strictly enforce the calibration constraints with the black-box methods.
Common practice is to instead add extra loss terms to “encourage” calibration—but
it can still be worthwile for the optimizer to simply incur that loss
in order to violate the constraints.
Thus, for fairness, we must recalibrate the
the tree marginals returned by all methods before evaluation.
The result is an even more significant advantage for the convex solver;
see
Figure

2
.

Evaluation on BNs.

We also applied the procedure of the Synthetic Experiment 2
to the smaller BNs in the

bnlearn
repository,
and found similar results (but with fewer examples; see
Section

C.3
).
But for a PDG that happens to also be a BN, it is possible to use belief propagation, which is much faster and at least as accurate.

Explicit details about all of our experiments,
and many more figures, can be found in
Appendix

C
.

7
Discussion and Conclusion

In this paper, we have provided the first practical algorithm for
inference in PDGs.
In more detail, we have defined a parametric family of PDG inference notions,
given a fixed-parameter tractable inference algorithm for a subset of these parameters,
proven our algorithm correct, implemented it, and
shown our code to empirically outperform baselines.
Yet many questions about PDG inference remain open.

Asymptotically, there may be a lot of room for improvement.
Our implementation runs in time
\tilde{O}(N^{4})
, and our analysis suggests one of time
\tilde{O}(N^{2.872})
.
But assuming bounded tree-width, most graph problems, including
inference inference for BNs and FGs, can be solved in time
O(N)
.

Furthermore, we have shown how to do inference for only a subset of
possible paramer values, specifically,
when
either
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq\gamma\boldsymbol{\alpha}
or
\boldsymbol{\beta}\gg\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.
The remaining cases are also of interest, and likely require different techniques.
When
\boldsymbol{\beta}=0
and
(\mathcal{A},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
encodes the structure of a BN,
for instance,
inference is about characterizing the BN’s independencies.
While we do not know how to tackle
the inference problem in the general setting,
our methods can be augmented with the convex-concave procedure

[
Yuille and Rangarajan 2003
]
to obtain an inference
algorithm that applies slightly more broadly; see
Appendix

B
.
We imagine that this extension could also be useful for computing with PDGs
beyond the specific inference problem considered in this paper.

Given the long history of improvements to our
understanding of inference for
Bayesian networks,
we are optimistic that
faster and more general
inference algorithms
for PDGs
are possible.

Appendix A
Proofs

Our results fall broadly into three categories:

1.

Foundational results about PDGs that we needed to prove to get an
inference procedure, but which are likely to be generally useful
for anyone working with PDGs
(
Section

A.1
);

2.

Correctness and efficiency results, showing that the optimization
problems we present in the main paper give the correct answers,
and that they can be formulated and solved in polynomial time;
(
Section

A.2
)

3.

Hardness results, i.e.,
Theorem

14
and
the constructions and lemmas needed to support it
(
Section

A.3
).

A.1
Novel Results about PDGs

.

See
3

Proof.

For contradiction, suppose that
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}
, but
there is some
(\hat{a},\hat{s},\hat{t})\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
such that
\beta_{a}>0
and

\mu_{1}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\hat{t},S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\hat{s})\mu_{2}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\hat{s})\neq\mu_{2}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\hat{t},S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\hat{s})\mu_{1}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\hat{s}).

For
t\in[0,1]
,
let
\mu_{t}:=(1-t)\mu_{0}+t\,\mu_{1}
as before.
Then define

\displaystyle F(t):=I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{t}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\;\Big\|\;\mu_{t}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\Big).

Since
\mu_{0}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})
and
\mu_{1}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})
are joint distributions voer two variables, with different conditional marginals, as above,
Lemma

16
applies, and so
F(t)
is strictly convex.

Let

\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}\setminus\hat{a}}:=\sum_{a\neq\hat{a}}\beta_{a}I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\;\|\;\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}))

be the observational incompatibility loss, but without the term corresponding to edge
\hat{a}
.
Since
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}\setminus\hat{a}}
is convex in its argument, it is in particular convex along the segment from
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
; that is, for
t\in[0,1]
, the function
t\mapsto\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}\setminus\hat{a}}(\mu_{t})
is convex.
Therefore, we know that the function

\displaystyle G(t):=\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu_{t})=\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}\setminus a}(\mu_{t})+\beta_{a}\,F(t),

is
strictly
convex.
But then this means
\mu_{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
satisfies

\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu_{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}})<\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu_{0}),

contradicting the premise that
\mu_{0}
minimizes
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
(i.e.,
\mu_{0}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}
).
Therefore, it must be the case that all distributions in
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}
have the same conditional marginals, as promised.
∎

.

See
4

Proof.

This is mostly a simple algebraic manipulation. By definition:

\displaystyle\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)

\displaystyle=-\H(\mu)+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{a}\H_{\mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})

\displaystyle=\Ex_{\mu}\left[-\log\frac{1}{\mu}+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{a}\log\frac{1}{\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}\mu(w)\left[\log\mu(w)+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\log\frac{1}{\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{a}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}\mu(w)\log\bigg\lparen{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mu(w)\penalty\ }$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\penalty\ \prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{a}}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mu(w)\penalty\ }$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\penalty\ \prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{a}}}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mu(w)\penalty\ }$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\penalty\ \prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{a}}}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mu(w)\penalty\ }$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\penalty\ \prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{a}}}$}}}\bigg\rparen

But, by
3
, if we restrict
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}
, then the conditoinal marginals in the denominator do not depend on the particular choice of
\mu
; they’re shared among all
\nu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}
.
∎

.

See
6

\text{Or symbolically: }\qquad\quad\mathbdcal{M}_{1}\mathbin{+}\mathbdcal{M}_{2}\penalty\ \models\penalty\ \mathcal{X}_{1}\mathbin{\bot\!\!\!\bot}\mathcal{X}_{2}\mid\mathcal{X}_{1}\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}.

Proof.

Note that,
save for the joint entropy, every summand the scoring function
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}:\Delta(\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}_{2})
, is a function of the conditional marginal of
\mu
along some edge.
In particular, those terms that correspond to edges of
\mathbdcal{M}_{1}
can be computed from the marginal
\mu(\mathcal{X}_{1})
, while those that correspond to edges of
\mathbdcal{M}_{2}
can be computed from the marginal
\mu(\mathcal{X}_{2})
.
Therefore, there are functions
f
and
g
such that:

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)=f(\mu(\mathcal{X}_{1}))+g(\mu(\mathcal{X}_{2}))-\gamma\H(\mu).

To make this next step extra clear, let
\mathbf{X}:=\mathcal{X}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{2}
and

\mathbf{Z}:=\mathcal{X}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{1}
, be the variables unique to each PDG, and
\mathbf{S}:=\mathcal{X}_{1}\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}
be the set of variables they have in common, so that
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{Z})
is a partition of all variables
\mathbf{X}_{1}\cup\mathbf{X}_{2}
.
Now define a new distribution
\mu^{\prime}\in\Delta(\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}_{2})
by

\bf\mu^{\prime}(X,S,Z):=\mu(S)\mu(Z\mid S)\mu(X\mid S)\hskip 22.99988pt\Big(\penalty\ =\mu(X,S)\mu(Z\mid S)=\mu(Z,S)\mu(X\mid S)\penalty\ \Big).

One can easily verify that
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Z}
are independent given
\mathbf{S}
in
\mu^{\prime}
(by construction), and the alternate forms on the right make it easy to see that
\mu(\mathcal{X}_{1})=\mu^{\prime}(\mathcal{X}_{1})
and
\mu(\mathcal{X}_{2})=\mu^{\prime}(\mathcal{X}_{2})
.
Furthermore, for any
\nu^{\prime}(\mathbf{X,S,Z})
, we can write

\displaystyle\H(\nu)

\displaystyle=\H_{\nu}(\mathbf{X,S,Z})=\H_{\nu}(\mathbf{X,S})+\H_{\nu}(\mathbf{Z}\mid\mathbf{X,S})

\displaystyle=\H_{\nu}(\mathbf{X,S})+\H_{\nu}(\mathbf{Z}\mid\mathbf{X,S})-\H_{\nu}(\mathbf{Z}\mid\mathbf{S})+\H_{\nu}(\mathbf{Z}\mid\mathbf{S})

\displaystyle=\H_{\nu}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{S})+\H_{\nu}(\mathbf{Z}\mid\mathbf{S})-\I_{\nu}(\mathbf{Z};\mathbf{X}|\mathbf{S}),

where
\I_{\nu}(\bf X;Z|S)
, the conditional mutual information between
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Z}
given
\mathbf{S}
(in
\nu
), is non-negative, and equal to zero if and only if
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Z}
are conditionally independent given
\mathbf{S}

[see, for instance,
MacKay 2003
, §1]
.
So
\I_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Z}|\mathbf{S})=0
, and

\H_{\mu^{\prime}}=\H_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{S})+\H_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{Z}|\mathbf{S})
.
Because
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
share marginals on
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
, while the terms
\H(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{S})
and
\H(\mathbf{Z}|\mathbf{S})
depend only on these marginals, respectively, we also know that
\H_{\mu}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{S})=\H_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{S})
and
\H_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z}|\mathbf{S})=\H_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{Z}|\mathbf{S})
; thus we have

\displaystyle\H(\mu)

\displaystyle=\H_{\mu}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{S})+\H_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z}\mid\mathbf{S})-\I_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z};\mathbf{X}|\mathbf{S})

\displaystyle=\H(\mu^{\prime})-\I_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z};\mathbf{X}|\mathbf{S}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)

\displaystyle=f(\mu(\mathcal{X}_{1}))+g(\mu(\mathcal{X}_{2}))-\gamma\H(\mu)

\displaystyle=f(\mu^{\prime}(\mathcal{X}_{1}))+g(\mu^{\prime}(\mathcal{X}_{2}))-\gamma\H(\mu^{\prime})+\gamma\I_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z};\mathbf{X}|\mathbf{S})

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu^{\prime})+\gamma\I_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z};\mathbf{X}|\mathbf{S}).

But conditional mutual information is non-negative, and by assumption,
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)
is minimal. Therefore, it must be the case that

\I_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z};\mathbf{X}|\mathbf{S})=\I_{\mu}(\mathcal{X}_{1};\mathcal{X}_{2}\mid\mathcal{X}_{1}\cap\mathcal{X}_{2})=0,

showing that
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
are conditionally independent given the varaibles that they have in common.

(The fact that
\I_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z};\mathbf{X}|\mathbf{S})=\I_{\mu}(\mathcal{X}_{1};\mathcal{X}_{2}\mid\mathcal{X}_{1}\cap\mathcal{X}_{2})
is both easy to show and an instance of a well-known identity; see CIRV2 in Theorem 4.4.4 of
Halpern 2017
, for instance.)
∎

.

See
6.1

Proof.

The set of distributions that can be represented by a calibrated tree marginal over
(\mathdcal C,\cal T)
is the same as the set of distributions that can represeted by a factor graph for which
(\mathdcal C,\cal T)
is a tree decomposition.
One direction holds because any such product of factors “calibrated”, via message passing algorithms such as belief propogation, to form a tree marginal.
The other direction holds because
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
itself is a product of factors that decomposes over
(\mathdcal C,\cal T)
.

Alternatively, this same set of distributions that satisfy the independencies of the Markov Network obtained by connecting every pair of variables that share a cluster.
More formally, this network is the graph
G:=(\mathcal{X},E:=\{(X{-}Y):\exists C\in\mathdcal C.\penalty\ \{X,Y\}\subseteq C\})
.
Also,
G
happens to chordal as well, which we prove at the end.

Using only the PDG Markov property (
Theorem

6
), we now show that every independence described by
G
also holds in every distribution
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
.
Suppose that,
for sets of variables
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
,

\I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{Z})
is an independence
described by
G
.
This means
[
Koller and Friedman 2009
, Defn 4.8]
that
if
X\in\mathbf{X}
,
Y\in\mathbf{Y}
, and
\pi
is a path in
G
between them, then
some node along
\pi
lies in
\mathbf{Z}
.

Let
\cal T^{\prime}
be the graph that results from removing each edge
(C{-}D)\in\cal T
that satisfies
C\cap D\subseteq\mathbf{Z}
, which is a disjoint union
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\mathcal{T}_{n}
of subtrees that have no clusters in common.
To parallel this notation, let
\mathdcal C_{1},\ldots,\mathdcal C_{n}
be their respective vertex sets.
Note that for every edge
e=(C{-}D)\in\cal T^{\prime}
, there must by definiton be some variable
U_{e}\in(C\cap D)\setminus\mathbf{Z}
.

We claim that no subtree
\mathcal{T}_{i}
can have both a cluster
D_{X}
containing a variable
X\in\mathbf{X}\setminus\mathbf{Z}
and also a cluster
D_{Y}
containing a variable
Y\in\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{Z}
.
Suppose that it did.
Then the (unique) path in
\cal T
between
D_{X}
and
D_{Y}
, which we label

\hbox to372.83pt{\vbox to16.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 186.41138pt\lower-8.15973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{118.09384pt}{2.75pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{175.49005pt}{4.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad,

and moreover, this path is disjoint from
\mathbf{Z}
.
This contradicts our assumption that every path in
G
between a member of
\mathbf{X}
and a member of
\mathbf{Y}
must intersect with
\mathbf{Z}
, and so no subtree can have both a cluster containing a variable
X\in\mathbf{X}\setminus\mathbf{Z}
and also one containing
Y\in\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{Z}
.

We can now partition the clusters as
\mathdcal C=\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{X}}\sqcup\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
, where

\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{X}}
is the set of the clusters that belong to subtrees
\mathcal{T}_{i}
with a cluster containing some
X\in\mathbf{X}\setminus\mathbf{Z}
, and
its
\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
is its complement, which in particular contains those subrees have some
Y\in\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{Z}
.
Or, more formally, we define

\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{X}}:=\penalty\ \bigcup_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
(\cup\mathdcal C_{i})\cap(\mathbf{X}\setminus\mathbf{Z})\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}}\,\mathdcal C_{i}\quad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}:=\penalty\ \bigcup_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
(\cup\mathdcal C_{i})\cap(\mathbf{X}\setminus\mathbf{Z})=\emptyset\end{subarray}}}\,\mathdcal C_{i}\quad.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}:=\cup\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{X}}
set of all variables appearing in the clusters
\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{X}}
; symmetrically, define
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}:=\cup\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
.

We claim that
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}\subset\mathbf{Z}
.
Choose any variable
U\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
.
From the definitions of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
, this means
U
is a member of some cluster
C\in\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{X}}
, and also a member of a cluster
D\in\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
.
Recall that the clusters of each disjoint subtree
\mathcal{T}_{i}
either fall entirely within
\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{X}}
or entirely within
\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
by construction.
This means that
C
and
D
, which are on opposite sides of the partition, must have come from distinct subtrees.
So, some edge
e=(C^{\prime}{-}D^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}
along the (unique) path from
C
to
D
must have been removed when forming
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, which by the definition of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, means that
(C^{\prime}\cap D^{\prime})\subset Z
.
But by the running intersection property (tree marginal property 2), every cluster along the path from
C
to
D
must contain
C\cap D
—in particular, this must be true of both
C^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime}
.
Therefore,

U\in C\cap D\subset C^{\prime}\cap D^{\prime}\subset\mathbf{Z}.

So
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}\subset\mathbf{Z}
, as promised. We will rather use it in the equivalent form
(\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+})\cup\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{Z}
.

Next, since
(\mathdcal C,\cal T)
is a tree decomposition of
\mathcal{A}
, each hyperarc
a\in\mathcal{A}
can be assigned to some cluster
C_{a}
that contains all of its variables; this allows us to lift the cluster partition
\mathdcal C=\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{X}}\sqcup\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
to a partition
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{X}}\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
of edges, and consequently, a partition of PDGs
\mathbdcal{M}=\mathbdcal{M}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbin{+}\mathbdcal{M}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
.
Concretely: let
\mathbdcal{M}_{\mathbf{X}}
be the sub-PDG of
\mathbdcal{M}
induced by restricting to the variables
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
arcs
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{X}}=\{a\in\mathcal{A}:C_{a}\in\mathdcal C_{\mathbf{X}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
; define
\mathbdcal{M}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
symmetrically. (To be explicit: the other data of
\mathbdcal{M}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\mathbdcal{M}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
are given by restricting each of
\{\mathbb{P},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
, respectively.)

This partition of
\mathbdcal{M}
allows us to use the PDG Markov property.
Suppose that for some
\gamma>0
that
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
.
We can then apply
Theorem

6
, to find that

\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
are independent given
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}
.
We use standard standard properties of random variable independence

[CIRV1-5 of
Halpern 2017
, Theorem 4.4.4]
to find that
\mu
must satisfy:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\bot\!\!\!\bot}\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}\mid\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}

\displaystyle\implies\qquad(\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\setminus\mathbf{Z})

\displaystyle\mathbin{\bot\!\!\!\bot}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}\setminus\mathbf{Z})\mid(\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+})\cup\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\big[\,\text{CIRV3}\,\big]

\displaystyle\implies\qquad(\mathbf{X}\setminus\mathbf{Z})

\displaystyle\mathbin{\bot\!\!\!\bot}(\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{Z})\mid(\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+})\cup\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\big[\,\text{by CIRV2, as $\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}$ and $\mathbf{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+}$}\,\big]

\displaystyle\implies\qquad(\mathbf{X}\setminus\mathbf{Z})

\displaystyle\mathbin{\bot\!\!\!\bot}(\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{Z})\mid\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\big[\,\text{since $(\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{X}}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Y}}^{+})\cup\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{Z}$}\,\big]

\displaystyle\iff\qquad\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\bot\!\!\!\bot}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\hskip-40.00006pt\big[\,\text{standard; e.g., Exercise 4.18 of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{halpern2017reasoning}{\@@citephrase{[}}{\@@citephrase{]}}}}\,\big]

Using only the PDG Markov property, we have now shown that every independence
modeled by the Markov Network
G
also holds
in every distribution
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
. Moreover,
G
is chordal (as we will prove momentarily),
and is well-known that distributions that have the independencies of a chordal graph can be can be represented by tree marginals
[
Koller and Friedman 2009
, Theorem 4.12]
.
Therefore, there is a tree marginal
\boldsymbol{\mu}
representing every
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
.

Claim 14.1
.

G
is chordal.

Proof.

Suppose that
G
contains a loop
X{-}Y{-}Z{-}W{-}X
.
Suppose further, for contradiction, that neither
X
and
Z
nor
Y
and
W
share a cluster.
Given a variable
V
, it is easy to see that property (2) of the tree decomposition ensures that the subtree
\mathcal{T}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{T}
induced by the clusters
C\in\mathdcal C
that contain
V
, is connected.
By assumption,
{\cal T}(Y)
and
{\cal T}(W)
must be disjoint.
There is an edge between
Y
and
Z
, so some cluster must contain both variables, meaning
{\cal T}(Y)\cap{\cal T}(Z)
is non-empty.
Similarly,
{\cal T}(Z)\cap{\cal T}(W)
is non-empty because of the edge between
Z
and
W
.
This creates an (indirect) connection in
\cal T
between
{\cal T}(Y)
and
{\cal T}(W)
. Because
\cal T
is a tree, and
{\cal T}(Y)\cap{\cal T}(W)=\emptyset
,
every path from a cluster
C_{1}\in{\cal T}(Y)
to a cluster
C_{2}\in{\cal T}(W)
must pass through
{\cal T}(Z)
, which is not part of
{\cal T}(Y)
or
{\cal T}(W)
.

{\cal T}(X)
and
{\cal T}(Y)
intersect as well, meaning that, for any
C\in{\cal T}(X)
, there is a (unique) path from
C
to that point of intersection, then across edges of
{\cal T}(Y)
, then edges of
{\cal T}(Z)
, and finally connects to the clusters of
{\cal T}(W)
. And also, since
\cal T
is a tree, that path must be unique.
The problem is that there is also an edge between
X
and
W
, so there’s some cluster that contains
X
and
W
; let’s call it
C_{0}
.
It’s distinct from the cluster
D_{0}
that contains
Z
and
W
, since no cluster contains both
X
and
Z
by assumption.
The unique path from
C_{0}
to
D_{0}

intersects with
{\cal T}(Y)
.
But now
W\in C_{0}\cap D_{0}
, and by the running intersection property, every node along this unique path must contain
W
as well.
But this contradicts our assumption that
W
is disjoint from
Y
! So
G
is chordal.
∎

∎

A.2
Correctness and Efficiency of Inference via Exponential Conic Programming

.

See
1

Proof.

Suppose that
(\mu,\mathbf{u})
is a solution to (
5
).
The exponential cone constraints ensure that, for every
(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
,

u_{a,s,t}\geq\mu(s,t)\log\frac{\mu(s,t)}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)\mu(s)}

where
\mu(s,t)
and
\mu(s)
, as usual, are shorthand for
\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t)
and
\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s)
, respectively.

Suppose, for contradiction, that one of these inequalities is strict at some an index
(a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
for which
\beta_{a^{\prime}}>0
.
Explicitly, this means

u_{a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}>\mu(s_{0},t_{0})\log\frac{\mu(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a^{\prime}\mskip-2.0mu}(t^{\prime}|s^{\prime})\mu(s^{\prime})}.

In that case, we can define a vector
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=[u^{\prime}_{a,s,t}]_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}
which is identical to
\mathbf{u}
, except that at
(a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
, it is halfway between the two quantities described as different above. More precisely:

u^{\prime}_{a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}u_{a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\log\mu(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\log\frac{\mu(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t^{\prime}|s^{\prime})\mu(s^{\prime})}.

Note that
u^{\prime}_{a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}<u_{a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
,
and also that, by construction,
(\mu,\mathbf{u}^{\prime})
also satisfies the constraints of (
5
).
In more detail: for
(a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
it doesn’t violate the associated exponential cone constraint, as

\left(\text{formally:}\quad u^{\prime}_{a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}u_{a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\log\mu(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\log\frac{\mu(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a^{\prime}\mskip-2.0mu}(t^{\prime}|s^{\prime})\mu(s^{\prime})}>\mu(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\log\frac{\mu(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a^{\prime}\mskip-2.0mu}(t^{\prime}|s^{\prime})\mu(s^{\prime})}\right),

and
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
remains unchanged at the other indices, and so satisfies the constraints at those indices, becasuse
\mathbf{u}
does.
But now, because
u^{\prime}_{a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}<u_{a^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
, and
\beta_{a^{\prime}}>0
, we also have

\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}u^{\prime}_{a,s,t}>\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}u^{\prime}_{a,s,t}.

Thus the objective value at
(\mu,\mathbf{u}^{\prime})
is strictly
smaller than the one at
(\mu,\mathbf{u})
, both of which are feasible points.
This contradicts the assumption that
(\mu,\mathbf{u})
is optimal.
We therefore conclude that none of these inequalities can be strict at points where
\beta_{a}>0
.
This can be compactly written as:

\displaystyle\forall(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}.\quad\beta_{a}u_{a,s,t}

\displaystyle=\beta_{a}\mu(s,t)\log\frac{\mu(s,t)}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)\mu(s)}

\displaystyle\implies\qquad\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}u_{a,s,t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}\mu(s,t)\log\frac{\mu(s,t)}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)\mu(s)}=\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu).

In other words, the objective of problem (
5
) at

(\mu,\mathbf{u})
is equal to the observational incompatibility
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)
of
\mu
with
\mathbdcal{M}
.
And, because
(\mu,\mathbf{u})
minimizes this value among all joint distributions,
\mu
must be a minimum of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
.

More formally: assume for contradiciton that
\mu
is not a minimizer of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
. Then there would be some other distribution
\mu^{\prime}
for which
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu^{\prime})<\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)
.
Let
\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}:=[\mu^{\prime}(s,t)\log\frac{\mu^{\prime}(s,t)}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)\mu^{\prime}(s)}]_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}
. Clearly
(\mu^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime})
satisfies the constraints of the problem, and moreover,

\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}u_{a,s,t}=\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)>\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu^{\prime})=\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}u^{\prime}_{a,s,t},

contradicting the assumption that the
(\mu,\mathbf{u})
is optimal for problem (
5
). Thus,
\mu
is a minimizer of
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
, and the objective value is
\inf_{\mu}\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu)=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0}
, as desired.
∎

.

See
2

For convenience, we repeat problem (
7
)
(left) and an equivalent variant of it that we implement (right) below.

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\mu,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)u_{a,s,t}\,+\gamma\sum_{\mathclap{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}}v_{w}

(
7
)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}

\displaystyle\quad\mu\in\Delta\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X},\quad(-\mathbf{v},\mu,\mathbf{1})\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}},

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.\penalty\

\displaystyle\big(-\mathbf{u}_{a},\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}),\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\big)\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}a},

\displaystyle\forall(a,s,t)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{0}\!.\penalty\ \mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\mskip 2.0mus,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\mskip 2.0mut)=0;

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\mu,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)u_{a,s,t}\,+\gamma\sum_{\mathclap{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}}v_{w}

(
7
b)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}\beta_{a}\,\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}

\displaystyle\quad\mu\in\Delta\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X},\quad(-\mathbf{v},\mu,\mathbf{1})\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}},

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.\penalty\

\displaystyle\big(-\mathbf{u}_{a},\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}),\big[\,\mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}=s)\big]_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{V}a}\big)\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}a},

\displaystyle\forall(a,s,t)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{0}\!.\penalty\ \mu(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\mskip 2.0mus,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}\mskip 2.0mut)=0.

Proof.

We start with the problem on the left, which is (
7
) from the main text.
Suppose that
(\mu,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
is a solution to (
7
).
The exponential constraints ensure that

\forall(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}.\penalty\ u_{a,s,t}\geq\mu(s,t)\log\frac{\mu(t|s)}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\forall w\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{X}.\penalty\ v_{w}\geq\mu(w)\log\mu(w).

As in the previous proof, we claim that these must hold with equality (except possibly for
u_{a,s,t}
at indices satisfying
\beta_{a}=\gamma\alpha_{a}
, when it doesn’t matter).
This is because otherwise one could reduce the value of a component of
u
or
v
while still satisfying all of the constraints, to obtain a strictly smaller objective, contradicing the assumption that
(\mu,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
minimizes it.

Thus,
\mathbf{v}
is a function of
\mu
, as is every value of
\mathbf{u}
that affects the objective value of (
7
), meaning that this objective value can be written as a function of
\mu
alone:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)u_{a,s,t}\penalty\ +\gamma\sum_{\mathclap{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}}v_{w}\penalty\ -\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\mu(s,t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)\left\lparen\mu(s,t)\log\frac{\mu(t|s)}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}\right\rparen\penalty\ +\penalty\ \gamma\sum_{\mathclap{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}}\mu(w)\log\mu(w)\penalty\ -\penalty\ \sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\mu(s,t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)\sum_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{V}a}\left\lparen\mu(s,t)\log\frac{\mu(t|s)}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}\right\rparen-\gamma\H(\mu)-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\sum_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\mu(s,t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)\sum_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{V}a}\mu(s,t)\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}-\log\frac{1}{\mu(t|s)}\right\rparen-\gamma\H(\mu)-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\Ex_{\mu}[\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\beta_{a}\!-\!\alpha_{a}\gamma)\Ex_{\mu}[-\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})]-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\beta_{a}\!-\!\alpha_{a}\gamma)\H_{\mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})-\gamma\H(\mu)-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\Ex_{\mu}[\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\Big(-\alpha_{a}\gamma-(\beta_{a}\!-\!\alpha_{a}\gamma)\Big)\Ex_{\mu}[\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})]+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\alpha_{a}\gamma\!-\!\beta_{a})\H_{\mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})-\gamma\H(\mu)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}\Ex_{\mu}[\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})]+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\alpha_{a}\gamma\!-\!\beta_{a})\H_{\mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})-\gamma\H(\mu).

( In the third step, we were able to convert
\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}
to
\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
because, as usual in when dealing with information-therotic quantities, we interpret
0\log\frac{1}{0}
as equal to zero, which is its limit. )

The algebra, for the right side variant
(
b
)
is slightly simpler. In this case the middle conic constraint is almost the same, except for that
\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)
has been replaced with
1
, and so it ensures that
u_{a,s,t}=\mu(s,t)\log\mu(t\mid s)
(i.e., the same as before, but without the probability in the denomiator). So,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)u_{a,s,t}\penalty\ +\gamma\sum_{\mathclap{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}}v_{w}\penalty\ -\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}\beta_{a}\,\mu(s,t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)\mu(s,t)\log\mu(t|s)\penalty\ +\penalty\ \gamma\sum_{\mathclap{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}}\mu(w)\log\mu(w)\penalty\ -\penalty\ \sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}\beta_{a}\,\mu(s,t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)\sum_{(s,t)\mathrlap{\in\mathcal{V}a}}\mu(s,t)\log\mu(t|s)-\gamma\H(\mu)-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}\,\sum_{(s,t)\mathrlap{\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}\mu(s,t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\alpha_{a}\gamma\!-\!\beta_{a})\H_{\mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})-\gamma\H(\mu)-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}\Ex_{\mu}[\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})].

In either case, the objective value is equal to
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)
, by (
6
).
Because
(\mu,\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
is optimal for this problem, we know that
\mu
is a minimizer of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)
, and that the objective value equals
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
.
∎

Lemma 15
.

The gradient and Hessian of the conditional relative entropy
are given by

\displaystyle\Big[\nabla_{\mu}I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(X,Y)\;\|\;\mu(X)p(Y|X))

\displaystyle\Big]_{u}=\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}

\displaystyle\Big[\nabla^{2}_{\mu}I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(X,Y)\;\|\;\mu(X)p(Y|X))

\displaystyle\Big]_{u,v}=\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!u{=}X\!v\land Y\!u{=}Y\!v]}{\mu(Y\!u,X\!u)}-\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!v=X\!u]}{\mu(X\!u)},

where
X\!u=X(u)
it the value of the variable
X
in the joint setting
u\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}
of all variables.

Proof.

Represent
\mu
as a vector
[\mu_{w}]_{w\in\mathcal{V}\mathcal{X}}
.
We will make repeated use of the following facts:

\displaystyle\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\mu(X{=}x)\right]=\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\mu_{w}\right]\!\mathbbm{1}[X\!w{=}x]\penalty\ =\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}[X\!u{=}x];\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\mu(y|x)\right]

\displaystyle=\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\frac{\mu(x,y)}{\mu(x)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mu(x,y)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\frac{1}{\mu(x)}\right]+\frac{1}{\mu(x)}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\mu(x,y)\right]

\displaystyle=-\mu(x,y)\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!u=x]}{\mu(x)^{2}}+\frac{1}{\mu(x)}\mathbbm{1}[X\!u{=}x\land Y\!u{=}y]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!u=x]}{\mu(x)}\Big(\mathbbm{1}[Y\!u=y]-\mu(y|x)\Big).

We now apply this to the (conditional) relative entropy:

\displaystyle\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(X,Y)\;\|\;\mu(X)p(Y|X))\right]

\displaystyle=\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\sum_{w}\mu_{w}\log\frac{\mu(Y\!w|X\!w)}{p(Y\!w|X\!w)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w}\mathbbm{1}[u{=}w]\log\frac{\mu(Y\!w|X\!w)}{p(Y\!w|X\!w)}+\sum_{w}\mu_{w}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\log\frac{\mu(Y\!w|X\!w)}{p(Y\!w|X\!w)}\right]

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}+\sum_{w}\mu_{w}\frac{p(Y\!w|X\!w)}{\mu(Y\!w|X\!w)}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\frac{\mu(Y\!w|X\!w)}{p(Y\!w|X\!w)}\right]

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}+\sum_{w}\mu_{w}\frac{1}{\mu(Y\!w|X\!w)}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{u}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\mu(Y\!w|X\!w)\right]

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}+\sum_{w}\mu_{w}\frac{1}{\mu(Y\!w|X\!w)}\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!u=X\!w]}{\mu(X\!w)}\Big(\mathbbm{1}[Y\!u=Y\!w]-\mu(Y\!w|X\!w)\Big)

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}+\sum_{w}\mu_{w}\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!u{=}X\!w\land Y\!u{=}Y\!w]}{\mu(X\!w,Y\!w)}-\sum_{w}\mu_{w}\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!u=X\!w]}{\mu(X\!w)}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}+\frac{1}{\mu(X\!u,Y\!u)}\sum_{w}\mu_{w}\mathbbm{1}[X\!u{=}X\!w\land Y\!u{=}Y\!w]-\frac{1}{\mu(X\!u)}\sum_{w}\mu_{w}\mathbbm{1}[X\!u=X\!w]

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}+\frac{\mu(X\!u,Y\!u)}{\mu(X\!u,Y\!u)}-\frac{\mu(X\!u)}{\mu(X\!u)}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}+1-1

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}.

This allows us to compute the Hessian of the conditional relative entropy, whose components are

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mu_{u}\partial\mu_{v}}\Big[I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(XY)\;\|\;\mu(X)p(Y|X))\Big]

\displaystyle=\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{v}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\log\frac{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}\frac{1}{p(Y\!u|X\!u)}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{v}}\!\!\left[\vphantom{\Big|}\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)}\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!v{=}X\!u]}{\mu(X\!u)}\Big(\mathbbm{1}[Y\!v{=}Y\!u]-\mu(Y\!u|X\!u)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!u{=}X\!v\land Y\!u{=}Y\!v]}{\mu(Y\!u,X\!u)}-\frac{\mathbbm{1}[X\!v=X\!u]}{\mu(X\!u)}.

∎

Lemma 16
.

Let
p(Y|X)
be a cpd,
and suppose that
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(X,Y)
are joint distributions that have different conditional marginals on
Y
given
X
; that is, that
there exist
(x,y)\in\mathcal{V}(X,Y)
such that

\mu_{0}(x,y)\mu_{1}(x)\neq\mu_{1}(x,y)\mu_{0}(x).

Then the conditional relative entropy

I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu(X,Y)\;\Big\|\;\mu(X)p(Y|X)\Big)

is strictly convex in
\mu
along the line segment from
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
.
More precisely, for
t\in[0,1]
, if we define

\mu_{t}:=(1-t)\mu_{0}+t\,\mu_{1}
, then
the function

t\penalty\ \mapsto\penalty\ I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{t}(X,Y)\;\Big\|\;\mu_{t}(X)p(Y|X)\Big)\qquad\text{is strictly convex. }

Proof.

The function of interest can fail to be strictly convex only if the direction
\delta
along
\mu_{1}-\mu_{0}
is in the null-space of the Hessian matrix
\mathbf{H}(\mu)
of the (conditional) relative entropy.
By
Lemma

15
,

\mathbf{H}_{(xy),(x^{\prime}y^{\prime})}=\frac{\mathbbm{1}[x{=}x^{\prime}\land y{=}y^{\prime}]}{\mu(x,y)}-\frac{\mathbbm{1}[x{=}x^{\prime}]}{\mu(x)}.

Consider a function
\delta:\mathcal{V}(X,Y)\to\mathbb{R}
that is not identically zero, which can be viewed as a vector
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=[\delta(x,y)]_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{V}(X,Y)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{V}(X,Y)}
.
We can also view
\delta
as a (signed) measure on
\mathcal{V}(X,Y)
, that has marginals in the usual sense. In particular, we use the analogous notation

\delta(x):=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{V}Y}\delta(x,y).

We then compute

\displaystyle\big(\,\mathbf{H}(\mu)\,{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\,\big)_{x,y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\delta(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\left(\frac{\mathbbm{1}[x{=}x^{\prime}\land y{=}y^{\prime}]}{\mu(x,y)}-\frac{\mathbbm{1}[x{=}x^{\prime}]}{\mu(x)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta(x,y)}{\mu(x,y)}-\frac{\delta(x)}{\mu(x)}.

and also

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\sf T}\mathbf{H}(\mu)\,{\boldsymbol{\delta}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y}\delta(x,y)(\,\mathbf{H}(\mu)\,{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\,)_{x,y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y}\delta(x,y)\left(\frac{\delta(x,y)}{\mu(x,y)}-\frac{\delta(x)}{\mu(x)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y}\frac{\delta(x,y)^{2}}{\mu(x,y)}-\sum_{x}\frac{\delta(x)}{\mu(x)}\sum_{y}\delta(x,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y}\frac{\delta(x,y)^{2}}{\mu(x,y)}-\sum_{x}\frac{\delta(x)^{2}}{\mu(x)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\frac{\delta(x)^{2}}{\mu(x)}\left(\sum_{y}\frac{\delta(x,y)^{2}}{\delta(x)^{2}\mu(y|x)}-1\right).

(16)

Now consider another discrete measure
|\delta|
, whose value at each component is the absolute value of the value of
\delta
at that component, i.e.,
|\delta|(x,y):=|\delta(x,y)|
.
By construction,
|\delta|
is now an unnormalized probability measure:
|\delta|=kq(X,Y)
, where
k=\sum_{x,y}|\delta(x,y)|>0
and
q\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(X,Y)
.

Note also that
|\delta|(x)^{2}=(\sum_{y}|\delta(x,y)|)^{2}\geq(\sum_{y}\delta(x,y))^{2}
, and strictly so if there are
y,y^{\prime}
such that
\delta(x,y)<0<\delta(x,y^{\prime})
.
In other words, the vector
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}=[\delta(x,y)]_{y\in\mathcal{V}Y}
is either non-negative or non-positive:
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}\geq 0
or
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}\leq 0
for each
x
.
Meanwhile,
|\delta|(x,y)^{2}=\delta(x,y)^{2}
is unchanged.
Thus, for every
x\in\mathcal{V}X
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{y}\frac{\delta(x,y)^{2}}{\delta(x)^{2}\mu(y|x)}-1

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{y}\frac{|\delta|(x,y)^{2}}{|\delta|(x)^{2}\mu(y|x)}-1

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\frac{k^{2}q(x,y)^{2}}{k^{2}q(x)^{2}\mu(y|x)}-1

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\frac{q(y|x)^{2}}{\mu(y|x)}-1

\displaystyle=\chi^{2}\Big(q(Y|x)\Big\|\mu(Y|x)\Big)\geq 0.

The final line depicts the
\chi^{2}
divergence between the distributions
q(Y|x)
and
\mu(Y|x)
, both distributions over
Y
. Since it is a divergence, this quantity is non-negative and equals zero if and only if
q(Y|x)=\mu(Y|x)
.

Picking up where we left off, we have:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\sf T}\mathbf{H}(\mu){\boldsymbol{\delta}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\frac{\delta(x)^{2}}{\mu(x)}\left(\sum_{y}\frac{\delta(x,y)^{2}}{\delta(x)^{2}\mu(y|x)}-1\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{x}\frac{\delta(x)^{2}}{\mu(x)}\left(\sum_{y}\frac{|\delta|(x,y)^{2}}{|\delta|(x)^{2}\mu(y|x)}-1\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\frac{\delta(x)^{2}}{\mu(x)}\chi^{2}\Big(q(Y|x)\Big\|\mu(Y|x)\Big)\geq 0.

As a non-negatively weighted sum of non-negative numbers, this final quantity is non-negative, and equals zero if and only if, for each
x\in\mathcal{V}X
, we have either
q(Y|x)=\mu(Y|x)
, or
\delta(x)=0
.
Furthermore, if
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\sf T}\mathbf{H}(\mu){\boldsymbol{\delta}}=0
, then
both
inequalities hold with equality. Therefore, we know that if
\delta(x)\neq 0
, then
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}\geq\mathbf{0}
or
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}\leq\mathbf{0}
.
These two conditions are also sufficient to show that
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\sf T}\mathbf{H}(\mu){\boldsymbol{\delta}}=0
.
To summarize what we know so far:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\sf T}\mathbf{H}(\mu){\boldsymbol{\delta}}=0\quad\iff\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{V}\!X.\quad\text{either}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ ({\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}\geq\mathbf{0}\text{ or }{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{y}\leq\mathbf{0})\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ and\penalty\ \penalty\ $|\delta|(Y|x)=\mu(Y|x)$}

or

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \delta(x)=0.

The second possibility, however, is a mirage: it cannot occur.
Let’s now return to the expression we had in (
16
) before considering
|\delta|
.
We’ve already shown that the contribution to the sum at each value of
x
is non-negative, so if
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\sf T}\mathbf{H}(\mu){\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is equal to zero, each summand which depends on
x
must be zero as well.
So if
x
is a value of
X
for which
\delta(x)=0
, then

\displaystyle 0=\frac{1}{\mu(x)}\left(\sum_{y}\frac{\delta(x,y)^{2}}{\mu(y|x)}-\delta(x)^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{\mu(x)}\sum_{y}\frac{\delta(x,y)^{2}}{\mu(y|x)}=\sum_{y}\frac{\delta(x,y)^{2}}{\mu(x,y)},

which is only possible if
\delta(x,y)=0
for all
y
.
This allows us to compute, more simply, that

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\sf T}\mathbf{H}(\mu){\boldsymbol{\delta}}=0\quad

\displaystyle\iff\quad(\forall x.\penalty\ {\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}\geq\mathbf{0}\text{ or }{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}\leq\mathbf{0})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\forall(x,y)\in\mathcal{V}(X,Y).\penalty\ \penalty\ \delta(x,y)\mu(x)=\delta(x)\mu(x,y)

Finally, we are in a position to prove the lemma.
Suppose that
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(X,Y)
and
(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathcal{V}(X,Y)

are such that
\mu_{0}(x^{*},y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})\neq\mu_{1}(x^{*},y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})
.
So, the quantity

\mathit{gap}:=\mu_{1}(x^{*},y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})-\mu_{0}(x^{*},y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})\quad\text{is nonzero}.

Then for all
t\in(0,1)
the intermediate point
\mu_{t}=(1-t)\,\mu_{0}+t\,\mu_{1}
must have different conditional marginals from both
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
, as

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})-\mu_{0}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{t}(x^{*})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1-t)\mu_{0}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})}}+t\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1-t)\mu_{0}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})}}-t\mu_{0}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})

\displaystyle=t\big\lparen\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})-\mu_{0}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})\big\rparen

\displaystyle=\penalty\ t\cdot\mathit{gap}\penalty\ \neq\penalty\ 0,

and analogously for
\mu_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})-\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{t}(x^{*})

\displaystyle=(1-t)\mu_{0}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})}}-(1-t)\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})}}

\displaystyle=(1-t)(\mu_{0}(x^{*},y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})-\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*}))

\displaystyle=\penalty\ -(1-t)\cdot\mathit{gap}\penalty\ \neq\penalty\ 0.

Then for any direction
\delta:=k(\mu_{0}-\mu_{1})
parallel to the segment between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
(intuitively a tangent vector at
\mu_{t}
, although this fact doesn’t affect the computation), of nonzero length (
k\neq 0
), we have:

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\delta(x^{*})-\delta(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{t}(x^{*})

\displaystyle=k\;\mu_{t}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\big\lparen\mu_{0}(x^{*})-\mu_{1}(x^{*})\big\rparen-k\;\big\lparen\mu_{0}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})-\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\big\rparen\mu_{t}(x^{*})

\displaystyle=k\Big\lparen\mu_{t}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})-\mu_{t}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})-\mu_{0}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{t}(x^{*})+\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{t}(x^{*})\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=k\Big\lparen\big\lparen\mu_{t}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{0}(x^{*})-\mu_{0}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{t}(x^{*})\big\rparen+\big\lparen\mu_{1}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{t}(x^{*})-\mu_{t}(x^{*}\!,y^{*})\mu_{1}(x^{*})\big\rparen\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=k\big\lparen+t\,\mathit{gap}+(1-t)\,\mathit{gap}\big\rparen

\displaystyle=k\,\mathit{gap}\qquad\neq\penalty\ 0.

So at every
t
, directions parallel to the segment are not in the null space of
\mathbf{H}(\mu_{t})
, meaning that

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\sf T}\mathbf{H}(\mu_{t}){\boldsymbol{\delta}}>0
and so our function is strictly convex along this segment.
∎

.

See
5

Proof.

Suppose that
(-\mathbf{u},\mu,\mathbf{k})
is a solution to problem (
10
).
The second constraint, by
3
, ensures that
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}
.
Then

\displaystyle(-\mathbf{u},\mu,\mathbf{k})\in K^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}\quad\implies\quad\forall w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}.\penalty\ u_{w}

\displaystyle\geq\mu(w)\log\frac{\mu(w)}{k_{w}}

\displaystyle=\mu(w)\log\bigg\lparen{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mu(w)\penalty\ }$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\penalty\ \prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{a}}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mu(w)\penalty\ }$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\penalty\ \prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{a}}}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mu(w)\penalty\ }$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\penalty\ \prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{a}}}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mu(w)\penalty\ }$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\penalty\ \prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mu(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(w))^{\alpha_{a}}}$}}}\bigg\rparen.

The same logic as in the

proofs

of

and

shows that this inequality must be tight, or else

(-\mathbf{u},\mu,\mathbf{k})
would not be optimal for (
10
).
So,
\mathbf{u}
is a function of
\mu
. Also, by
4
, the problem objective satisfies

\mathbf{1}^{\sf T}\mathbf{u}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{V}\!X}u_{w}=\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\mu).

Finally, because
\mu
is optimal, it must be the unique distribution

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}
, which among those distributions that minimize
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}

\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
, meaning

.

Proof.

The final constraints alone are enough to ensure that
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is calibrated.
Much like before, the exponential conic constraints tell us that

\forall(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}.\quad u_{a,s,t}\geq\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\log\frac{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)}{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s)\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}

and they hold with equality (at least at those indices where
\beta_{a}>0
) because
\mathbf{u}
is optimal.
So

\displaystyle\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}u_{a,s,t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\log\frac{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)}{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s)\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}\beta_{a}\sum_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{V}a}\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\log\frac{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)}{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s)\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}

\displaystyle=\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}).

Because
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is optimal, it is the choice of calibrated tree marginal that minimizes this quantity.
By
6.1
, the distribution
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}
can be represented by such a tree marginal, and by
Richardson and Halpern 2021
,
this distribution minimizes
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
.
All this is to say that there exist tree marginals of this form whose corresonding distributions attain the minimum value
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0}
.
So
\boldsymbol{\mu}
must be one of them, as it minimizes
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}(\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
among such tree marginals by assumption. Thus
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}
and the objective value of (
11
) equals
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0}
.
∎

Proof.

Suppose that
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
is a solution to (
14
).
The first and fourth lines of constraints ensures that
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is indeed a calibrated tree marginal. The second line of constraints, plays exactly the same role that it did in the previous problems, most directly in the variant (
11
) for
\gamma=0
. In particular, it tells says

\forall(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}.\quad u_{a,s,t}\geq\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\log\frac{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)}{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s)\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}

as before, this holds with equality (at least at those indices where
\beta_{a}>\alpha_{a}\gamma
) because
\mathbf{u}
is optimal.
Because
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq\gamma\alpha
by assumption, either
\beta_{a}>\gamma\alpha_{a}
or the two are equal, for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Either way,
the argument used at this point in
the proof of

7
goes through, giving us:

\displaystyle\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}(\beta_{a}-\alpha_{a}\gamma)u_{a,s,t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}((\beta_{a}-\alpha_{a}\gamma)\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\log\frac{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)}{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s)\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}(\beta_{a}-\alpha_{a}\gamma)\sum_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{V}a}\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\log\frac{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)}{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s)\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}(\beta_{a}-\alpha_{a}\gamma)\penalty\ I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\;\Big\|\;\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\,\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\Big)

This time, though, that’s not the problem objective. In this regard, our problem (
14
) is more closely related to (
14
).

Before we get to that, we have to first bring in the final collection of exponential constraints, which show that

\displaystyle\forall C\in\mathdcal C.\penalty\ \forall c\in\mathcal{V}(C).\quad v_{C,c}\geq\mu_{C}(c)\log\frac{\mu_{C}(c)}{\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c)},

and yet again these constraints hold with equality,
for otherwise
\mathbf{v}
would not be optimal (since we assumed
\gamma>0
). Therefore,

\sum_{\mathclap{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}}v_{C,c}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \sum_{\mathclap{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}}\mu_{C}(c)\log\frac{\mu_{C}(c)}{\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ -\H(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\quad\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:cluster-ent-decomp}}.

The objective of our problem (
14
) is essentially the same as that of (
7
), so the analysis in
the proof of

2
applies with only a handful of superficial modifications.
Using that proof to take a shortcut, the objective of (
14
) must equal

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathclap{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}\!-\alpha_{a}\gamma)u_{a,s,t}\penalty\ +\gamma\sum_{\mathclap{(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}}v_{C,c}\penalty\ -\sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathclap{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}}(\beta_{a}-\alpha_{a}\gamma)\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\log\frac{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)}{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s)\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}\penalty\ -\penalty\ \gamma\H(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\penalty\ -\penalty\ \sum_{\mathrlap{\!\!\!(a,s,t)\in\smash{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{+}}}}\alpha_{a}\gamma\,\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}\Ex_{\mu_{C_{\!a}}}[\log\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})]+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(\alpha_{a}\gamma\!-\!\beta_{a})\H_{\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})-\gamma\H(\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}),\quad.

Finally, since
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is such that this quantity is minimized, and because
its unique minimizer can be represented as a cluster tree (by
6.1
), we conclude that
\boldsymbol{\mu}
must be the cluster tree representation of it.
Therefore,
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is the unique element of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
, and the objective at
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
equals
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
, as desired.

See

Proof.

Suppose that
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{u})
is a solution to (
15
).
The exponential cone constraints state that

\displaystyle\forall C\in\mathdcal C.\penalty\ \forall c\in\mathcal{V}(C).\quad u_{C,c}

\displaystyle\geq\mu_{C}(c)\log\frac{\mu_{C}(c)}{k_{C,c}\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{C}(c)\log\frac{\mu_{C}(c)}{\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c)}-\mu_{C}(c)\log\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{C}}\nu_{C}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))^{\alpha_{a}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{C}(c)\log\frac{\mu_{C}(c)}{\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c)}-\mu_{C}(c)\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{C}}\alpha_{a}\log\nu_{C}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)),

and once again this holds with equality, as each
u_{C,c}
is minimal with this property.
The third line of constraints

\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.\penalty\ \penalty\ \mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\nu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})=\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})\nu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})

and the assumption that
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}
, suffice to ensure that
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{0}
by
3
.
They also allow us to replace each
\nu_{C_{a}}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))
with

\nu_{C_{a}}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))
, in cases where
S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)\neq 0
.
Therefore, we calculate the objective to be:

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}^{\sf T}\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\mathdcal C}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{V}(C)}\left(\mu_{C}(c)\log\frac{\mu_{C}(c)}{\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c)}-\mu_{C}(c)\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{C}}\alpha_{a}\log\nu_{C}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\mathdcal C}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{V}(C)}\mu_{C}(c)\log\frac{\mu_{C}(c)}{\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c)}-\sum_{C\in\mathdcal C}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{V}(C)}\mu_{C}(c)\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbbm{1}[C=C_{a}]\alpha_{a}\log\nu_{C}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))

\displaystyle=-\H(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{a}\sum_{C\in\mathdcal C}\mathbbm{1}[C=C_{a}]\sum_{c\in\mathcal{V}(C)}\mu_{C}(c)\log\nu_{C}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))\qquad\qquad\big[\,\text{by \eqref{eq:cluster-ent-decomp}}\,\big]

\displaystyle=-\H(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{a}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{V}(C)_{a}}\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(c)\log\nu_{C_{\!a}}\!(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))

\displaystyle=-\H(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{a}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{V}(C)_{a}}\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(c)\log{\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c)|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))}\qquad\Big[\penalty\ \text{since $\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c))>0$ whenever
$\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(c)>0$}\penalty\ \Big]

\displaystyle=-\H(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\alpha_{a}\H_{\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}|S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}})

\displaystyle=\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}).

To summarize:
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
minimizes
\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(\Pr\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
among calibrated tree marginals with condtional marginals matching those of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
.
Since we know that there is a unique distribution that minimizes
\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}
among the elements
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}^{*}
, and also that this distribution can be represented by a tree marginal (by
6.1
), we conclude that
\boldsymbol{\mu}
must represent this distribution. Thus,
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}
as desired.
∎

The next lemma packages the results of
Dahl and Andersen 2022
,
Nesterov et al. 1999
in a precise form that we will be able to make use of.

Lemma 17
.

Fix integers
n_{\sf o},n_{\sf e}\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
n:=3n_{\sf e}+n_{\sf o}
.
Suppose that

K=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n_{\sf o}}\times K^{n_{\sf e}}_{\exp}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a product cone, consisting of
n_{\sf o}
copies of the non-negative orthant and
n_{\sf e}
copies of the exponential cone.
Let

\mathbf{c}\in[-1,1]^{n}
and
\mathbf{b}\in[-1,1]^{m}

be vectors, and
A\in[-1,1]^{m\times n}

be a matrix, defining
an exponential conic program

\displaystyle\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{minimize}$}}\limits_{\mathbf{x}\in K}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{c}^{\sf T}\mathbf{x}\quad{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}$}}\quad A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b},.

(
3
)

If this program
is strictly feasible (i.e., if there exists
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{int}\,K
such that
A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
),
as is its dual problem

\mathop{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{maximize}$}}\limits\limits_{\mathbf{s}\in K^{*},\,\mathbf{y}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{m}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{b}^{\sf T}\mathbf{y}\quad{\scalebox{0.98}{$\mathsf{subject\penalty\ to}$}}\quad A^{\sf T}\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{c},

(i.e, if there exists
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{int}\,K_{*}
such that
A^{\sf T}\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{c}
),
then both
can be simultaneously
solved to precision
\epsilon

in
O(n(m+n)^{\omega}\log\frac{n+m}{\epsilon})
time,
where
\omega
is the smallest exponent such that a linear system of
k
variables and equations can be solved in
O(k^{\omega})
time.
Furthermore, MOSEK solves this problem in
O(n(m+n)^{3}\log\frac{n+m}{\epsilon})
time.

Proof.

For this, we begin by appealing to the algorithm and analysis of

Badenbroek and Dahl 2021
, threading details through for this specific choice of cone
K
.
To finish the proof, however, we will also need to supplement that analysis with some other well-established results of
Nesterov et al. 1999
that the authors were no doubt familiar with, but did not bother referencing.

First, we’ll need some background material from convex optimization.
A
logarithmically homogeneous self-concordant barrier
with parameter
\nu
(
\nu
-LHSCB) for a cone
K
is a thrice differentiable strictly convex function
F:\mathrm{int}\,K\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

F(tx)=F(x)-\nu\log t

for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathrm{int}\,K
.
In some sense, the point of such a barrier function is to augment the optimization objective so that we remain within the cone during the optimization process.

For the positive orthant cone
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the function

x\mapsto-\log x
is a 1-LHSCB.
We now fill in some background facts about exponential cones.
The
dual
of the exponential cone is

\displaystyle K_{\exp}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\;:\;\forall(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in K_{\exp}.\penalty\ \penalty\ x_{1}s_{1}+x_{2}s_{2}+x_{3}s_{3}\geq 0\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\;:\;-s_{1}\log(-s_{1}/s_{3})+s_{1}-s_{2}\leq 0,\,s_{1}\leq 0,\,s_{3}\geq 0\big\}.

Consider points
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in K_{\exp}
.
The function

F_{\exp}(x):=-\log\Big(x_{2}\log\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}}-x_{3}\Big)-\log x_{1}x_{2}

(17)

is a
3
-LHSCB for
K_{\exp}
, since

\displaystyle F_{\exp}(tx)

\displaystyle=-\log\Big(tx_{2}\log\frac{tx_{1}}{tx_{2}}-tx_{3}\Big)-\log(t^{2}x_{1}x_{2})

\displaystyle=-\log\Big(t\big(\log\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}}-x_{3}\big)\Big)-\log(x_{1}x_{2})-2\log t

\displaystyle=F_{\exp}(x)-3\log t

Such barrier functions can be combined to act on product cones by summation.
Concretely, suppose that for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
we have a
\nu_{i}
-LHSCB
F_{i}:\mathrm{int}\,K_{i}\to\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,
.
For
x=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\in\prod_{i}K_{i}
, the function

F(x):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}F_{i}(x_{i})
is a
(\sum_{i}\nu_{i})
-LHSCB for
\prod_{i}K_{i}
,
since

F(tx)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}F_{i}(tx_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(F(x_{i})-\nu_{i}\log t)=F(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\nu_{i}.

In this way, our product cone
K=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n_{\sf o}}\times K^{n_{\sf e}}_{\exp}
admits a LHSCB
F
with parameter
\nu=n_{\sf o}+3n_{\sf e}=n
.
Furthermore it can be evaluated in
O(n)
time, as can each component of
its gradient
F^{\prime}(x)
and Hessian
F^{\prime\prime}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
at
x
, all of which can be expressed analytically.
In addition, the convex conjugate of
F

also has a known analytic form.

Generally speaking,
the idea behind primal-dual interior point methods
[
Nesterov and Nemirovskii 1994
]
such as the one behind MOSEK, is
to maintain both a point
x\in K
and a dual point
s\in K_{*}
(as well as
y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
)
and iteratively update them, as we slowly relax the barrier and approach a point on the boundary of the cone.
The quantity
\mu(z):=\nicefrac{{\langle s,x\rangle}}{{\nu}}\geq 0
, called the complementarity gap, is a measure of how close the process is to converging.

Because the initial points may not satisfy the constraints, instead
the standard algorithms work with “extended points”
\bar{x}=(x,\tau)
and
\bar{s}=(s,\kappa)
, for which the analogous complementarity gap is

\mu^{\sf e}(\bar{x},\bar{s}):=(\langle x,s\rangle+\kappa\tau)/(\nu+1)
.
Altogether, the data at each iteration may be summarized as a point
z=(y,x,\tau,s,\kappa)\in\mathbb{R}\times(K\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\times(K_{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.
The primary object of interest is then something called the
homogenous self-dual
model.
Originally due to
Nesterov et al. 1999
and also used by others
[
Skajaa and Ye 2015
]
,
it can be defined as a linear operator:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle:\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{m+2n+2}\to\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{n+m+1}

\displaystyle G(y,x,\tau,s,\kappa)

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}0&A&-b\\
-A^{\sf T}&0&c\\
b^{\sf T}&-c^{\sf T}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}y\\
x\\
\tau\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}0\\
s\\
k\end{bmatrix}.

The reason for our interest is that if
z
is such that
G(z)=0
and
\tau>0
, then
(\nicefrac{{x}}{{\tau}})
is a solution to the primal problem, and
\nicefrac{{(y,s)}}{{\tau}}
is a solution to the dual problem
[
Skajaa and Ye 2015
, Lemma 1]
, while if
G(z)=0
and
\kappa>0
, then at least one of the two problems is infeasible.

We now are in a better position to describe the algorithm.
According to the MOSEK documentation
[
Dahl and Andersen 2022
]
,
for the exponential cone,
begins with an initial point

\mathbf{v}:=(1.291,0.805,-0.828)\in(K_{\exp}\,\cap\,K_{\exp}^{*})

for this particular cone
K
,
the algorithm begins at the initial point

\displaystyle z_{0}:=(y_{0},x_{0},\tau_{0},s_{0},\kappa_{0})\qquad\text{where}\quad x_{0}

\displaystyle=s_{0}=(\overbrace{\vphantom{|}1,\ldots,1}^{n_{\sf o}\text{ copies}},\penalty\ \overbrace{\vphantom{|}\mathbf{v},\ldots,\mathbf{v}}^{n_{\sf e}\text{ copies}})\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n_{\sf o}}\times(K_{\exp}\,\cap\,K_{\exp}^{*})^{n_{\sf e}},

\displaystyle\qquad y_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\quad\tau_{0}=\kappa_{0}=1.

At each iteration, the first step is to predict a direction for which

Badenbroek and Dahl 2021

compute a scaling matrix
W
.
To describe it, we first need to define
shadow iterates

\tilde{x}:=-F^{\prime}_{*}(s)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{s}:=-F^{\prime}(x).

which are in a sense reflections of
s
and
x
across their barrier functions, and can be computed in in
O(n)
time.
The analogous notion of complementarity can then be defined as
\tilde{\mu}(z):=\nicefrac{{\langle\tilde{x},\tilde{s}\rangle}}{{\nu}}
.
The scaling matrix, which we do not interpret here, can then be calculated as:

W:=\mu F^{\prime\prime}(x)+\frac{ss^{\sf T}}{\nu\mu}-\frac{\mu\tilde{s}\tilde{s}^{\sf T}}{\nu}+\frac{(s-\mu\tilde{s})(s-\mu\tilde{s})^{\sf T}}{(s-\mu\tilde{s})^{\sf T}(x-\mu\tilde{x})}-\frac{\mu[F^{\prime\prime}(x)\tilde{x}-\tilde{\mu}\tilde{s}][F^{\prime\prime}(x)\tilde{x}-\tilde{\mu}\tilde{s}]^{\sf T}}{\tilde{x}^{\sf T}F^{\prime\prime}(x)\tilde{x}-\nu\tilde{\mu}^{2}}

(18)

Doing so requires
O(n^{2})
steps (although it may be parallelized).
The first four terms clearly require
O(n^{2})
steps, since each one is an outer product resulting in a
n\times n
matrix.
The last term computes a matrix-vector product (which requires
O(n^{2})
steps), and computes an outer product with the resulting vector, which takes
O(n^{2})
steps as well.

The next step involves finding a solution
\Delta{z}^{\text{aff}}=(\cdots)

to the system of equations

\displaystyle G(\Delta{z}^{\text{aff}})

\displaystyle=-G(z)

(19a)

\displaystyle\tau\Delta{\kappa}^{\text{aff}}+\Delta{\tau}^{\text{aff}}

\displaystyle=-\tau\kappa

(19b)

\displaystyle W\Delta{x}^{\text{aff}}+\Delta{s}^{\text{aff}}

\displaystyle=-s.

(19c)

(
19
a-c) describe a system of
(n+m+1)+1+(n)=2n+m+2
equations and equally many unknowns,
and solved in
O((n+m)^{\omega})
steps.
It may be possible to exploit the sparsity of
G
to do better.

The next step is to center that search direction so that it lies on the central path. This is done by finding a solution
\Delta{z}^{\text{cen}}
to

\displaystyle G(\Delta{z}^{\text{cen}})

\displaystyle=G(z)

(20a)

\displaystyle\tau\Delta{\kappa}^{\text{cen}}+\kappa\Delta{\tau}^{\text{cen}}

\displaystyle=\mu^{e}

(20b)

\displaystyle W\Delta{x}^{\text{cen}}+\Delta{s}^{\text{cen}}

\displaystyle=\mu^{e}\tilde{s},

(20c)

which again can be done in
O((n+m)^{3})
steps with Gaussian elimination, or
with a fancier solver in
O((n+m)^{2}.332)
steps.
The two updates are then applied to the current point
z
to obtain

z_{+}=(y_{+},x_{+},\tau_{+},s_{+},\kappa_{+}):=z+\alpha(\Delta{z}^{\text{aff}}+\gamma\Delta{z}^{\text{cen}}).

Finally, a “correction step”, which is the primary innovation of
Badenbroek and Dahl 2021
and used in MOSEK’s algorithm,
is a third direction
\Delta z_{+}^{\text{cor}}
, which is found by solving the system of equations

\displaystyle G(\Delta z^{\text{cor}})

\displaystyle=0

(21a)

\displaystyle\tau_{+}\Delta\kappa^{\text{cor}}+\kappa_{+}\Delta\tau^{\text{cor}}

\displaystyle=0

(21b)

\displaystyle W_{+}\Delta{x_{+}}^{\text{cor}}+\Delta{s}^{\text{cen}}

\displaystyle=\mu^{e}\tilde{s},

(21c)

where

W_{+}
is defined the same way that
W
is, except that it uses the components of
z_{+}
instead of
z
.
After adding the correction step
\Delta z_{+}^{\text{cor}}
to
z
, we repeat the entire process. The full algorithm, then, is summarized as follows:

z\leftarrow(y_{0},x_{0},\tau_{0},s_{0},\kappa_{0})
;

while

do

Compute scaling matrix
W
as in (
18
);

Find the solution
\Delta{z}^{\text{aff}}
to (
19
a-c),
and the solution
\Delta{z}^{\text{cen}}
to (
20
a-c);

z_{+}\leftarrow z+\alpha(\Delta{z}^{\text{aff}}+\gamma\Delta{z}^{\text{cen}})
;

Compute the saling matrix
W_{+}
;

Find the solution
\Delta z^{\text{cor}}_{+}
to (
21
a-c);

z\leftarrow z_{+}+\Delta z_{+}^{\text{cor}}
;

end

while

We have verified that each iteration of this process can be done in
O((n+m)^{\omega}))
time.
Their main result
[
Badenbroek and Dahl 2021
, Theorem 3]
, states that for every
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
the algorithm results in a solution
z
satisfying

\mu^{\sf e}(z)\leq\epsilon\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|G(z)\|\leq\epsilon\|G(z_{0})\|

in
O(n\log(1/\epsilon))
iterations,
for a total cost of

O(n(m+n)^{3}\log(1/\epsilon))
time with Gaussian elimination, or

O(n(m+n)^{2.332}\log(1/\epsilon))
time using the linear solver with best
known asymptotatic complexity as of 2022
Duan et al. 2022
.

Verifying that the solution is approximately optimal.

What we have at this point is not quite enough: simply because the residual quantity
G(z)
is approximately zero (so that we have approximately solved the homogenous model), does not mean that we’ve approximately solved the original problem.
Specifically, it’s entirely possible a priori that the parameter
\tau
goes to zero at the same rate as everything else, and the quantity
(x/\tau)
does not converge to a solution to the primal problem.
To address this issue, we must also trace the analysis of the seminal work of
Nesterov et al. 1999
, who use slightly different quantities, conflicting with the notation we have been using thus far.

Following
Nesterov et al. 1999
, fix an initial point
z_{0}
, and let
shifted feasible set

\mathcal{F}:=\{z\in\mathbb{R}\times K\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times K^{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}:G(z)=G(z_{0})\}

be the collection of all points that have the same residual as
z_{0}
.

Nesterov et al. 1999
also refer to a complementary gap by
\mu(z)
and define it identically, but the meaning of this parameter is different, because the set
\mathcal{F}
on which it’s defined is quite distinct from (if closely related to) the iterates of
Badenbroek and Dahl 2021
’s algorithm.
In the service of clarity,
will call this quantity
\mu^{\sf N}(z^{\sf N})
, for

z^{\sf N}=(y^{\sf N},x^{\sf N},\tau^{\sf N},s^{\sf N},\kappa^{\sf N})\in\mathcal{F}
.

Although we made a point of emphasizing that the two are distinct,
the actual relationship between them is straightforward.
Let
z=(y,x,\tau,s,\kappa)
be the final output of
Badenbroek and Dahl 2021
.
In proving their main theorem, they also prove that

G(z)=\epsilon G(z_{0})
, and
\mu^{\sf e}=\epsilon
;
because
G
is linear, we know that
G(\nicefrac{{z}}{{\epsilon}})=G(z_{0})
.
This means that
z^{\sf N}:=\nicefrac{{z}}{{\epsilon}}\in\mathcal{F}
.
Therefore,

\mu^{\sf N}(z^{\sf N})=\frac{1}{\nu+1}\left(\left\langle\frac{s}{\epsilon},\frac{x}{\epsilon}\right\rangle+\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}\right)=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\mu^{\sf e}(z)=\frac{1}{\epsilon}.

So, roughly speaking,
\mu^{\sf N}
and
\mu^{\sf e}
are reciprocals.

Badenbroek and Dahl 2021
also
prove that, every iterate
z
satisfies their assumption (A2): for a fixed constant
\beta
(equal to 0.9 in their analysis),
\beta\mu^{\sf e}(z)\leq\tau\kappa
.
Consequently,
it happens that the same inequality holds with Nesterov’s notation:
