\tau^{\sf N}\kappa^{\sf N}=\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}=\frac{\tau\kappa}{\epsilon^{2}}\geq\frac{\beta\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2}}=\frac{\beta}{\epsilon}=\beta\mu^{\sf N}(z^{\sf N}).

This witnesses that
z^{\sf N}=\frac{z}{\epsilon}
satisfies equation (81) of
Nesterov et al. 1999
, which allows us to apply one of their main theorems, which addresses these issues.
Supposing that the orignal problem is solvable,
let
(x^{*},s^{*})
be any solution to the primal and dual problems,
and define the value
\psi:=1+\langle s_{0},x^{*}\rangle+\langle s^{*},x_{0}\rangle\geq 1
,
which depends only on the problem and the choice of initialization.
Then Theroem 1, part 1 of
Nesterov et al. 1999
, allows us to conclude that

\frac{\kappa}{\epsilon}\leq\psi\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}\geq\frac{\beta}{\epsilon\psi}\qquad\qquad\iff\qquad\qquad\kappa\leq\epsilon\psi\quad\text{and}\quad\tau\geq\frac{\beta}{\psi}.

Finally, the original theorem guarantees that

\|G(x)\|\leq\epsilon\|G(z_{0})\|
, meaning that

\Big\|A\Big(\frac{x}{\tau}\Big)-b\Big\|\tau\penalty\ +\penalty\ \Big\|A^{\sf T}\Big(\frac{y}{\tau}\Big)-\frac{s}{\tau}-c\Big\|\tau\penalty\ +\penalty\ \Big\|b^{\sf T}\left(\frac{y}{\tau}\right)-c^{\sf T}\left(\frac{x}{\tau}\right)-\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\Big\|\tau\leq\epsilon\|G(z_{0})\|.

Since the euclidean norm is an upper bound on the deviation in any component
(
\|v\|:=\sqrt{\sum_{i}v_{i}^{2}}\geq\sqrt{\max_{i}v_{i}^{2}}=\max_{i}v_{i}=:\|v\|_{\infty}
), this means that in light of our bound on
\tau
above, we have

\Big\|A\Big(\frac{x}{\tau}\Big)-b\Big\|_{\infty}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \Big\|A^{\sf T}\Big(\frac{y}{\tau}\Big)+\frac{s}{\tau}-c\Big\|_{\infty}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \Big\|b^{\sf T}\left(\frac{y}{\tau}\right)-c^{\sf T}\left(\frac{x}{\tau}\right)-\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\Big\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon\frac{\beta\|G(z_{0})\|}{\psi}.

The first two components show that the total constraint violation (in the primal and dual problems, respectively) is at most
\nicefrac{{\epsilon\beta}}{{\psi}}\|G(z_{0})\|
.
Meanwhile, the final component shows that the duality gap

\mathit{gap}=b^{\sf T}(\frac{y}{\tau})-c^{\sf T}(\frac{x}{\tau})
,
which is positive and an upper bound on the difference between the objective at
x/\tau
and the optimal objective value, satisfies

\mathit{gap}\leq\mathit{gap}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\leq\frac{\epsilon\beta\|G(z_{0})\|}{\psi}.

Thus
x/\tau
is an
(\epsilon\|G(z_{0})\|)
-approximate solution to the original exponential conic problem.
Since also
\psi\geq 1
, we may freely drop it to get a looser bound.
All that remains is to investigate
\|G(z_{0})\|
,
the residual norm of the initial point
chosen by the MOSEK solver, which equals:

\|G(z_{0})\|=\|Ax_{0}-b\|+\|A^{\sf T}y_{0}+s_{0}-c\|+|c^{\sf T}x-b^{\sf T}y+1|.

Making use of our assumption that every component of
A
,
b
, and
c
is at most one, we find that

\displaystyle\|Ax_{0}-b\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}(\sum_{i}A_{j,i}(1.3)-b_{j})^{2}\leq m(1.3n+1)^{2}\in O(mn^{2})

\displaystyle\subset O((m+n)^{3})

\displaystyle\|A^{\sf T}y_{0}+s_{0}-c\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(\sum_{j}(A_{j,i})\leq n(m+2)^{2}\in O(nm^{2})

\displaystyle\subset O((m+n)^{3})

\displaystyle|c^{\sf T}x-b^{\sf T}y+1|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1.3n+m+1)^{2}\in O((n+m)^{2})

\displaystyle\subset O((n+m)^{3}).

Therefore, the residual of the initial point is

G(z_{0})\in O((n+m)^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}})
.

To obtain a solution at most
\epsilon_{0}
away from the true
solution in any coordinate, we need to select
\epsilon
small enough
that the final output of the algorithm
z
satisfies

\epsilon\|G(z_{0})\|\leq\epsilon_{0}\qquad\iff\qquad\frac{1}{\epsilon}\geq\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}}\|G(z_{0})\|

It therefore suffices to choose

\frac{1}{\epsilon}\in O(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}}(n+m)^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}})
,
leading to
\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}=O(\log\frac{n+m}{\epsilon_{0}}))

iterations.

Thus, we arrive at our total advertised asymptotic complexity of time

O\Big(n(n+m)^{\omega}\log\frac{n+m}{\epsilon_{0}}\Big).

In particular, to attain machine precision, we can fix

\epsilon_{0}
to be the smallest gap between numbers representable
(say with 64-bit floats, leading to
\epsilon_{0}=10^{-78}
in the worst case), and omit the dependance on
\epsilon_{0}
for the price of relatively
small constant (78, for 64-bit floats).
∎

Having combed through all of the details of the analysis of
Badenbroek and Dahl 2021
and
Nesterov et al. 1999
for exponential
conic programs as we have defined them, we are ready to show that this algorithm solves the problems presented in
Section

4
within polynomial time.

In the results that follow, we use the symbol
O_{\text{BP}}(\,\cdot\,)
to describe the complexity under the
bounded precision
assumption: the numerical values of
(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbb{P})
that describe the PDG, as well as
\gamma
, lie within a fixed range, e.g., are 64-bit floating point numbers.
Correspondingly, we use
\tilde{O}_{\text{BP}}(\,\cdot\,)
to describe the complexity
under the same assumption, but hiding logarithmic factors for parameters on which the complexity also depends polynomially.

Lemma 18
.

Problem (
11
) can be solved to
\epsilon
precision in time

O\Big((\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C)^{1+\omega}(\log\frac{|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|+|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}{\epsilon}+\log\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}\Big)\quad\subset\quad\tilde{O}_{\text{BP}}\Big(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big),

where
\beta^{\max}:=\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}
is the largest value of
\beta
,
and
\beta^{\min}:=\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\{\beta_{a}:\beta_{a}>0\}
is the smallest positive one.

Proof.

Problem (
11
)
can translated via the DCP framework to
the following exponential conic program, which has:

\blacktriangleright

variables

x=(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\boldsymbol{\mu})\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}
,
where

•

\mathbf{u,v,w}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}

are all vectors over
\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
,
that at index
\iota=(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
, have
components
u_{\iota}
,
v_{\iota}
, and
w_{\iota}
, respectively;

•

\boldsymbol{\mu}=[\mu_{C}(C{=}c)]_{C\in\mathdcal C,\,c\in\mathcal{V}(C)}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}
is a vector representation of a tree marginal over clusters
\mathdcal C
;

\blacktriangleright

constraints as follows:

•

two linear constraints for every
(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
to ensure that

\displaystyle v_{a,s,t}

\displaystyle=\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(s,t)\qquad\Big(\penalty\ =\sum_{\bar{c}\in\mathcal{V}(C_{a}\setminus\{S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\})}\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(\bar{c},s,t)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{and}\qquad w_{a,s,t}

\displaystyle=\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s)\,\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t\mid S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s)\qquad\Big(\penalty\ =\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t\mid S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s)\sum_{\bar{c}\in\mathcal{V}(C_{a}\setminus\{S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\})}\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(\bar{c},s)\Big);

•

for every edge
(C\!{-}\!D)\in\cal T
, and every value
\omega\in\mathcal{V}(C\cap D)
of the variables that clusters
C
and
D
have in common, a linear constraint

\sum_{\bar{c}\in\mathcal{V}(C\setminus D)}\mu_{C}(\bar{c},\omega)=\sum_{\bar{d}\in\mathcal{V}(D\setminus C)}\mu_{D}(\bar{d},\omega);

•

and one constraint for each cluster
C\in\mathdcal C
to ensure that
\mu_{C}
lies on the probability simplex, i.e.,

\sum_{c\in\mathcal{V}(C)}\mu_{C}(c)=1.

Altogether this means that we have an exponential conic program in the form
of
Lemma

17
, with

n=3|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|+|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|
variables,
and

m=2|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|+|\mathcal{V}\mathcal{T}|+|\mathdcal C|
constraints,
where

\mathcal{V}\mathcal{T}=\{(C\!{-}\!D,\omega):C\!{-}\!D\in\mathcal{T},\omega\in\mathcal{V}(C\cap D)\}
.
Since we can simply disregard variables whose value sets are singletons, we can assume
\mathcal{V}(C)>1
; summing over all clusters yields
\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C>|\mathdcal C|
.
At the same time, since
\mathcal{V}\mathcal{T}\leq\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C
,
we have

m,n,(m+n)\in O(\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A},+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C).

We now give the explicit construction of the data
(A,b,c)
of the exponential conic program that (
11
) compiles to.
The variables are indexed by tuples
of the form
i=(\ell,a,s,t)
for
(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
and
\ell\in\{u,v,w\}
,
or by tuples of the form
(C,c)
, for
c\in\mathcal{V}(C)
and
C\in\mathdcal C
,
while the
constraints are indexed by tuples of the form

j=(\ell,a,s,t)
for
(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
and
\ell\in\{v,w\}
,
of the form
(C\!{-}\!D,\omega)
, for an edge
(C\!{-}\!D)\in\mathcal{T}
and
\omega\in\mathcal{V}(C\cap D)
,
or simply by
(C)
, the name of a cluster
C\in\mathdcal C
.
The problem data
A=[A_{j,i}],b=[b_{j}],c=[c_{i}]
of this program are zero, except (possibly) for the
components:

\displaystyle c_{(u,a,s,t)}

\displaystyle=\beta_{a}

\displaystyle A_{(v,a,s,t),(C,c)}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}[C{=}C_{a}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c){=}s\penalty\ \land\penalty\ T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c){=}t]

\displaystyle A_{(w,a,s,t),(C,c)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t\mid S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s)\mathbbm{1}[C{=}C_{a}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}(c){=}s]

\displaystyle A_{(w,a,s,t),(w,a,s,t)}

\displaystyle=-1

\displaystyle A_{(v,a,s,t),(v,a,s,t)}

\displaystyle=-1

\displaystyle A_{(C\!{-}\!D,\omega),(C^{\prime},c)}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}[C{=}C^{\prime}]-\mathbbm{1}[C^{\prime}{=}D]

\displaystyle A_{(C),(C,c)}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle b_{(C)}

\displaystyle=1,

where
\mathbbm{1}[\varphi]
is equal to 1 if
\varphi
is true, and zero if
\varphi
is false.
We note that we can equivalently divide each
\beta_{a}
by
\max_{a}\beta_{a}
without affecting the problem,
although this could affect the approximation accuracy by the same factor.
Thus, we get another factor of

\log(\max\{1\}\cup\{\beta_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A}\})\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ O(\log(1+\max_{a}\beta_{a})).

Finally, to find a point that is
\epsilon
-close (say, in 2-norm) to the limiting point
\mu^{*}
on the central path, as opposed to simply one that for which the suboptimality gap is at most
\epsilon
, we can appeal to strong concavity of the objective function.
(Conditional) relative entropy is 1-strongly convex, and each relative entropy term is scaled by
\beta_{a}
.
Furthermore, we’re only considering marginal conditional entropy, so this convexity may not hold in all directions.
Still, if the next step direction
\delta
is not far from the gradient (as is the case if the interior point method has nearly converged), then, in that direction, the objective will be at least (
\min_{a}\{\beta_{a}:\beta_{a}>0\}
)-strongly convex.
Therefore, by multiplying the requested precision by an additional factor of
\min_{a}\{\beta_{a}:\beta_{a}>0\}
, we can guarantee that our point is
\epsilon
-close to
\mu^{*}
, and not just in complementarity gap.

To summarize, applying
Lemma

17
, we find that we can solve problem (
11
) in time

\displaystyle O\Big(\big(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|+|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|\big)^{1+\omega}\Big(\log\frac{|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|+|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}{\epsilon}+\log\frac{\beta_{\max}}{\beta_{\min}}\Big)\Big)\quad\subset\tilde{O}_{BP}\Big(\big(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|+|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|\big)^{4}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big).

The factor of
\log\frac{\beta_{\max}}{\beta_{\min}}
can be treated as a constant under the bounded precision assumption.
∎

We now quickly step through the analogous construction for problems (
14
) and (
15
), which
solve the
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-inference problem, and
0^{+}
-inference, respectively.

Lemma 19
.

Problem (
14
) is solved to precision
\epsilon
in time

O\bigg(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{1+\omega}\Big(\log\frac{|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|+|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}{\epsilon}+\log(1+\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{\infty})+\log\log\frac{1}{p^{\min}}\Big)\bigg)\quad\subset\quad\tilde{O}_{\text{BP}}\Big(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)

where
p^{\min}
is the smallest nonzero probability in the PDG.

Proof.

Problem (
14
) has

\blacktriangleright

variables

x=(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{w},\,\,\mathbf{v},\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{z})\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\times K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}

where

•

\mathbf{u,y,w}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}

are all vectors over
\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}

that at index
\iota=(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
, have
components
u_{\iota}
,
v_{\iota}
, and
w_{\iota}
, respectively;

•

Meanwhile,

\mathbf{v,\boldsymbol{\mu},z}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}

are all vectors over
\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C

which at index
(C,c)
, have
components
v_{C,c}
,
\mu_{C}(c)
, and
z_{C,c}
, respectively.
Once again,
\boldsymbol{\mu}=[\mu_{C}(C{=}c)]_{C\in\mathdcal C,\,c\in\mathcal{V}(C)}\in\mskip 1.0mu\overline{\mskip-1.0mu\mathbb{R}\!}\,^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}
is intended to be a vector representation of a tree marginal.

\blacktriangleright

constraints as follows:

•

two linear constraints for each
(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
, to ensure that

y_{a,s,t}=\mu_{C_{\!a}}(s,t)\qquad\text{and}\qquad w_{a,s,t}=\mu_{C_{\!a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s)\,\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t\mid S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s),

•

for every edge
(C\!{-}\!D)\in\cal T
, and every value
\omega\in\mathcal{V}(C\cap D)
of the variables that clusters
C
and
D
have in common, a linear constraint

\sum_{\bar{c}\in\mathcal{V}(C\setminus D)}\mu_{C}(\bar{c},\omega)=\sum_{\bar{d}\in\mathcal{V}(D\setminus C)}\mu_{D}(\bar{d},\omega)

•

for every
(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{0}
, a linear constraint
that ensures

0=\mu_{C_{a}}\!(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t)\qquad\Big(\penalty\ \penalty\ =\sum_{\bar{c}\in\mathcal{V}(C\setminus\{S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}},T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}\})}\mu_{C_{a}}(\bar{c},s,t)\Big)

•

a linear constraint for every value
c\in\mathcal{V}(C)
of every cluster
C\in\mathdcal C
, to ensure that

z_{C,c}=\mu_{C}(\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c))\qquad\Big(\penalty\ \penalty\ =\sum_{\bar{c}\in\mathcal{V}(C\setminus\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C})}\mu_{C}(\bar{c},\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c))\penalty\ \penalty\ \Big)

•

and one constraint for each cluster
C\in\mathdcal C
to ensure that
\mu_{C}
lies on the probability simplex, i.e.,

\sum_{c\in\mathcal{V}(C)}\mu_{C}(c)=1.

So in total, there are

n=|3\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+3\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|
variables,
and

m=2|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|+|\mathcal{V}\mathcal{T}|+|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}^{0}|+|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|+|\mathdcal C|
constraints.
The same arguments made in
Lemma

18
show that both
n,m\in O(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|)
.

Also like before, it is easy to see that the components of
A
and
b
are all at most 1. However, we will need to rescale the objective
c
in order for each of its components to be most 1. We can do this by dividing it by

\max\{-\beta_{a}\log{p_{a}(t|s)}\}_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\cup\{1\}
.

Finally, to ensure that we have a solution that is
\epsilon
-close to the end of the central path, as opposed to one that is merely
\epsilon
-close in compelementarity gap, we must appeal to convexity.
As in the proof of
Lemma

18
, this amounts to reducing the target accuracy by a factor of the smallest possible coefficient of strong convexity, along the next step direction.
In this case, the bound is simpler: because negative entropy is (unconditionally) 1-strongly convex, and since
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq\boldsymbol{\alpha}\gamma
, the remaining terms are convex, this could be, at worst,
\frac{1}{\gamma}
.

This gives rise to our result: problem (
14
) can be solved in

\displaystyle O\left(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{1+\omega}\left(\log\frac{|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}{\epsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\gamma}\left(1+\max_{(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}}\beta_{a}\log\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}_{\!a\mskip-2.0mu}(t|s)}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\subset O\left(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{1+\omega}\left\{\log\frac{|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}{\epsilon}+\log\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\gamma}+\log\log\frac{1}{p^{\min}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\subset\tilde{O}_{\text{BP}}\left(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{1+\omega}\Big(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)\right)

operations, where
p
is the smallest nonzero probability in the PDG, and
\beta^{\max}
is the largest confidence in the PDG larger than 1.
∎

Lemma 20
.

Problem (
15
) is solved to precision
\epsilon
in

O\Big(|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C||\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{\omega}\log\frac{|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}{\epsilon}\Big)\quad\subset\quad\tilde{O}_{\text{BP}}\Big(|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C+\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|^{4}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ time}.

Proof.

Problem (
15
) is slightly more straightforward; having done

Lemmas

18
and
19
in depth, we do this one more quickly.
In the standard form, problem (
15
), has variables

x=(\mathbf{u},\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{w})\in K_{\exp}^{\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}
.
The constraints are:

•

one linear constraint for each
(C,c)\in\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C
, to ensure that

w_{C,c}=k_{(C,c)}\mu_{C}(\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c))\qquad\Big(\penalty\ \penalty\ =\sum_{\bar{c}\in\mathcal{V}(C\setminus\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C})}\mu_{C}(\bar{c},\mathit{V\mskip-5.0muC\mskip-3.5muP\!}_{C}(c))\Big)

•

for every edge
(C\!{-}\!D)\in\cal T
, and every value
\omega\in\mathcal{V}(C\cap D)
of the variables that clusters
C
and
D
have in common, a linear constraint

\sum_{\bar{c}\in\mathcal{V}(C\setminus D)}\mu_{C}(\bar{c},\omega)=\sum_{\bar{d}\in\mathcal{V}(D\setminus C)}\mu_{D}(\bar{d},\omega)

•

for every
(a,s,t)\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}
, a linear constraint
that ensures

\mu_{C_{a}}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t)\,\nu_{C_{a}}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s)=\nu_{C_{a}}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s,T\mskip-3.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}t)\,\mu_{C_{a}}(S\mskip-2.0mu\vphantom{|}_{{a}}{=}s).

This is linear, because recall that
\nu
is a constant in this optimization
problem, found by having previously solved (
11
).

•

and one constraint for each cluster
C\in\mathdcal C
to ensure that
\mu_{C}
lies on the probability simplex.

So in total, there are

n=3|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|
variables,
and

m=|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|+|\mathcal{V}\mathcal{T}|+|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|+|\mathdcal C|
constraints.
Once again the components of
A
and
b
are all at most one, and now the components of the cost function
c=\mathbf{1}
are identically one.
Furthermore, our objective is 1-strongly convex, so no additional multaplicative terms are required to convert an
\epsilon
-close solution in the sense of suboptimality, to an
\epsilon
-close solution in the sense of proximity to the true solution.

Therefore (
15
) can be solved in

\displaystyle O\Big(|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C||\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{\omega}\log\frac{|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}{\epsilon}\Big)\quad\subset\quad\tilde{O}_{\text{BP}}(|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C+\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|^{4}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})

operations.
∎

.

See
10

Proof.

Suppose that the PDG has
N
variables
(each of which can take at most
V
distinct values),
and
A
hyperarcs, which together form a structure has tree-width
T
.
Then each cluster (of which there are at most
N
)
can have at most
T+1
variables, and so can take at most
V^{T}
values.
Therefore,
|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|\leq NV^{T+1}
.
Since each arc must be entirely contained within some cluster,

|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|\leq AV^{T}
.
So,
|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|\leq(N+A)V^{T+1}
.

By
Lemma

19
,
we know that, for
\gamma\in(0,\min_{a}\frac{\beta_{a}}{\alpha_{a}}]
,
a tree marginal
\epsilon
-close (in
\ell_{2}
norm) to
the one that represents the unique distribution in the

{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-semantics can be found in time
in time

O\Big((N+A)^{4}V^{4T+4}\log\Big(V^{T+1}(N+A)\frac{1}{\epsilon}\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\gamma}+\log\frac{1}{p^{\min}}\Big)\Big).

Similarly, by
Lemmas

18
and
20

a tree marginal
\epsilon
-close to the one representing the
0^{+}

semantics can be found in time

\displaystyle O\Big(|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C+\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|^{4}\log\frac{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}{\epsilon}\Big)+O\Big(|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C+\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}|^{4}\log\frac{\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C}{\epsilon}\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}\Big)

\displaystyle\subseteq O\Big((N+A)^{4}V^{4(T+1)}\log\Big(V^{T+1}(N+A)\frac{1}{\epsilon}\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}\Big)\Big).

Either way, a tree marginal
\epsilon
-close to the one that represents the
{\mathrlap{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\hat{\phantom{x}}$}}\gamma}
-semantics, for
\gamma\in\{0^{+}\}\cup(0,\min_{a}\frac{\beta_{a}}{\alpha_{a}})
,
can be found in

O\bigg(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\log\Big(\frac{|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}{\epsilon}\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}+\log\frac{1}{p^{\min}}\Big)\bigg)\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \tilde{O}_{\text{BP}}\Big(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)

arithmetic operations, each of which can be done in
O(k\log k)
time.

If
\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbb{P}
, and
\gamma
are all binary numbers specified in
k

bits, then
\log_{2}\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}\leq 2k
and
\log\log\frac{1}{p^{\min}}\leq\log k+\log(2)
,
Thus, under these assumptions, such a tree marginal can be found in

\displaystyle O\bigg(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\Big(\log\frac{|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}{\epsilon}+k+\log k\Big)k\log k\bigg)\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \tilde{O}\bigg(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\log\Big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)k\bigg)

time.
Finally, we prove part (b). The
\infty
-norm is smaller than the
\ell_{2}
norm,
so if
\|\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\|_{2}<2^{-(k+1)}
, then any change to
\boldsymbol{\mu}

of size
2^{-k}
or larger will cause it to be further from
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}
.
Thus, selecting
\epsilon=2^{-(k+1)}
produces the tree marginal of
k
-bit
numbers that is closest to
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}
. Plugging in this value of
\epsilon
,
we find that finding it takes

\tilde{O}(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}k^{2})
time.
∎

Lemma 21
.

Let
k\geq 1
be a fixed integer, and
\Phi
,
K_{0}
,
K_{1},\ldots,K_{k}
be parameters.
Given a procedure that
produces
\epsilon
-approximate unconditional probabilities in

O(\Phi\cdot(K_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}\frac{1}{\epsilon}))
time,
we can approximate conditional probabilities
\Pr(B|A)
to within
\epsilon
in

O(\Phi\cdot(K_{0}\log\log\frac{1}{\Pr(A)}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}\frac{1}{\epsilon\Pr(A)}))

time.

Proof.

Let
f
be our algorithm for approximating unconditional probabilities.
If
A
is an event and
\epsilon>0
, we write
f(A;\epsilon)

for the corresponding approximation to
\Pr(A)
, which by definition satisfies

\Pr(A)-\epsilon\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ f(A;\delta)\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \Pr(A)+\epsilon.

Now suppose that
A
and
B
are both events, and
we want to find the conditional probability

\Pr(B|A)
.
To do so, we can run the following algorithm.

1:

\delta\leftarrow\epsilon
;

2:

loop

3:

let
a\leftarrow f(A;\delta)
;

4:

if

a>2\delta

then

5:

let
\delta^{*}\leftarrow\epsilon(a-\delta)/3
;

6:

let
p\leftarrow f(A;\delta^{*})
and
q\leftarrow f(A\cap B;\delta^{*})
;

7:

return

q\penalty\ /\penalty\ (p+\delta^{*})
.

8:

else

9:

\delta\leftarrow\delta^{2}
;

10:

end

if

11:

end

loop

Proof of correctness.

We claim that the final output of the algorithm is within
\epsilon
of the true conditional probability
\Pr(B|A)
.
In the first iteration in which
a>2\delta
(line 4),
we know that
\delta\leq a-\delta\leq\Pr(A)
.

By assumption,

\displaystyle\Pr(A)-\delta^{*}\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ p\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \Pr(A)+\delta^{*}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Pr(A\cap B)-\delta^{*}\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ q\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \Pr(A\cap B)+\delta^{*},

from which it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\Pr(A\cap B)-\delta^{*}}{\Pr(A)+2\delta^{*}}\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \frac{q}{p+\delta^{*}}\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \frac{\Pr(A\cap B)+\delta^{*}}{\Pr(A)}.

(22)

We now extend the bounds on
q/(p+\delta^{*})
in both directions,
starting with the upper bound.
Because
a-\delta\leq\Pr(A)
, the RHS of (
22
) is at most

\frac{\Pr(A\cap B)+\delta^{*}}{\Pr(A)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \Pr(B|A)+\frac{\delta^{*}}{\Pr(A)}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \Pr(B|A)+\frac{\epsilon}{3}\frac{(a-\delta)}{\Pr(A)}\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \Pr(B|A)+\frac{\epsilon}{3}\frac{\Pr(A)}{\Pr(A)}\penalty\ <\penalty\ \Pr(B|A)+\epsilon.

The analysis of the lower bound (the LHS of (
22
)) is slightly more complicated, but we still find that

\displaystyle\frac{\Pr(A\cap B)-\delta^{*}}{\Pr(A)+2\delta^{*}}

\displaystyle=\Pr(B|A)-\frac{\mu^{*}(x,y)}{\Pr(A)}+\frac{\Pr(A\cap B)-\delta^{*}}{\Pr(A)+2\delta^{*}}

\displaystyle=\Pr(B|A)+\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\Pr(A)\,\Pr(A\cap B)}}-2\delta^{*}\Pr(A\cap B)+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pr(A)\Pr(A\cap B)}}-\delta^{*}\Pr(A)}{\Pr(A)(\Pr(A)+2\delta^{*})}

\displaystyle=\Pr(B|A)+\frac{-2\delta^{*}\Pr(B|A)-\delta^{*}}{\Pr(A)+2\delta^{*}}

\displaystyle=\Pr(B|A)-\delta^{*}\Big(\frac{2\Pr(B|A)+1}{\Pr(A)+2\delta^{*}}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\Pr(B|A)-\delta^{*}\frac{3}{\Pr(A)+\delta^{*}}\qquad\Big[\text{ since $\Pr(B|A)\leq 1$, and thus $-2\Pr(B|A)\geq-2$ }\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr(B|A)-\delta^{*}\frac{3}{\Pr(A)}\qquad\Big[\text{ as eliminating $\delta^{*}$ makes this more negative }\Big]

\displaystyle=\Pr(B|A)-\frac{\epsilon(a-\delta)}{3}\frac{3}{\Pr(A)}\qquad\Big[\text{ by definition of $\delta^{*}$ }\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr(B|A)-\frac{\epsilon\Pr(A)}{\Pr(A)}\qquad\Big[\text{ since $-(a-\delta)\geq-\Pr(A)$ }\Big]

\displaystyle=\Pr(B|A)-\epsilon.

These two arguments extend the bounds of (
22
) in both directions. Chaining all of these inequalities together, we have shown that our procedure returns a number
\mathtt{output}
satisfying

\Pr(B|A)-\epsilon\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \mathtt{output}\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \Pr(B|A)+\epsilon,

and hence calculates the desired conditional probability to within
\epsilon
.

Analysis of Runtime.

Let
m
denote the total number of iterations of the algorithm.
We deal with the simple case of
m=1
separately.
If
m=1
, then already in the first iteration

a>2\delta=2\epsilon
, so by definition
\delta^{*}>\frac{1}{3}\epsilon^{3}
.
Line 6 is just two calls to the procedure, and takes

O\left(\Phi\Big(K_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}\frac{1}{\delta^{*}}\Big)\!\right)=O\left(\Phi\Big(K_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}\frac{3}{\epsilon^{3}}\Big)\!\right)\subseteq O\left(\Phi\Big(K_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)\!\right)\penalty\ \text{time}.

(23)

Now consider the case where
m>1
.
Observe that, in the final iteration,
\delta=\epsilon^{2^{m-1}}
.
The procedure halts when
a>2\delta
, and the smallest possible value of
a
that our approximation can return is
\Pr(A)-\delta
. Thus, the procedure must halt by the time
\Pr(A)>3\delta=3\epsilon^{2^{m-1}}
.
On the other hand, since
m-1
iterations are not enough to ensure termination, it must be that
\Pr(A)-\delta^{\prime}\leq 2\delta^{\prime}
,
where
\delta^{\prime}:=\epsilon^{2^{m-2}}
is the value of
\delta
in the penultimate iteration.
Together, these two facts give us a relationship between
m
and
\Pr(A)
:

\displaystyle 3\epsilon^{2^{m-2}}\penalty\

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\Pr(A)\penalty\

\displaystyle>\penalty\ 3\epsilon^{2^{m-1}}

\displaystyle\iff\qquad

\displaystyle-\log_{2}3-2^{m-2}\log_{2}\epsilon\penalty\

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\log_{2}

\displaystyle\Pr(A)\penalty\

\displaystyle<\penalty\ -\log_{2}3-2^{m-1}\log_{2}\epsilon

\displaystyle\iff\qquad

\displaystyle 2^{m-2}\penalty\

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Big(\log_{2}\frac{3}{\Pr(A)}\Big)

\displaystyle/\log_{2}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})\penalty\

\displaystyle<\penalty\ 2^{m-1}.

(24)

In particular, the first inequality tells us that the number of required iterations is at most

m\leq 2+\log_{2}\log_{2}\frac{3}{\Pr(A)}-\log_{2}\log_{2}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\quad=2+\log_{2}\log_{\epsilon}\frac{\Pr(A)}{3}.

Across all iterations, the total cost of line 3 is on the order of

\displaystyle m\Phi K-\Phi\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\log^{i}(\epsilon^{2^{j-1}})

\displaystyle=m\Phi K-\Phi\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}(\epsilon)\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}2^{kj}

\displaystyle=m\Phi K+\Phi\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\,\frac{2^{im}-1}{2^{i}-1}

\displaystyle<\Big(\log\log\frac{3}{\Pr(A)}-\log\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)\Phi K_{0}+\Phi\,\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\log^{i}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})}}\cdot\left[4^{i}\Big(\log^{i}\frac{3}{\Pr(A)}\Big)\penalty\ /\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\log^{i}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})}}\right]/(2^{i}-1)

\displaystyle\leq\Phi K_{0}\log\log\frac{3}{\Pr(A)}+\Phi\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\frac{4^{i}}{2^{i}-1}\log^{i}\frac{3}{\Pr(A)}

\displaystyle\subseteq O\Big(\Phi\cdot\Big(K_{0}\log\log\frac{1}{\Pr(A)}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}\frac{1}{\Pr(A)}\Big)\Big).

(25)

Line 6 is the last part of the procedure that
incurs a nontrivial cost. The procedure executes it one time, in the final iteration.
Because
a>2\delta
at this point, we know that

\delta^{*}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \frac{\epsilon}{3}(a-\delta)\penalty\ >\penalty\ \frac{\epsilon}{3}\delta\penalty\ =\penalty\ \frac{\epsilon}{3}\epsilon^{2^{m-1}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \frac{\epsilon}{3}\frac{9}{9}\epsilon^{2(2^{m-2})}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \frac{\epsilon}{27}\Big(3\epsilon^{2^{m-2}}\Big)^{2}\penalty\ \geq\penalty\ \frac{\epsilon}{27}\Pr(A)^{2}.

Thus line 6 requires time

\displaystyle O\Big(\Phi\cdot\Big(K_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}\frac{27}{\Pr(A)^{2}\epsilon}\Big)\Big)\subseteq O\Big(\Phi K_{0}+\Phi\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\Big(\log\frac{1}{\Pr(A)}+\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)^{\!i\,}\Big).

(26)

Summarizing, the total running time is (at most) the sum of (
23
), (
25
), and (
26
),
or explicitly,

\displaystyle O\Big(\Phi\cdot\Big(K\log\log\frac{1}{\Pr(A)}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}K_{i}\log^{i}\frac{1}{\epsilon\Pr(A)}\Big)\Big).

∎

.

See
11

Proof.

Theorem

10
gives us an approximation to a calibrated
tree marginal that represents the distribution of interest,
and
Lemma

21
allows us to approximate conditional probabilities
once we can approximate unconditional ones.
The final ingredient is tto approximate unconditional
probabilities using an approximate tree marginal.

Concretely, suppose that we are
looking to find
\mu^{*}(X{=}x)
, where
\mu^{*}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
.
Once we have a calibrated tree marginal
\boldsymbol{\mu}
that represents
\mu^{*}
,
calcluating a marginal
\mu^{*}(X{=}x)
(exactly) from
\boldsymbol{\mu}

can be done with standard methods
[
Koller and Friedman 2009
, §10.3.3]
.
In the worst case, it requires taking a marginal of every cluster,
which can be done in
O(|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|)\subseteq O(NV^{T+1})
arithmetic operations.

The wrinkle is that
\boldsymbol{\mu}
only
approximately
represents
\mu^{*}
,
in the sense that there is some
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}
that does represent
\mu^{*}
such that the L2 norm of
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\mu}
is small.
As usual, we write
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{C}
for the components of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
that
are associated with cluster
C
.
For each
C\in\mathdcal C
, let
E_{C}
denote the event that

(X\cap C)=x|_{C}
.
That is, the variables of
X
that lie in cluster
C
take the values
prescribed by
x
. Then

\displaystyle\big|\Pr\nolimits_{\!\boldsymbol{\mu}}(X{=}x)-\Pr\nolimits_{\!\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}}(X{=}x)\big|\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \sum_{C\in\mathdcal C}\big|\Pr\nolimits_{\!\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}_{C}}(E_{C})-\Pr\nolimits_{\!\boldsymbol{\mu}_{C}}(E_{C})\big|\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \sum_{C\in\mathdcal C}\big\|\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}_{C}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{C}\big\|_{1}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \big\|\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\big\|_{1}\penalty\ .

Applying the L2-L1 norm inequality to the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}
,
we find

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\big\|_{1}\leq\big\|\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\big\|_{2}\sqrt{|\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|}\leq\sqrt{NV^{T+1}}\big\|\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\big\|_{2}.

Thus, to answer unconditional queries about
X
within (absolute) precision

\epsilon
, it suffices to find a tree marginal within
\epsilon/\sqrt{NV^{T+1}}

of
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}
by L2 norm.

From the proof of
Theorem

10
, we know that we can find
such a
\boldsymbol{\mu}

in

\displaystyle O\bigg((N{+}A)^{4}V^{4(T+1)}\log\Big(\frac{(N{+}A)^{4}V^{4(T+1)}\cdot N^{\frac{1}{2}}V^{\frac{T+1}{2}}}{\epsilon}\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}+\log\frac{1}{p^{\min}}\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \tilde{O}\bigg((N{+}A)^{4}V^{4(T+1)}\Big(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\log\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \tilde{O}_{\text{BP}}\Big(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{V}\mathdcal C|^{4}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big)

arithmetic operations,
which dominates the number of operations required to then find the marginal probability
\Pr_{\!\boldsymbol{\mu}}(X{=}x)
given the tree marginal
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
Thus, the complexity of finding unconditional probabilities is the same.
The arithmetic operations need to be done to precision at most
k\in O(\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon}})
, and can be done in time
O(k\log k)
.
Thus, unconditional inference can be done in

\displaystyle O\bigg((N{+}A)^{4.5}V^{4.5(T+1)}\log\Big(\frac{(N{+}A)^{4}V^{4(T+1)}\cdot N^{\frac{1}{2}}V^{\frac{T+1}{2}}}{\epsilon}\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}+\log\frac{1}{p^{\min}}\Big)\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \tilde{O}\bigg((N{+}A)^{4}V^{4(T+1)}\Big(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\log\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}\Big)\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg)\quad\text{time}.

Now that we have characterized the cost of unconditional inference, we can apply
Lemma

21
with
\Phi:=(N+A)^{4}V^{4(T+1)}
,

k=2
,

K_{0}=0
,

K_{1}:=\log\Phi+\log\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}+\log\log\frac{1}{p^{\min}}
,
and

K_{2}=1

to find that conditional probabilities can be found in

\displaystyle\tilde{O}\bigg(\!(N{+}A)^{4}\,V^{4(T+1)}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon\,\mu^{\mskip-2.0mu*\!}(x)}\Big(\log\frac{\beta^{\max}}{\beta^{\min}}+\log\frac{1}{\epsilon\,\mu^{\mskip-2.0mu*\!}(x)}\Big)\bigg)\quad\text{time},

where
\mu^{\mskip-2.0mu*\!}(x)
is shorthand for

\mu^{*}(X{=}x)
.
∎

A.3
Hardness Results and Reductions

We now turn to
Theorem

13
. We begin by proving
parts (a) and (b) directly
by reduction to SAT and #SAT, respectively.

.

See
13

Proof.

(a).

We can directly encode SAT problems in PDGs.
Choose any CNF formula

\varphi=\bigwedge_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\bigvee_{i\in\mathcal{I}(j)}(X_{j,i})

over binary variables
\mathbf{X}:=\bigcup_{j,i}X_{j,i}
,
and let
n:=|\mathbf{X}|
denote the total number of variables in
\varphi
.
Let

\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}
be the PDG containing every variable
X\in\mathbf{X}
and a binary
variable
C_{j}
(taking the value 0 or 1) for each clause
j\in\mathcal{J}
, as well as the following edges, for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
:

•

a hyperedge
\{X_{j,i}:i\in\mathcal{I}(j)\}\rightarrow\mathrel{\mspace{-15mu}}\rightarrow C_{j}
, together with a degenerate cpd
encoding the boolean OR function (i.e., the truth of
C_{j}
given
\{X_{j,i}\}
);

•

an edge
\mathrlap{\mathit{1}}\mspace{2.3mu}\mathit{1}\rightarrow\mathrel{\mspace{-15mu}}\rightarrow C_{j}
, together with a cpd asserting
C_{j}
be equal to 1.

First, note that the number of nodes, edges, and non-zero entries in the cpds are polynomial in the
|\mathcal{J}|,|\mathbf{X}|
, and the total number of parameters in a simple matrix representation of the cpds is also polynomial if
\mathcal{I}
is bounded (e.g., if
\varphi
is a 3-CNF formula).
A satisfying assignment
\mathbf{x}\models\varphi
of the variables
\mathbf{X}
can be regarded as a degenerate joint distribution
\delta_{\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}}
on
\mathbf{X}
, and extends uniquely to a full joint distribution
\mu_{\mathbf{x}}\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi})
consistent with all of the edges, by

\mu_{\mathbf{x}}=\delta_{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\delta_{\{C_{j}=\vee_{i}x_{j,i}\}}

Conversely, if
\mu
is a joint distribution consistent with the edges above, then any point
\mathbf{x}
in the support of
\mu(\mathbf{X})
must be a satisfying assignment, since the two classes of edges respectively ensure that
1=\mu(C_{j}\!=\!1\mid\mathbf{X}\!=\!\mathbf{x})=\bigvee_{i\in\mathcal{I}(j)}\mathbf{x}_{j,i}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and so
\mathbf{x}\models\varphi
.

Thus,
\{\hskip-23.17232pt\hskip 1.2pt\{\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\}\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-23.17232pt\}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
\varphi
is satisfiable, so
an algorithm for determining if a PDG is consistent can also be adapted (in polynomial space and time) for use as a SAT solver, and so the problem of determining if a PDG consistent is NP-hard.

(b) Hardness of exact computation.

We prove this by reduction to #SAT. Again, let
\varphi
be some CNF formula over
\mathbf{X}
, and construct

\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}
as in
the proof
of

Theorem

13
.
Furthemore, let
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]:=\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\models\varphi\}
be the set of assingments to
\mathbf{X}
satisfying
\varphi
, and
\#_{\varphi}:=|[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]|
denote the number such assignments. We now claim that

\#_{\varphi}=\exp\left[-\frac{1}{\gamma}\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}\right].

(27)

Once we do so, we will have a reduced the #P-hard problem of
computing

\#_{\varphi}
to the problem of
computing

\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
(exactly).

We now prove (
27
).
By definition, we have

\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}=\inf_{\mu}\Big[\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu)+\gamma\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu)\Big].

We start with a claim about first term.

Claim 21.1
.

\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}\!(\mu)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\penalty\ \supp\mu\subseteq[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]\times\{\mathbf{1}\}\\
\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Writing out the definition explicitly, the first can be written as

\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}\!(\mu)=\sum_{j}\left[I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu(C_{j})\;\Big\|\;\delta_{1}\Big)+\Ex_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mu(\mathbf{X}_{j})}I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu(C_{j}\mid\mathbf{X}_{j}=\mathbf{x})\;\Big\|\;\delta_{\lor_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j,i}}\Big)\right],

(28)

where
\mathbf{X}_{j}=\{X_{ij}:j\in\mathcal{I}(j)\}
is the set of variables that
appear in clause
j
, and
\delta_{(-)}
is the probability distribution placing all mass on the point indicated by its subscript.
As a reminder, the relative entropy is given by

I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu(\Omega)\;\Big\|\;\nu(\Omega)\Big):=\Ex_{\omega\sim\mu}\log\frac{\mu(\omega)}{\nu(\omega)},\quad\parbox{101.17755pt}{\centering and in particular, \\
if $\Omega$ is binary,\@add@centering}\quad I\mkern-8.0muD\big(\mu(\Omega)\;\big\|\;\delta_{\omega}\big)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\penalty\ \mu(\omega)=1;\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Applying this to (
28
), we find that either:

1.

Every term of (
28
) is finite (and zero) so
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu)=0
, which happens when
\mu(C_{j}=1)=1
and
\mu(C_{j}=\vee_{i}\penalty\ x_{j,i})=1
for all
j
. In this case,
\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{1}=\{\vee_{i}\penalty\ x_{j,i}\}_{j}
so
\mathbf{x}\models\varphi
for every
(\mathbf{c,x})\in\supp\mu
;

2.

Some term of (
28
) is infinite, so that
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu)=\infty
, which happens if some
j
, either

(a)

\mu(C_{j}\neq 1)>0
— in which case there is some
(\mathbf{x,c})\in\supp\mu
with
\mathbf{c}\neq 1
, or

(b)

\supp\mu(\mathbf{C})=\{\mathbf{1}\}
, but
\mu(C_{j}\neq\vee_{i}\penalty\ x_{j,i})>0
— in which case there is some
(\mathbf{x,1})\in\supp\mu
for which
1=c_{j}\neq\vee_{i}\penalty\ x_{j,i}\;
, and so
\mathbf{x}\not\models\varphi
.

Condensing and rearranging slightly, we have shown that

\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\penalty\ \mathbf{x}\models\varphi\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \mathbf{c}=\mathbf{1}\penalty\ \text{for all}\penalty\ (\mathbf{x},\mathbf{c})\in\supp\mu\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\penalty\ .

∎

Because
\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}
is bounded, it follows immediately that

\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
, is finite if and only if
there is some distribution
\mu\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{C})
for which
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu)
is finite, or equivalently, by
21.1
, iff there exists some
\mu(\mathbf{X})\in\Delta\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{X})
for which
\supp\mu(\mathbf{X})\subseteq[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]
, which in turn is true if and only if
\varphi
is satisfiable.

In particular, if
\varphi
is not satisfiable (i.e.,
\#_{\varphi}=0
), then
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}=+\infty
, and

\exp\left[-\frac{1}{\gamma}\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}\right]=\exp[-\infty]=0=\#_{\varphi},

so in this case (
27
) holds as promised. On the other hand, if
\varphi

is
satisfiable, then, again by
21.1
, every
\mu
minimizing
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
, (i.e., every
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}^{*}
) must be supported entirely on
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]
and have
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}\!(\mu)=0
. As a result, we have

\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}=\inf\nolimits_{\mu\in\Delta\big[[\hskip-16.8269pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-16.8269pt]\times\{\mathbf{1}\}\big]}\gamma\;\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu).

A priori, by the definition of
\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}
, we have

\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu)=-\H(\mu)+\sum_{j}\Big[\alpha_{j,1}\H_{\mu}(C_{j}\mid\mathbf{X}_{j})+\alpha_{j,0}\H_{\mu}(C_{j})\Big],

where
\alpha_{j,0}
and
\alpha_{j,1}
are values of
\alpha
for the edges of
\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}
, which we have not specified because they are rendered irrelevant by the fact that their corresponding cpds are deterministic. We now show how this plays out in the present case.
Any
\mu\in\Delta\big[[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]\times\{\mathbf{1}\}\big]
we consider has a degenerate marginal on
\mathbf{C}
. Specifcally, for every
j
, we have
\mu(C_{j})=\delta_{1}
, and since entropy is non-negative and never increased by conditioning,

0\leq\H_{\mu}(C_{j}\mid\mathbf{X}_{j})\leq\H_{\mu}(C_{j})=0.

Therefore,
\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu)
reduces to the negative entropy of
\mu
.
Finally, making use of the fact that the maximum entropy distribution
\mu^{*}
supported on a finite set
S
is the uniform distribution on
S
, and has
\H(\mu^{*})=\log|S|
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\inf\nolimits_{\mu\in\Delta\big([\hskip-16.8269pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-16.8269pt]\times\{\mathbf{1}\}\big)}\gamma\;\mathit{S\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf\nolimits_{\mu\in\Delta\big([\hskip-16.8269pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-16.8269pt]\times\{\mathbf{1}\}\big)}-\,\gamma\,\H(\mu)

\displaystyle=-\gamma\,\sup\nolimits_{\mu\in\Delta\big([\hskip-16.8269pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-16.8269pt]\times\{\mathbf{1}\}\big)}\H(\mu)

\displaystyle=-\gamma\,\log(\#_{\varphi}),

giving us

\#_{\varphi}=\exp\left[-\frac{1}{\gamma}\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}\right],

as desired. We have now reduced #SAT to computing
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
, for
\gamma>0
and an arbitrary PDG
\mathbdcal{M}
, which is therefore #P-hard.

To show the same for
\gamma=0
, it suffices to add an additional hyperedge pointing to all variables, and associate it with a joint uniform distribution, and confidence 1, resulting in a new PDG
\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}^{\prime}
.
Because this new edge’s contribution to
\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}}

equals
I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu\;\|\;\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{X}))=\log|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}|-\H(\mu)
,
we have

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{0}(\mu)=\mathit{O\mskip-2.5muI\mskip-3.5mun\mskip-1.7muc}_{\mathbdcal{M}_{\varphi}^{\prime}}(\mu)=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt](\mu)+\log|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}|-\H(\mu)=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{1}(\mu)+\log|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}|.

Since this is true for all
\mu
,
we can take the of this equation over
\mu
,
and so conclude that

\displaystyle\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0}=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{1}+\log|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}|=\log\big(|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}|/\#_{\varphi}\big)

\displaystyle\implies\qquad\#_{\varphi}=|\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}|\exp(-\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0})

Thus, the number of satisfying assignments can be found through
via an oracle for
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{0}
, as well. This shows that calculating
this purely observational inconsistency is #P-hard as well.

(c) Hardness of approximation.

To calculate
\#_{\varphi}
exactly, it turns out that
we do not need to know
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
exactly.
Instead, we claim it suffices to approximate it to within

\epsilon<\gamma\log(1+2^{-(n+1)})
.

Suppose that
|r-\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}|<\epsilon
.
Then

\displaystyle\exp\Big(-\frac{r}{\gamma}\Big)

\displaystyle\in\exp\Big[-\frac{1}{\gamma}\Big(\big\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\big\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\big\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\big\rangle_{\gamma}\pm\epsilon\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\exp\Big[-\frac{1}{\gamma}\big\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\big\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\big\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\big\rangle_{\gamma}\Big]\cdot\exp(\pm\nicefrac{{\epsilon}}{{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\#_{\varphi}\cdot\exp(\pm\nicefrac{{\epsilon}}{{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=[\#_{\varphi}\exp(-\nicefrac{{\epsilon}}{{\gamma}}),\penalty\ \#_{\varphi}\exp(+\nicefrac{{\epsilon}}{{\gamma}})].

Since
\#_{\varphi}
is a natural number and at most
2^{n}
,
If we can get a relative approximation of it to
within a factor of
2^{-(n+1)}
, then rounding that approximate value
to the nearest whole number gives the exact value of
\#_{\varphi}
.
Thus, it suffices to choose
\epsilon
small enough that

\exp(-\nicefrac{{\epsilon}}{{\gamma}})>1-2^{-(n+1)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\exp(+\nicefrac{{\epsilon}}{{\gamma}})<1+2^{-(n+1)};

this is satisfied any choice of
\epsilon<\gamma\log(1+2^{-(n+1)})
.
Thus, being able to approximate
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
sufficiently closely
will tell us whether or not
\varphi\in
SAT
.
Note that for large
n
, the maximum value of
\epsilon
for which this is true
is on the order of
\epsilon_{\max}\in\Theta(\gamma 2^{-n})
.
It follows that
\log(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\epsilon_{\max}}})\in O(n)
, and so
values of
\epsilon
small enough to determine the satisfiability of
a formula
\varphi
with
n
variables can be specified in time
O(n)
.
Thus, the problem
APPROX-CALC-INC
is #P hard (in the size of its input).
∎

Inference via Inconsistency Minimization.

We now address

Theorem

14
,
which is closely related to
Richardson and Halpern 2021
’s original idea for an inference
algorithm. While that idea does not yield an efficient inference algorithm,
it does yield an efficient reduction from inconsistency minimization to inference.
In order to prove this, we first need another construction with PDGs.
A probability over a (set of) variables can be viewed as a vector whose
elements sum to one.
It turns out that it is possible to use the machinery of PDGs
to, effectively, give only one value of such a probability vector.
That is, for any
p\in[0,1]
, we can construct a PDG
that represents the belief that
\Pr(Y{=}y)=p
, but say nothing about
how the probability splits between other values of
y
.
We now describe that construction.

We first introduce an auxiliary binary variable

{Y_{y}}
, with
\mathcal{V}({Y_{y}})=\{y,\lnot y\}
,
and takes the value
y
if
Y=y
, and
\lnot y
if
Y\neq y
.
Note that this variable is a function of the value of variable
Y

(although we will need to enforce this with an additional arc), and
therefore there is a unique way to extend a distribution over
variables including
Y
to also include the variable
{Y_{y}}
.

With this definition, there is now an obvious way to add a hyperarc with no source and
target
{Y_{y}}
, together with a asserting that
\Pr(Y{=}y)=p
.
This cpd is written as a vector
\hat{p}
on the right of the figure below.
The PDG we have just constructed is illustrated on the left of the figure below.
In addition to
\hat{p}
and the new variable, this PDG
includes the structural constraint
s
needed to define the variables

{Y_{y}}
in terms of
Y
; it is a deterministic function,
drawn with a double-headed gray arrow.

y
\lnot y

{Y_{y}}
Y
s
\hat{p}

\displaystyle s({Y_{y}}|Y)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}y&\text{if $Y=y$}\\
\lnot y&\text{if $Y\neq y$}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\hat{p}({Y_{y}})

\displaystyle:=\penalty\ \penalty\ \begin{matrix}\begin{matrix}y&\lnot y\end{matrix}\\
\begin{bmatrix}\;p&1-p\;\end{bmatrix}\end{matrix}

So, when we add
\Pr(Y=y)=p
to a PDG
\mathbdcal{M}
, what we really mean is:
first convert construct a widget as above, and add that structure (i.e., the new variable
{Y_{y}}
, its definition
s
, and the cpd
\hat{p}
) to
\mathbdcal{M}
.
In what sense does this “work”?
The first order of business
is to prove that it behaves as we should expect,
semantically, in the case we’re interested in.

Lemma 22
.

Suppose that
\mathbdcal{M}
is a PDG with variables
\mathcal{X}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq\mathbf{0}
.
Then, for all
Y\subseteq\mathcal{X}
,
y\in\mathcal{V}Y
,
p\in[0,1]
and
\gamma\geq 0
,
we have that:

\Big\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\Big\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\Big\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\Big\rangle_{\gamma}\geq\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma},

with equality if and only if there exists
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}

such that
\mu(Y{=}y)=p
.

Proof.

The inequality is immediate; it is an instance of monotonicity of inconsistency

[
Richardson 2022
, Lemma 1]
. Intuitively: believing more cannot make you any less
inconsistent. We now prove that equality holds iff there is a minimizer with the appropriate conditional probability.

(\impliedby)
. Suppose that there is some
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
with
\mu(Y{=}y)=p
.
Because
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
, we know that

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle
.
Let
\hat{\mu}
be the extension of
\mu
to the new variable
``{Y_{y}}
”,
whose value is a function of
Y
according to
s
. Then

\displaystyle\Big\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\Big\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\Big\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\Big\rangle_{\!\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq\Big[\hskip-22.63342pt\hskip 1.2pt\Big[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\Big]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.63342pt\Big]_{\gamma}(\hat{\mu})

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)+\Ex_{\mu}\left[\log\frac{\hat{\mu}({Y_{y}})}{\hat{p}({Y_{y}})}\right]

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)+\mu(Y{=}y)\log\frac{\mu(Y{=}y)}{p}+\mu(Y{\neq}y)\log\frac{\mu(Y{\neq}y)}{1-p}

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)+\mu(Y{=}y)\log(1)+\mu(Y{\neq}y)\log(1)

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}.

To complete this direction of the proof, it suffices to observe
that we already knew the inequality held in the opposite direction
(by monotonicity), so the two terms are equal.

(\implies)
. Suppose that the two inconsistencies are equal, i.e.,

\Big\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\Big\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\Big\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\Big\rangle_{\gamma}=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}.

This time, choose
\hat{\mu}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
,
and define
\mu
to be its marginal on the variables of
\mathbdcal{M}

(which contains the same information as
\hat{\mu}
itself).
Let
q:=\mu(Y{=}y)
. Then

\displaystyle\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\Big\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\Big\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\Big\rangle_{\!\gamma}

\displaystyle=\Big[\hskip-22.63342pt\hskip 1.2pt\Big[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\Big]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.63342pt\Big]_{\gamma}(\hat{\mu})

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)+\mu(Y{=}y)\log\frac{\mu(Y{=}y)}{p}+\mu(Y{\neq}y)\log\frac{\mu(Y{\neq}y)}{1-p}

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)+\left[q\log\frac{q}{p}+(1-q)\log\frac{1-q}{1-p}\right]

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)+I\mkern-8.0muD(q\;\|\;p)

\displaystyle\geq\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}+I\mkern-8.0muD(q\;\|\;p)

Therefore
0\geq I\mkern-8.0muD(q\;\|\;p)
. But relative entropy is non-negative
(Gibbs inequality; see any introductory text on information theory, such as

MacKay 2003
),
so we actually know that
I\mkern-8.0muD(q\;\|\;p)=0
, and thus

p=\mu(Y{=}y)
.
In addition, the algebra above shows that
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
, as its
score is
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}
.
Thus, we have found
\mu\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
such that
\mu(Y{=}y)=p
, completing the proof.
∎

We next show that the overall inconsistency is strictly convex in the parameter
p\in[0,1]
.
It is (notationally) simpler to state (and equally easy to prove) this result in the general case.

†
†
margin:

[

link to

proof

]

Lemma 23
.

Fix
Y\subseteq\mathcal{X}
,
and
\gamma\in(0,\min_{a}\frac{\beta_{a}}{\alpha_{a}})
.
As
h=h(Y)
ranges over
\Delta\mathcal{V}Y
,
the function
h\mapsto\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}

is strictly convex.

Proof.

We start by expanding the definitions. If
h
is a cpd on
Y
given
X
, then

\displaystyle\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu}\penalty\ [\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mu}\left[[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)+I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu(Y)\;\Big\|\;h(Y\Big)\right].

Fix
\gamma\leq\min_{a}\frac{\beta_{a}}{\alpha_{a}}
. Then we know that
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu)
is a
\gamma
-strongly convex
(so, in particular, strictly convex)
function of
\mu
,
and hence there is a unique joint distribution which minimizes it. We now show that the overall inconsistency is strictly convex in
h
.

Suppose that
h_{1}(Y)
and
h_{2}(Y)
are two distributions over
Y
.
Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{\lambda}
be the joint distributions that minimze
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
and
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
, respectively.
For every
\lambda\in[0,1]
, define
h_{\lambda}:=(1-\lambda)h_{1}+\lambda h_{2}
,

\mu_{\lambda}:=(1-\lambda)\mu_{1}+\lambda\mu_{2}
, and

\mu_{\lambda}^{*}
to be a minimizer of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
.
The following is a simple consequence of these definitions:

\displaystyle\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{\lambda}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{\lambda})+I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{\lambda}(Y)\;\Big\|\;h_{\lambda}(Y)\Big).

By the convexity of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
and
I\mkern-8.0muD
, we have

\displaystyle[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq(1-\lambda)[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{1})+\lambda[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{2})

(29)

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{\lambda}(Y)\;\Big\|\;h_{\lambda}(Y)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\lambda)I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{1}(Y)\;\Big\|\;h_{1}(Y)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda\;\;I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{2}(Y)\;\Big\|\;h_{2}(Y)\Big).

(30)

If
\mu_{1}\neq\mu_{2}
then since
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]
is strictly convex, (
29
) must
be a strict inequality. On the other hand, if
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
, then since
\mu_{\lambda}=\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
and
I\mkern-8.0muD
is stricly convex in its second argument when its first argument is fixed, (
30
) must be a strict inequality.
In either case, the sum of the two inequalities must be strict.
Combining this with the first inequality, we get

\displaystyle\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{\lambda})+I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{\lambda}(Y)\;\Big\|\;h_{\lambda}(Y)\Big)

\displaystyle<(\lambda-1)\left[[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{1})+I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{1}(Y)\;\Big\|\;h_{1}(Y)\Big)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda\left[[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{2})+I\mkern-8.0muD\Big(\mu_{2}(Y)\;\Big\|\;h_{2}(Y)\Big)\right]

\displaystyle=(\lambda-1)\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle+\lambda\,\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle,

which shows that
\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle
is
strictly
convex in
h
, as desired.
∎

Let
\mathbdcal{M}
be a PDG with
\boldsymbol{\beta}\geq\mathbf{0}
and
variables
\mathcal{X}
, and fix
Y\subseteq\mathcal{X}
,
y\in\mathcal{V}Y
, and

\gamma\in(0,\min_{a}\frac{\beta_{a}}{\alpha_{a}})
.
For
p\in[0,1]
, define

f(p):=\Big\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\Big\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\Big\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\Big\rangle_{\gamma}.

(31)

The next several results (
23.1
,
24
,
25
,
27
,
28
and
29
) are properties of this function
f(p)
.

Corollary 23.1
.

The function
f
defined in (
31
)
is strictly convex.

Proof.

Simply take
h
to be the cpd
\hat{p}
,
absorb the the other components of (the PDG representation of)
\Pr(Y{=}y)=p

into
\mathbdcal{M}
, and then apply
Lemma

23
.
∎

The results from this point until

the proof of Theorem 14

are all technical results that support the more precise analysis of part (b).
We recommend returning to these these results as needed, after first reading
the proof of part (a).

Lemma 24
.

For
p\in(0,1]
, let
\mu_{p}
be the unique element of
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
.
Then

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(p)=\frac{p-\mu^{*}_{p}(Y{=}y)}{p(1-p)}.

Proof.

First, suppose
\mu_{p}^{*}
is in the interior of the simplex.
Since it minimizes the differentiable function

\big[\hskip-21.48824pt\hskip 1.2pt\big[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\big]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.48824pt\big]_{\gamma}=\mu\mapsto[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}+I\mkern-8.0muD\big(\mu(Y{=}y)\;\big\|\;p\big),

the gradient of that function at
\mu^{*}_{p}
must be zero:

\displaystyle\nabla\big[\hskip-21.48824pt\hskip 1.2pt\big[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\big]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.48824pt\big]_{\gamma}(\mu^{*}_{p})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mu}\Big[I\mkern-8.0muD\big(\mu(Y{=}y)\;\big\|\;p\big)\Big]_{\mu=\mu^{*}_{p}}+\nabla[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt](\mu^{*}_{p})=0.

(32)

What is the derivative of
f
?
Observe that
f
is the sum of two compositions of differentiable maps:

\displaystyle f{M}

\displaystyle:=\qquad p\mapsto\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mu^{*}_{p}\penalty\ \mapsto[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu^{*}_{p})

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad f_{\Pr}

\displaystyle:=\qquad p\mapsto(p,\mu^{*}_{p})\mapsto I\mkern-8.0muD\big(\mu^{*}_{p}(Y{=}y)\;\big\|\;p\big).

Thus, we can use the multivariate chain rule.
for any differentiable functions
h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, their composition
g\circ h:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is also a differentiable map whose Jacobian is

\mathbf{J}_{g\circ h}(x)=\mathbf{J}_{g}(h(x))\mathbf{J}_{h}(x)
.
In our case,
g
will be a scalar map (
n=1
), so
\mathbf{J}_{g}(h(x))=[\ldots,\frac{\partial g}{\partial x_{j}},\ldots](h(x))=\nabla g(h(x))^{\sf T}
.
Let
\mathbf{J}_{\mu^{*}_{p}}(p)
be the Jacobian of the map
p\mapsto\mu^{*}_{p}
.
Then

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(p)

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}_{\mathbdcal{M}}(p)+f^{\prime}_{\Pr}(p)

\displaystyle=\big(\nabla[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt](\mu^{*}_{p})\big)^{\sf T}\mathbf{J}_{\mu^{*}_{p}}+\left(\nabla_{\mu}\Big[\mu(x)I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(y|x)\;\|\;p)\Big]_{\mu=\mu^{*}_{p}}\right)^{\sf T}\mathbf{J}_{\mu^{*}_{p}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\Big[I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu^{*}_{p}(Y{=}y)\;\|\;p)\Big]

(Alternatively, the line above can be derived from the law of total
derivative.
3
3

3

Law of total derivative:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}f}{\mathrm{d}p}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}\frac{\partial I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(y|x)\;\|\;p)}{\partial\mu^{*}_{p}(w)}(\mu_{p}^{*},p)\frac{\partial\mu^{*}_{p}(w)}{\partial p}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \frac{\partial}{\partial p}I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu^{*}_{p},p)+\sum_{w\in\mathcal{V}\!\mathcal{X}}\frac{\partial[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}}{\partial\mu_{p}^{*}(w)}(\mu_{p}^{*})\frac{\partial\mu^{*}_{p}(w)}{\partial p}.
)

\displaystyle=\Big({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\mu}\Big[I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(Y{=}y)\;\|\;p)\Big]_{\mu=\mu^{*}_{p}}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt](\mu^{*}_{p})}}\Big)^{\sf T}\mathbf{J}_{\mu^{*}_{p}}(p)+\frac{\partial}{\partial p}I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu^{*}_{p}(Y{=}y)\;\|\;p)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial p}I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu^{*}_{p}(Y{=}y)\;\|\;p)

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{eqn:matching-gradients-at-optimum}}.

Finally,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p}\Big[I\mkern-8.0muD(q\;\|\;p)\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p}\Big[q\log\frac{q}{p}+(1-q)\log\frac{1-q}{1-p}\Big]

\displaystyle=q\Big(\frac{p}{q}\Big)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p}\Big[\frac{q}{p}\Big]+(1-q)\Big(\frac{1-p}{1-q}\Big)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p}\Big[\frac{1-q}{1-p}\Big]

\displaystyle=pq\Big(\frac{-1}{p^{2}}\Big)+(1-p)(1-q)\Big(\frac{-1}{(1-p)^{2}}\Big)(-1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{q}{p}+\frac{1-q}{1-p}

\displaystyle=\frac{-(1-p)q+p(1-q)}{p(1-p)}

\displaystyle=\frac{pq-q+p-pq}{p(1-p)}=\frac{p-q}{p(1-p)}.

Thus, we find

f^{\prime}(p)=\frac{p-\mu^{*}_{p}(Y{=}y)}{p(1-p)},\qquad\qquad\text{as promised}.\qed

Lemma 25
.

If
0<p_{1}<p_{2}<1

and
\mu_{1}^{*},\mu_{2}^{*}
are respective minimizing distributions,
then

f(p_{2})\geq f(p_{1})+f^{\prime}(p_{1})(p_{2}-p_{1})+\frac{1}{2}\gamma\|\mu_{1}^{*}-\mu_{2}^{*}\|_{1}^{2}.

Proof.

The general approach is to adapt and strengthen
the
the proof of

Lemma

23
,
to show something like strong convexity, in this special case.
Define

\mathbdcal{M}_{1}:=\mathbdcal{M}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \Pr(Y{=}y)=p_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbdcal{M}_{2}:=\mathbdcal{M}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \Pr(Y{=}y)=p_{2}.

Choose
\mu_{1}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
and
\mu_{2}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
.
As before, let
m_{1}:=\mu_{1}(Y{=}y)

and

m_{2}:=\mu_{2}(Y{=}y)
.
Then

\displaystyle f(p_{1})

\displaystyle=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{1})=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{1})+I\mkern-8.0muD(m_{1}\;\|\;p_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\text{and}\qquad f(p_{2})

\displaystyle=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{2})=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{1})+I\mkern-8.0muD(m_{2}\;\|\;p_{2}),

where, as before,
I\mkern-8.0muD(m\;\|\;p)=m\log\frac{m}{p}+(1-m)\log\frac{1-m}{1-p}
is
the relative entropy between Bernouli distributions with respective parameters
m
and
p
.

For each
\lambda\in[0,1]
, define

\displaystyle p_{\lambda}:=(1-\lambda)p_{1}+\lambda p_{2},\qquad\quad\mathbdcal{M}_{\lambda}:=\mathbdcal{M}+\Pr(Y{=}y)=p_{\lambda},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu_{\lambda}:=(1-\lambda)\mu_{1}+\lambda\mu_{2}\penalty\ .

We now provide stronger analogues of (
29
) and (
30
).
Since
[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}
is not just convex but also

\gamma
-strongly convex, with respect to the 1-norm (
Lemma

26
, below),
we can strengthen (
29
) to

\displaystyle[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq(1-\lambda)[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{1})+\lambda[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{2})-\frac{\gamma}{2}(1-\lambda)\lambda\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|^{2}_{1}.

Adding this inequality to the analogous one describing joint convexity of
I\mkern-8.0muD
in its two arguments (
30
), we find that

\displaystyle[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{\lambda})+I\mkern-8.0muD(m_{\lambda}\;\|\;p_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq(1-\lambda)\Big([\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{1})+I\mkern-8.0muD(m_{1}\;\|\;p_{1})\Big)+\lambda\Big([\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{2})+I\mkern-8.0muD(m_{2}\;\|\;p_{2})\Big)-\frac{\gamma}{2}(1-\lambda)\lambda\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|^{2}_{1}

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)f(p_{1})+\lambda f(p_{2})-\frac{\gamma}{2}(1-\lambda)\lambda\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|^{2}_{1}.

(33)

Putting it all together, we find that

\displaystyle f(p_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\langle\hskip-22.09453pt\hskip 1.2pt\langle\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}\rangle\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-22.09453pt\rangle_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{\lambda})+I\mkern-8.0muD(m_{\lambda}\;\|\;p_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq(1-\lambda)f(p_{1})+\lambda f(p_{2})-\frac{\gamma}{2}(1-\lambda)\lambda\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|^{2}_{1}

\displaystyle\text{\eqref{eq:mu-lambda-intermediate}}.

Since this is true for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
, we can divide by
\lambda
, rearrange, and take the limit as
\lambda\to 0
, to find:

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{2}(1-\lambda)\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|^{2}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1-\lambda)f(p_{1})-f(p_{1}+\lambda(p_{2}-p_{1}))}{\lambda}+f(p_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{f(p_{1})-f(p_{1}+\lambda(p_{2}-p_{1}))}{\lambda}+f(p_{2})-f(p_{1})

\displaystyle\implies\qquad f(p_{2})-f(p_{1})

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{\lambda\to 0}f(p_{1})+\frac{f(p_{1}+\lambda(p_{2}-p_{1}))-f(p_{1})}{\lambda}\frac{\gamma}{2}(1-\lambda)\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|^{2}_{1}

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}(p_{1})(p_{2}-p_{1})+\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|^{2}_{1},

as desired.
∎

Lemma 26
.

Negative entropy is 1-strongly convex with
respect with respect to the L1 norm, i.e.,

f(\mathbf{p})=\sum_{i}p_{i}\log p_{i}
satisfies

f(\mathbf{q})\geq f(\mathbf{p})+\nabla f(\mathbf{p})^{\sf T}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{1}^{2}.

Proof.

First,
\nabla f(\mathbf{p})=\log(\mathbf{p})+1
.
Thus,

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{q})-f(\mathbf{p})-\nabla f(\mathbf{p})^{\sf T}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left[q_{i}\log q_{i}-p_{i}\log p_{i}-(\log p_{i}+1)(q_{i}-p_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left[q_{i}\log q_{i}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{i}\log p_{i}}}-q_{i}\log p_{i}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{i}\log p_{i}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}q_{i}\log\frac{q_{i}}{p_{i}}

\displaystyle=I\mkern-8.0muD(q\;\|\;p)

\displaystyle\geq 2\delta(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})^{2}

\displaystyle[\text{by Pinsker's inequality
\cite[citep]{[\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{pinsker-inequality}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}]}
}]

\displaystyle=2\Big(\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{1}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{1}^{2}

where
\delta(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
is the total variation distance between
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
as measures, and
\|p-q\|_{1}=\sum_{i}|p_{i}-q_{i}|
is the L1 norm of their difference, as
points on a simplex.
∎

Lemma

25
guarantees
that if the optimal distributions corresponding to adding
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
to the PDG
(
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, respectively) are far apart, then so are

f(p_{1})
and
f(p_{2})
. But what if these optimal distributions are close together?
It turns out that if
\mu_{1}\approx\mu_{2}
then it’s still the case that
f(p_{1})
and
f(p_{2})
are far apart, provied that
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are.
However, showing this requires an entirely appraoch, which we pursue in
Lemma

28
.
But first, we need two intermediate technical results.

Lemma 27
.

For all
p_{1},p_{2}\in[0,1]
,

[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{1}^{*})-[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{2}^{*})\geq(m_{2}-m_{1})\log\frac{m_{2}}{p_{2}}\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-m_{2}},

where

m_{1}=\mu_{1}^{*}(Y{=}y)
is the marginal of
\mu_{1}^{*}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]^{*}_{\gamma}
,
and
m_{2}=\mu_{2}^{*}(Y{=}y)
is defined symmetrically.

Proof.

For
p,q\in[0,1]
,
I\mkern-8.0muD(p\;\|\;q):=p\log\frac{p}{q}+(1-p)\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}
is the relative entropy between the Bernouli distributions described by their parameters.
First, we calculate

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p}I\mkern-8.0muD(p\;\|\;q)

\displaystyle=\log\frac{p}{q}+\frac{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p}\left[\frac{p}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\right]+(-1)\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1-q)}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p}\left[\frac{1-p}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cancel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1-q}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\log\frac{p}{q}+1-\log\frac{1-p}{1-q}-1

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{p}{q}\frac{1-q}{1-p}\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\big[I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(Y{=}y)\;\|\;q)]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mu}[\mu(Y{=}y)]\log\left(\frac{\mu(Y{=}y)}{q}\frac{1-q}{1-\mu(Y{=}y)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}[Y{=}y]\log\left(\frac{\mu(Y{=}y)}{q}\frac{1-q}{1-\mu(Y{=}y)}\right).

Recall the stationary conditions, which state that,
since
\mu_{2}^{*}\in[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]
, we have

\nabla_{\mu}\Big[I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(Y{=}y)\;\|\;p_{2})\Big]_{\mu=\mu_{2}^{*}}=-\nabla_{\mu}[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{2}^{*}).

Now use the above to compute the directional derivative of interest:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}[\hskip-21.01675pt\hskip 1.2pt[\mathopen{}\hbox{$#1$}\mathclose{}]\hskip 1.2pt\hskip-21.01675pt]_{\gamma}(\mu_{2}^{*})^{\sf T}(\mu_{1}^{*}-\mu_{2}^{*})

\displaystyle=-(\mu_{1}^{*}-\mu_{2}^{*})^{\sf T}\nabla_{\mu}\big[I\mkern-8.0muD(\mu(Y{=}y)\;\|\;p_{2})\big]_{\mu=\mu_{2}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\big(\mu_{2}^{*}(Y{=}y)-\mu_{1}^{*}(Y{=}y)\big)\log\left(\frac{\mu_{2}^{*}(Y{=}y)}{p_{2}}\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-\mu_{2}^{*}(Y{=}y)}\right)

\displaystyle=(m_{2}-m_{1})\log\frac{m_{2}}{p_{2}}\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-m_{2}}.

Finally, since
